# D13 Physical Infrastructure — Sub-Domain 1: Transit and Mobility

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain number:** SD1
**Sub-domain name:** Transit and Mobility
**Scope statement:** The federal/state/local mandate-stack architecture for public transportation serving PA-3, with primary institutional embodiment in SEPTA as the state-created regional authority; the federal-floor protections through Title VI, ADA Title II, and Federal Transit Administration funding programs (Sections 5307, 5337, 5339, 5310, 5309); the state-architecture vulnerability produced by PA Act 89's 2022 transition shifting the dedicated PA Turnpike Commission $400M contribution to General Fund; the 2024-2026 SEPTA fiscal crisis with staged service-cut implementation (August 24, 2025), 21.5% fare increase (September 1, 2025), court-ordered reversal on disparate-impact theory (September 4, 2025), capital-to-operating buffer ($394M, September 8, 2025), service restoration (September 14, 2025), and second-wave aversion (January 1, 2026); the FY 2026-27 budget context with Shapiro proposal of 1.75% sales tax to PTTF; and the structural constraint that federal protections are stable, state support is structurally vulnerable, and local leverage on SEPTA's operational decisions is indirect.

**Data provenance regime:** Internet-access regime. Track 1 architecture is directly documented from U.S. Code, PA statutes, and SEPTA primary sources. Track 2 outcomes draw on SEPTA system performance data, PA Governor's Office press releases, Philadelphia Court of Common Pleas filings, and DVRPC Tracking Progress dashboard.

**Data provenance scope statement:** Philadelphia-specific and directly documented: 2025-2026 SEPTA fiscal chronology (SEPTA board action June 26, 2025; service-cut implementation August 24, 2025; fare increase September 1, 2025; Judge Sierra Thomas-Street order September 4, 2025; PA Governor's Office $394M capital approval September 8, 2025; PA budget November 12, 2025; SEPTA FY 2027 proposed budget April 9, 2026); commute mode share data from ACS for Philadelphia County; SEPTA's structural ~$213M FY26 operating deficit and ~$192M FY27 deficit projection. National benchmark applied by inference: Title VI service-equity baseline per FTA Circular 4702.1B; ADA Title II compliance baseline per 42 U.S.C. § 12131 et seq. Structural inference: fare burden as share of household income by sub-area inversely related to income (ACS B19001 applied to documented fare structure). Held-open: precise documentary adequacy of SEPTA's Title VI fare equity analysis for the June 2025 action (G13-SD1-01); precise federal operating support eligibility within Section 5307 for SEPTA's specific situation (G13-SD1-05).

## Section 2: Authority Chain

**Constitutional Foundation.**

*U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cl. 3 — Commerce Clause.* Grounds federal regulation of mobility systems affecting interstate commerce. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: N/A.*

*U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cl. 1 — Spending Clause.* Authorizes federal financial assistance to state and local transit programs subject to federal program requirements (with limits per *South Dakota v. Dole* and *NFIB v. Sebelius*). *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: N/A.*

*U.S. Const. amend. XIV § 1 — Equal Protection Clause.* Grounds Title VI service-equity requirements applied to federally-funded transit (42 U.S.C. § 2000d). The September 4, 2025 court order — issued on a disparate-impact theory — operates within this constitutional grounding. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: HIGH (post-2025 federal EJ administrative architecture thinning per MC-03 affects implementation; statutory floor preserved).*

*Americans with Disabilities Act Title II (42 U.S.C. § 12131 et seq.).* Operates at intersection of Spending Clause and § 5 of the 14th Amendment grounds; requires public transit accessibility. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (DOJ Civil Rights Division enforcement is complaint-driven).*

*PA Const. art. VIII (Taxation and Finance) and art. IX (Home Rule).* Article VIII grounds General Assembly authority to appropriate state funds; Article IX establishes Philadelphia's authority to raise local revenue for SEPTA subject to LTEA enabling. The 1% local sales tax allocation to SEPTA operates within this state-permission framework (cross-reference D9 SD1 LTEA architecture). *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: LOW.*

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

*49 U.S.C. § 5301 et seq. — Federal Transit Act framework.* Authorizes the federal public transportation program under Chapter 53 of Title 49. The Federal Transit Administration (FTA), an operating administration within USDOT, is the principal federal agency for PA-3 transit. **FTA Region III** is headquartered in Philadelphia, with jurisdiction over PA, DE, MD, VA, WV, and DC. **FTA Region III Regional Administrator: Theresa "Terry" Garcia Crews** (per FTA Region 3 Staff Organization page; *Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per FTA staff page*). **FTA national Administrator: Marcus J. Molinaro** per MC-10 (*Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per Federal Register signature January 2026*). SEPTA is a direct grant recipient under multiple FTA program authorities. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: variable by program.*

*49 U.S.C. § 5307 — Urbanized Area Formula Program.* Formula-distributed federal operating and capital assistance. Operates as a statutory entitlement: formula set by statute; apportionment mechanical once national appropriations made; an urbanized area cannot be excluded from formula apportionment by administrative discretion. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: LOW.*

*49 U.S.C. § 5337 — State of Good Repair Formula Program.* Formula funding for fixed-guideway and high-intensity bus systems. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: LOW.*

*49 U.S.C. § 5339 — Bus and Bus Facilities Program.* Formula and competitive sub-allocations. *Statutory stability: HIGH (formula); MEDIUM (competitive). Administrative vulnerability: LOW formula; MEDIUM competitive.*

*49 U.S.C. § 5310 — Enhanced Mobility of Seniors and Individuals with Disabilities.* Federal floor for senior and disability paratransit access supplements. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: LOW.*

*49 U.S.C. § 5309 — Capital Investment Grants Program (CIG).* Primary federal discretionary program for fixed-guideway expansion projects (New Starts, Small Starts, Core Capacity). IIJA appropriated $1.6 billion per year from the general fund for CIG plus authorized another $3.0 billion per year subject to appropriation. *Verification clarification per MC-12: SEPTA's major Section 5309 CIG project is **King of Prussia (KOP) Rail** — the four-mile Norristown High Speed Line extension — which entered Project Development Phase under New Starts following FTA approval October 2021 (re-confirmed April 2025); SEPTA committed $390 million in its FY23 capital budget seeking up to 60% federal CIG share. **Trolley Modernization** is funded through SEPTA capital, state Act 89, IIJA, and FTA pilot grants (e.g., a $460,000 grant for a Route 10 corridor study), with a $714 million Alstom contract for 130 ADA-compliant trolley cars; first delivery 2027, all in service by 2031.* *Statutory stability: HIGH (statute). Administrative vulnerability: HIGH (multi-stage administrative review with significant discretion at every stage).*

*Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act (IIJA / "Bipartisan Infrastructure Law"), P.L. 117-58.* Signed November 15, 2021. Authorized surface transportation programs for FY2022-2026 and authorized up to $108 billion to support federal public transportation programs, including approximately $91 billion in guaranteed funding. **IIJA authorization expires September 30, 2026.** *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-02: As of April-May 2026, no draft text of the next surface transportation reauthorization has been released; USDOT issued a nationwide call for feedback July 2025; congressional extensions are the expected mechanism if no reauthorization is enacted by Oct 1, 2026. BASICS Act introduced February 9, 2026 by Reps. Rob Bresnahan (R-PA) and Kristen McDonald Rivet (D-MI) reflects ongoing legislative activity but is not the master reauthorization vehicle. House T&I Chairman Sam Graves (R-MO) signaled "back to basics" approach.* *Statutory stability: HIGH for current authorization period; trajectory uncertain post-September 30, 2026. Administrative vulnerability: HIGH on reauthorization outcome.*

*Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 (P.L. 119-75 / H.R. 7148).* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-01: Signed February 3, 2026 ending a partial government shutdown; THUD Act provisions **transferred** approximately $2.3 billion in unobligated IIJA balances — moving approximately $879 million from the NEVI Program ($503.8M formula + $300M competitive + $75M Joint Office of Energy and Transportation) and additional funds from the Reduction of Truck Emissions at Port Facilities Program into Tribal Transportation Program, INFRA, and Reconnecting Communities Program. Reconnecting Communities Program funded at $30M — 15% of the $200M obligated for FY2026 in original IIJA. CRISI rail program received only $7M in new appropriations in FY26 (95% of its $137M total derived from IIJA unobligated balances; 93% reduction from FY2025).* *Statutory stability: P.L. 119-75 enacted. Administrative vulnerability: HIGH on FY27 appropriations cycle.*

*Federal funding flow for SEPTA operating support:* Congress appropriates IIJA-authorized funds annually → FTA apportions Section 5307 formula funds based on population, density, and ridership data → SEPTA as designated recipient draws down apportioned funds. Operating-use eligibility for large urbanized areas like Philadelphia faces formula limits; capital-use eligibility is broad. FTA Triennial Review and Title VI Program review provide federal compliance oversight.

**State Statutory Layer.**

*Act 44 of 2007.* Required PA Turnpike Commission to provide PennDOT with $450 million annually for highways, bridges, and public transit. The Act's central funding mechanism — converting I-80 to a tolled highway — was rejected by FHWA in September 2008 (after a second rejection), but the PA Turnpike's annual payment obligation remained in place. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: LOW.*

*Act 89 of 2013.* Signed by Governor Tom Corbett; restructured the architecture: dedicated the full $450 million annual PA Turnpike payment to public transit, eliminated the $0.12/gallon state retail gas and diesel tax while raising the cap on the Oil Company Franchise Tax, and established a multi-modal fund. **The 2022 transition.** Act 89 contained a built-in transition that fundamentally altered the funding architecture beginning in fiscal 2022: the PA Turnpike Commission's annual payment to PennDOT was reduced from $450 million to $50 million in 2022, with the $400 million difference shifting to the state's General Fund. This converted the dedicated Turnpike-toll-funded transit subsidy into an annual General Fund appropriation, creating a structural vulnerability that did not previously exist: General Fund appropriations are subject to annual budget negotiations in a way statutory dedicated revenue streams are not. *Statutory stability: HIGH for the Act itself. Administrative vulnerability: HIGH on annual General Fund appropriation outcomes (the source of the structural state-match vulnerability).*

**State Agency Layer.**

*Pennsylvania Department of Transportation (PennDOT).* Primary state agency for federal-aid highway pass-through; administers state Act 89 dedicated funding flows; coordinates with SEPTA on state operating-support determinations. **PennDOT Secretary: Mike Carroll** per MC-10 (*Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per Governor's Office press releases on SEPTA fiscal interventions*). *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (operates within Act 89 framework but with significant agency discretion on federal-state coordination).*

**Pennsylvania-specific features.** Pennsylvania remains among the most generous state transit funders on a per-capita basis, but the Act 89 transition has created a structural state-match vulnerability other states with dedicated revenue streams do not face. The 2024-2026 SEPTA fiscal crisis is an artifact of PA's transit-funding architecture, not of national-level transit funding decline. SEPTA is also unusual nationally in operating all five major terrestrial transit modes (bus, trolleybus, light rail, rapid transit, regional rail) under a single authority — making federal program eligibility analysis more complex than for single-mode systems.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

*Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority (SEPTA).* Created by the Pennsylvania General Assembly on August 17, 1963; commenced operations February 18, 1964. SEPTA serves five counties: Philadelphia, Delaware, Montgomery, Bucks, and Chester. The majority of SEPTA's board members are appointed by the five counties. SEPTA is therefore not a Philadelphia city agency — it is a state-created regional authority with city representation on its board. SEPTA serves approximately 800,000 daily riders, including 52,000 students in the School District of Philadelphia. **SEPTA General Manager: Scott A. Sauer** ("interim" removed from title June 2, 2025; *Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per SEPTA press releases*). **SEPTA Board Chair: Kenneth E. Lawrence Jr.** (*Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per SEPTA June 26, 2025 board action press release*). *Statutory stability: HIGH (state-created authority). Administrative vulnerability: HIGH on operating budget (state appropriation dependent per Act 89 architecture).*

*Philadelphia 1% local sales tax allocation to SEPTA.* Local revenue mechanism through which Philadelphia directly funds SEPTA operations. *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-15: Per D9 Finance & Taxation v1.2 retrofit (verified 2026-05-11) handoff of F9-SD7-06 / UV-D9-12 to D13 retrofit scope, the precise "1% city / 1% SEPTA" characterization of Philadelphia's local 2% sales tax allocation remains an institutional-retrieval item; the structural finding that Philadelphia's 1% local sales tax allocation flows to SEPTA operations under LTEA-enabled architecture stands at any plausible operative split. Cross-reference D9 G9-SD7-06 / G9-SD7-07.* *Statutory stability: HIGH (LTEA architecture). Administrative vulnerability: LOW on the allocation itself; HIGH on the structural future under Shapiro's proposed FY 2026-27 1.75% statewide reallocation per MC-06.*

*SEPTA's 2024-2026 fiscal crisis — staged implementation, court reversal, and capital buffer.* Per MC-04 chronology — *August 6, 2025:* SEPTA announced new state funding must be secured by August 14 to avert service reduction beginning August 24. *August 24, 2025:* First wave implemented — 32 bus routes eliminated, 16 shortened, broader frequency reductions on Metro and Regional Rail, all special services ended. *September 1, 2025:* 21.5% fare increase took effect; $2.90 base fare for bus and Metro (tying NYC MTA for highest in the country). *September 4, 2025:* **Judge Sierra Thomas-Street, Philadelphia Court of Common Pleas, ordered SEPTA to immediately reverse all service cuts** (32 routes, 16 shortened routes, broader reductions, no further cuts permitted "until further order of court"). Order did not prohibit fare increases. Plaintiffs: consumer advocate Lance Haver and two riders, represented by attorney George Bochetto (Bochetto & Lentz; *Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: case-name parties and legal counsel in litigation citation per court filings*). Lawsuit theory: cuts disproportionately burdened Black, Latino, and low-income riders while sparing Regional Rail predominantly serving wealthier suburban commuters. *September 8, 2025:* **Shapiro Administration approved SEPTA's request to use up to $394 million of its FY2025-26 capital assistance allocation that had yet to be obligated** for operating purposes. *Note: this is SEPTA's own unobligated capital, not (as the prior-method OUTPUT's flag framing suggested) "PennDOT capital flexibility" — the action does not redirect PTTF dollars; it permits SEPTA's own capital to flow to operations.* *September 14, 2025:* Full service restored; 21.5% fare increase took effect simultaneously. *October 8, 2025:* Bochetto filed proposed class action targeting fare increases. *October 2025:* Federal Railroad Administration ordered emergency safety inspections of SEPTA's 223 Silverliner IV cars after a series of electrical fires; major Regional Rail service disruptions through fall; SEPTA borrowed 10 train cars from Maryland MARC. *November 12, 2025:* PA budget signed (Day 135 of impasse); $50.1B; **no new transit funding included** per MC-05. *November 24, 2025:* Shapiro directed PennDOT Secretary Carroll to allocate **$219.9M in additional capital funding to SEPTA** for Silverliner IV safety upgrades, restoration of full Regional Rail service, and federal safety order compliance. *January 1, 2026 second wave averted.* The five-Regional-Rail-line elimination (Paoli/Thorndale, Trenton, Wilmington/Newark, Chestnut Hill West, Cynwyd) plus 18 additional bus routes plus 9 PM curfew on remaining rail did not occur.

*SEPTA FY 2026-27 budget proposed April 9, 2026.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-13: SEPTA is proposing a total budget of $2.7 billion for Fiscal Year 2027 — a $1.84 billion Operating Budget and $920.7 million Capital Budget; increase of just 1.9% over current year. This proposal does not include fare increases or service cuts. The proposed budget reflects the second and final year of the $394 million capital funds transfer approved by PennDOT to support operations. SEPTA has realized close to $30 million in annual savings through ongoing austerity measures. The $920.7 million proposed Capital Budget is part of a $16.3 billion 12-year Capital Program with $7.7 billion dedicated to fleet replacement for trolley cars, L (Market-Frankford Line) cars, and Regional Rail cars. SEPTA's state of good repair backlog has doubled in the past decade to $10.2 billion. SEPTA Board to consider the proposals at June 2026 meeting; if approved, effective July 1, 2026.*

*Federal funding flow at the local level.* SEPTA receives FTA Section 5307 formula funds directly from FTA Region III. PennDOT acts as the state-pass-through entity for state Act 89 dedicated funding. Philadelphia provides the 1% local sales tax allocation. SEPTA fare revenue covers a portion of operating expenses (well below 50% systemwide; typical for major U.S. transit systems).

**Local Agency Layer.**

*Philadelphia Office of Transportation, Infrastructure, and Sustainability (OTIS).* Coordinates city transportation policy across departments. *Statutory stability: HIGH (Home Rule Charter). Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM.*

*Philadelphia Streets Department.* Streets Commissioner: **Kristin Del Rossi** per MC-10 (*Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per December 19, 2025 State of the City*). Jurisdiction over local streets used by SEPTA buses.

*Philadelphia City Council Streets & Services Committee.* Primary Council jurisdiction over transit-related city issues.

*Home Rule authority and limits.* Philadelphia's Home Rule Charter authorizes the city to enter intergovernmental agreements with SEPTA and to allocate the 1% local sales tax. The charter does not authorize the city to unilaterally modify SEPTA service levels or fare policy — those decisions rest with SEPTA's board. Philadelphia's leverage on SEPTA is exercised through board appointments, intergovernmental coordination, and budget negotiations, not through direct regulatory authority.

**Constituent Layer.** The aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5; placeholder reference here.

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

**Triggering event 1 — Transit-dependent low-wage worker accessing employment.**

*Step 1.* Worker without regular access to a personal vehicle, employed in a service-sector or healthcare-support job, commuting from a North/Northwest Core or West Core residence to a downtown or anchor-institution worksite. Monthly fare expenditure (post-September 2025: $2.90 per trip, or monthly TransPass at corresponding tier) consumes a significant portion of monthly take-home pay. *Decision point:* fare affordability vs. alternative-mode availability.

*Step 2.* Service reliability affects job retention — a SEPTA bus delay can mean job-loss-level consequences for workers whose employers do not tolerate tardiness. The August 2025 service cuts directly exposed this population: workers whose previously-used routes were among the 32 eliminated routes faced either longer combined trips with transfers or paid ride-share alternatives they could not afford. The September 14 service restoration removed the immediate exposure, but the fare increase persisted. *Decision point:* route reliability awareness; alternative-mode availability; employer flexibility.

*Step 3.* Title VI fare equity analysis (federal floor) is the formal protection for this population; whether the analysis was sufficient to flag the disparate impact of the June 2025 action is a question for SEPTA's Title VI documentation review (the underlying disparate-impact theory is what drove the September 4 court order). *Decision point:* whether disparate-impact concern was foreclosed at SEPTA's analytical stage or surfaced at the court-order stage.

*Step 4.* Federal Section 5307 formula apportionment continues regardless of state-level support fluctuations; the worker's transit access ultimately depends on whether SEPTA's operating budget can sustain service at the level federal capital and operating funding can support. *Pathway breakdown:* the federal-floor formula protection does not substitute for state operating support; the operating gap manifests as service reductions and fare increases experienced at the constituent level.

**Triggering event 2 — Senior or disability-status rider accessing paratransit.**

*Step 1.* Rider age 65+, or rider with documented disability, eligible for SEPTA's reduced-fare programs and (where eligible) for paratransit (CCT Connect or "Shared Ride Program"). ADA Title II requires SEPTA to provide complementary paratransit service in the area within ¾ mile of fixed-route service (49 CFR Part 37 implementing regulations). *Decision point:* eligibility application; functional assessment.

*Step 2.* Once eligible, paratransit service quality (on-time performance, ride duration, scheduling availability) determines effective access. Bus accessibility for fixed-route use depends on the 46% station-accessibility rate — meaning a majority of SEPTA stations are not fully accessible to riders using mobility devices. *Decision point:* paratransit availability for trip; trip-time tolerance; alternative-mode availability.

*Step 3.* Senior fare programs (free or reduced fare) operate under state authorization (Pennsylvania's senior transit subsidy is a state program, federal pass-through). The June 2025 fare action's impact on this population is mediated through state senior fare programs; for non-eligible disability riders the fare impact is direct. *Pathway breakdown:* the gap between formal ADA Title II accessibility protection and 46% station accessibility is the structural mismatch this pathway encounters.

**Triggering event 3 — Cross-jurisdictional commuter using Regional Rail.**

*Step 1.* Worker living in PA-3 (Philadelphia) and employed in a suburban job site (Bucks, Chester, Delaware, Montgomery counties), commuting via Regional Rail. The SEPTA Regional Rail system was historically structured as commuter rail bringing suburban residents to Center City employment; the reverse-commute pattern is structurally less well-served. *Decision point:* mode availability; transit-feasibility of suburban job site.

*Step 2.* Service frequency on the reverse-commute pattern is typically lower than on the inbound pattern. Fare structure is distance-based (zone-based), making Philadelphia-to-suburban Regional Rail trips relatively expensive on a per-trip basis. *Decision point:* fare affordability; service-frequency tolerance.

*Step 3.* The five-Regional-Rail-line elimination contemplated for January 1, 2026 — if implemented — would have severely disrupted this pathway for affected lines (Paoli/Thorndale serving Main Line employment; Trenton; Wilmington/Newark serving Delaware corridor; Chestnut Hill West; Cynwyd). The averting of those cuts per MC-04 preserved access; the residual fare-burden and service-frequency questions remain. *Pathway breakdown:* federal Section 5337 State of Good Repair funding supports SEPTA's Regional Rail infrastructure; federal Section 5307 supports operations; structural representation question is whether federal House representation engages the cross-jurisdictional commute pattern as a federal-program issue or as a SEPTA-specific operational concern (G13-SD1-04).

**Pathway breakdown patterns in practice.** The documented breakdown points for PA-3 SD1 constituents concentrate at the fare-burden / service-reliability pathway intersection (Triggering event 1) where transit-dependent low-wage workers experience the structural mismatch between federal-floor formula protection (Section 5307 stability) and state-architecture operating-funding vulnerability (Act 89 transition). Secondary breakdown points concentrate at ADA Title II station accessibility (Triggering event 2, Step 2) where the 46% accessibility figure represents accumulated capital backlog rather than current compliance posture; and at cross-jurisdictional reverse-commute service frequency (Triggering event 3, Step 2) where structural network design favors the historically dominant suburban-to-city commute over the reverse pattern.

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

*Data provenance note:* Commute mode share data is Philadelphia-specific and directly documented from ACS estimates for Philadelphia County. SEPTA system performance data (174M paid passenger trips FY23; ~$213M FY26 structural operating deficit; ~$192M FY27 projected deficit) is from SEPTA primary sources. The 46% bus accessibility station compliance figure is from SEPTA Title VI Program documentation. Sub-area-specific commute and fare-burden patterns are structurally inferred from documented citywide commute mode share applied to PA-3 sub-area income distribution (ACS B19001 by sub-area). Tract-level fare-burden retrieval was not performed at Phase 2; structural inferences hold at the sub-area level.

**PA-3 statistical profile.**

- Philadelphia County commute mode share: approximately 6 in 10 Philadelphia workers commute by car; about 18% commute by bus — the second-most-common transportation mode. Walking, bicycling, regional rail, subway/elevated, and trolley account for the remainder. Philadelphia is the 5th-highest U.S. metropolitan area for non-car commuters at 28%.
- Bus-mode share around 18% citywide (25% in some referenced data points) is among the highest in the United States, reflecting structural transit dependence of a substantial portion of the Philadelphia workforce.
- Suburban contrast: workers living in the Philadelphia suburbs overwhelmingly drive (87%) and rarely use buses (2%).
- SEPTA paid passenger trips FY23: approximately 174 million (an 18% year-over-year increase reflecting partial post-pandemic recovery).
- SEPTA structural operating deficit FY26: approximately $213 million (reduced from $240M after $30M+ in austerity savings, including a 7.5% fare increase prior to the 21.5% increase, hiring freeze, and consultant cuts). FY27 projected: approximately $192M (per SEPTA FY27 proposed budget April 9, 2026 per MC-13).
- SEPTA state of good repair backlog: $10.2 billion (per SEPTA FY27 proposed budget per MC-13; doubled over the past decade).
- 2025 fare structure: $2.90 base fare for bus and Metro post-September 1, 2025 fare increase (21.5% increase, ties NYC MTA for highest in the country). FY27 proposed: no fare increase.
- Bus accessibility station compliance: approximately 46% — second-lowest among major U.S. transit systems, behind only New York City MTA (G13-SD1-02).

**Geographic variation across four sub-areas.**

- *North/Northwest Philadelphia Core* (Strawberry Mansion, Hunting Park, Nicetown-Tioga, Cecil B. Moore, North Central Philadelphia, Fairhill, Kensington, Fishtown, Olney, Logan, Frankford). High bus-mode share concentration; bus-dependent population structurally most exposed to the August 2025 service cuts (the 32 routes eliminated included routes serving these neighborhoods). Subway/elevated (Broad Street Line; Market-Frankford Line) provides backbone north-south and east-west service through North Philadelphia. The North/Northwest Core sub-area population is the most fare-burden-sensitive sub-area in PA-3 given income distribution (ACS B19001 concentration in lower brackets per D1).

- *West Philadelphia Core* (Haddington, Cobbs Creek, Kingsessing, Mantua, Powelton, Mill Creek, Cedar Park, Walnut Hill). Bifurcated commute pattern: university/hospital professional employment around Penn, Drexel, UPHS at higher-wage end; surrounding neighborhoods mirror the North/Northwest Core pattern. Market-Frankford Line provides backbone east-west service; the 52nd Street and 40th Street stations serve major bus-rail transfer points.

- *Northwest Philadelphia* (Mt. Airy, Chestnut Hill, East Germantown, Germantown, Roxborough, Manayunk, East Falls). Auto-mode-share dominance higher than in the Cores; Regional Rail (Chestnut Hill West, Chestnut Hill East lines) serves commuter pattern to Center City and to suburban employment. The averted January 2026 Chestnut Hill West elimination per MC-04 would have severely disrupted the reverse-commute pattern (West Mt. Airy to Conshohocken / King of Prussia employment).

- *South/Southwest Philadelphia* (Devil's Pocket, Dickinson Narrows, Forgotten Bottom, Grays Ferry, Lower Moyamensing, Newbold, Passyunk Square, Point Breeze, Queen Village, Schuylkill, South Philadelphia, West Passyunk, Whitman; Eastwick, Elmwood, Kingsessing southern portion). The Broad Street Line provides north-south service through South Philadelphia, terminating at NRG Station; the southern terminus is principal access for Sports Complex events and Navy Yard employment. Broad Street Line does not reach Southwest Philadelphia; bus dependence is correspondingly higher in Southwest. Bicycle commuting ranks second in two South Philadelphia tracts — a sub-area-specific mode pattern not present elsewhere in PA-3. Eastwick / Southwest Philadelphia faces airport adjacency questions: SEPTA Airport Line Regional Rail serves PHL Airport but does not serve as commuter access for most Eastwick/Southwest residents.

The geographic variation pattern across the four sub-areas is characterized less by access-or-no-access than by what kind of transit access is available, what fare structure applies to it, and what reliability profile attends each modal option.

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

Per Standard 13, profiles are aggregate constituent types, not named individuals; profiles use stated categories with explicit illustrative-calculation data provenance disclosure.

**Profile 1: Transit-dependent home health aide — North/Northwest Core.**

*Constituent type:* Single adult, household earning approximately $28,000 from home health agency wages, residing in Strawberry Mansion or Hunting Park, no household vehicle, commuting daily to multiple client residences across Philadelphia and Center City.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Daily round-trip commute between residence and multiple client visit sites; reliance on SEPTA bus and Broad Street Line.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 1 — SEPTA TransPass purchase ($2.90 base / ~$96-104 monthly TransPass for unlimited rides); reliance on Section 5307-funded service frequency; exposure to August 2025 service cuts (this constituent's bus route to a North Philadelphia client residence may have been one of the 32 eliminated routes from August 24 to September 14, 2025); restoration via court order; persistence of the 21.5% fare increase post-September 14.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* At $28,000 annual wages (~$2,333 monthly gross; ~$1,950 monthly take-home after FICA, federal income tax, PA PIT, Philadelphia wage tax per D9 SD1 G9-SD1-01 architectural finding), the monthly TransPass ($96-104) represents approximately 5% of monthly take-home pay. *Illustrative calculation at stated wage level applied to documented fare structure; verification of monthly TransPass exact price at septa.org before public use — F13-SD1-01.* For context, the PA-3 income distribution per ACS B19001 places this household near the median for the North/Northwest Core sub-area. Pre-September 2025 fare structure (prior $2.50 base; ~$80-90 monthly TransPass) would have represented approximately 4.2% of monthly take-home — the 21.5% fare increase produced an approximately 0.8 percentage point increase in fare-burden share. *Illustrative calculation; structural derivation from documented rate change applied to stated wage level; structural finding (regressive fare-burden incidence) holds at any plausible specific figures.*

*Outcome:* The home health aide receives federal-formula-stable service (Section 5307 apportionment unaffected by state operating-funding fluctuations) at fare levels structurally above pre-September 2025 baseline; service reliability fluctuates with state-and-local operating support; Title VI fare equity analysis nominally protects against disparate impact but the September 4, 2025 court-order intervention was required to surface disparate impact in this case.

**Profile 2: Senior with mobility limitation — Northwest Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Retired worker age 72, on Social Security with modest pension, residing in Mt. Airy, uses a walker for mobility, lives alone, accesses healthcare at Penn Medicine Mt. Sinai and at Center City offices.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* Northwest Philadelphia.

*Triggering event:* Healthcare appointments and prescription pickup require travel to Penn Medicine sites and to Center City pharmacy; mobility limitation makes walking long distances infeasible.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 2 — eligibility for SEPTA Senior Citizen Free Transit Program (state-authorized; federal pass-through); functional assessment for CCT Connect paratransit if needed; for fixed-route use, dependence on station accessibility (the 46% station-compliance figure means this rider faces structurally elevated probability of encountering an inaccessible station).

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* PA Senior Transit Subsidy authorizes free fixed-route SEPTA service for seniors 65+; on Regional Rail, seniors pay $1 for off-peak travel; ADA Title II complementary paratransit fare is at most twice the fixed-route base fare ($5.80 per trip at current rates). *Illustrative figures from PA DOT Senior Citizen Transit Program and SEPTA CCT Connect fare documentation; verify current eligibility thresholds and fare structures at septa.org and dot.pa.gov before public use — F13-SD1-02.* The 46% accessibility figure (G13-SD1-02) translates structurally to a Mt. Airy resident potentially encountering inaccessible stations on a substantial fraction of trips; specific route-level station accessibility variation was not retrieved at Phase 2.

*Outcome:* The senior accesses fixed-route SEPTA via the free state-subsidized program; the fare-burden vector documented in Profile 1 does not apply at the same intensity, but the accessibility-deficit vector applies with particular force. The ADA Title II floor is the formal protection; the gap between the floor and 46% station compliance is the structural mismatch.

**Profile 3: Cross-jurisdictional commuter — West Philadelphia Core to Main Line employment.**

*Constituent type:* Single adult, household earning approximately $58,000 from administrative position at a Main Line corporate office, residing in Cobbs Creek, owns one vehicle but uses Regional Rail for daily commute due to parking cost and traffic congestion.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* West Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Daily round-trip commute to a Bryn Mawr or King of Prussia employment site.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 3 — Regional Rail Paoli/Thorndale Line access from Center City; the reverse-commute pattern (from Philadelphia outbound to suburbs in morning, returning evening) is less well-served than the inbound pattern; the averted January 2026 Paoli/Thorndale line elimination per MC-04 was a near-miss for this pathway.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* SEPTA Regional Rail TrailPass (zone-based monthly pass) for Cobbs Creek (Zone 1 origin) to Paoli (Zone 4 destination) is approximately $190-220 per month at current fare structure. *Illustrative figures from SEPTA Regional Rail fare schedule; verify current zone-based monthly pass price at septa.org before public use — F13-SD1-03.* At $58,000 annual wages, the monthly Regional Rail pass represents approximately 4.7% of monthly take-home pay — comparable to Profile 1's fare-burden share despite higher absolute income, reflecting Regional Rail's distance-based fare structure imposing higher per-trip cost.

*Outcome:* The cross-jurisdictional commuter accesses federal-Section-5337-supported State of Good Repair-maintained Regional Rail infrastructure; service frequency on the reverse-commute pattern is structurally lower than on inbound; fare-burden as share of take-home approximates Profile 1's pattern despite higher household income, because Regional Rail's zone-based fare structure imposes higher per-trip cost. Federal House representation's lever on this pathway is reauthorization-period engagement on Section 5337 funding levels and on cross-jurisdictional regional transit advocacy through congressional delegation coordination (G13-SD1-04).

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The most consequential thing to understand about SEPTA from a PA-3 representation perspective is that the agency's fiscal vulnerability is not a function of federal funding decline. Federal Section 5307 formula apportionment — the operating-and-capital backbone that makes SEPTA a viable transit system at all — operates as a statutory entitlement. The formula is set by Congress; the apportionment is mechanical once national appropriations are made; an urbanized area cannot be excluded from formula apportionment by administrative discretion. That federal-floor protection is the most stable element of the entire SEPTA financial architecture.

What is structurally vulnerable is the state-architecture operating-funding stream that Act 89 of 2013 was supposed to stabilize. The 2022 transition shifted $400 million annually from the dedicated PA Turnpike payment to the state General Fund. Once in the General Fund, transit operating support became one of many competing priorities subject to annual budget negotiation. The 2024-2026 SEPTA fiscal crisis is the operational consequence of this architectural decision — the General Fund route exposes transit operating support to political negotiation in ways the dedicated Turnpike route did not.

The 2025 sequence — board action June 26, fare increase September 1, service cuts August 24, court reversal September 4, capital-to-operating buffer September 8, service restoration September 14 — looks like crisis management because it was crisis management. What the chronology obscures is that the underlying architecture has not been resolved. Governor Shapiro's FY 2026-27 proposal to dedicate 1.75% of state sales tax revenue to PTTF would create a more stable funding stream beginning July 1, 2027 — but as of the retrofit verification date the proposal has not been enacted, and the Republican-controlled state Senate has previously rejected analogous proposals. SEPTA's April 9, 2026 FY27 budget proposal (no fare increases, no service cuts) reflects the second and final year of the $394M capital-to-operating buffer; SEPTA's General Manager Sauer described the long-term situation as "uncertain" in the budget release.

For the cross-jurisdictional commuter, the senior with mobility limitation, and the transit-dependent home health aide who appear in this sub-domain's aggregate constituent profiles, the experience of SEPTA is mediated by these architectural decisions in ways that are largely invisible to them. The home health aide whose route was eliminated in August and restored in September experienced a federal Title VI disparate-impact theory — articulated by attorney George Bochetto on behalf of Lance Haver and two other plaintiffs at the Court of Common Pleas — without ever needing to learn its legal architecture. The senior who encounters an inaccessible station encounters the ADA Title II compliance gap as a personal logistics problem rather than as a 46%-vs-100% structural metric. The Regional Rail commuter who paid $190-220 monthly for a TrailPass that nearly disappeared on January 1, 2026 experienced a federal-Section-5337-State-of-Good-Repair-supported infrastructure asset as a service-frequency and fare-burden question.

The structural representation question for SD1 is whether federal House representation engages this multi-channel federal-state-local architecture at the level where PA-3 residents experience it — bus route reliability, fare burden, station accessibility, paratransit availability — as opposed to the level at which federal program structure is most legible. The June 2025 fare and service action was technically a SEPTA Board action under state-created authority. The federal-aid program structure that protects against complete federal collapse did not prevent the action. The federal-floor protections that exist (Title VI, ADA Title II) required litigation by named plaintiffs at the state-court level to enforce in the disparate-impact analysis. Federal representation's engagement with this entire sequence — reauthorization advocacy, FTA program oversight, Title VI compliance attention, KOP Rail Section 5309 CIG advancement — is the substance of the representation question at SD1.

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G13-SD1-01: Title VI fare and service equity adequacy for the June 2025 SEPTA action.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented
- *Confidence:* HIGH that the action triggers Title VI analytical requirements; HIGH that the disparate-impact theory motivated the court order; MEDIUM on the documentary adequacy of SEPTA's analysis
- *Gap statement:* The SEPTA board's June 26, 2025 action (fare increase to $2.90, 50 bus route eliminations announced — 32 implemented in August 2025 first wave, 18 averted in January 2026 second wave; 5 Regional Rail line cuts averted; 21.5% fare increase implemented) is the type of major service change requiring Title VI fare equity analysis and service equity analysis under FTA Circular 4702.1B. The September 4, 2025 court order — issued on a disparate-impact theory — itself indicates that the original Title VI analysis did not adequately capture or did not foreclose the disparate-impact concern. Whether SEPTA's analysis adequately captured disparate impact on bus-dependent populations concentrated in the North/Northwest Core and West Core is structurally important and not yet documented at the analytical level the equity-impact assessment would require.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-04 chronology (court order September 4, 2025 on disparate-impact theory); Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (FTA Circular 4702.1B Title VI requirements); Section 4 Statistical Profile (bus-mode share concentration in PA-3 Cores).
- *Representation implication:* Federal House representation has direct levers on FTA Title VI compliance review with FTA Region III.

**G13-SD1-02: ADA Title II station accessibility deficit.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (figure); [SD] Structurally derived (gap characterization)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the 46% figure; HIGH on the structural gap; MEDIUM on the specific compliance trajectory and timeline
- *Gap statement:* SEPTA bus accessibility station compliance is approximately 46%, second-lowest among major U.S. transit systems behind New York City MTA. ADA Title II requires accessible transit operations and complementary paratransit; the 46% figure represents a structural gap between formal protection and actual accessibility for fixed-route users with mobility limitations.
- *Evidence trace:* SEPTA Title VI Program documentation (46% figure); Section 5 Profile 2 (senior-mobility constituent pathway encounter).
- *Representation implication:* DOJ Civil Rights Division engagement; FTA accessibility program advocacy; federal-aid project ADA compliance attention.

**G13-SD1-03: PA Act 89 transit funding state-match structural vulnerability.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented
- *Confidence:* HIGH
- *Gap statement:* The Act 89 transition that took effect in fiscal 2022 reduced the PA Turnpike Commission's annual contribution to PennDOT for transit from $450 million to $50 million, with the $400 million difference shifting to the General Fund. This converted the dedicated Turnpike-toll-funded transit subsidy into an annual General Fund appropriation, creating a structural state-match vulnerability that did not previously exist and did not exist in this form for other PA infrastructure programs (Motor License Fund constitutional dedication for highways; gaming-revenue dedication for parks via C2P2). The 2024-2026 SEPTA fiscal crisis is an artifact of this architecture; the November 2025 PA budget passing with no new transit funding (per MC-05) confirms the political durability of the underlying configuration.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 State Statutory Layer (Act 89 architecture); MC-04 SEPTA fiscal crisis chronology; MC-05 PA budget November 2025; MC-06 Shapiro FY 2026-27 proposal.
- *Representation implication:* Federal House representation has indirect levers through congressional delegation coordination with state legislative actors; reauthorization-period engagement on federal transit funding levels affects state-match calculations.

**G13-SD1-04: Cross-jurisdictional commute pattern federal-program engagement.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (structural feature); [SI] Structural inference (representational expression)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the structural feature; MEDIUM on its current representational expression
- *Gap statement:* Reverse-commute and intra-regional commute patterns within the SEPTA service area cross PA-3 / adjacent district boundaries (PA-5 Delaware County, PA-4 Montgomery County, PA-1 Bucks County). Federal transit programs operate at the urbanized-area level rather than at the congressional-district level, meaning the formula-funded service flows benefit cross-district commute patterns regardless of whose district members commute through. The structural feature: PA-3 representation engages the cross-jurisdictional commute pattern at the urbanized-area level rather than the district level.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (Section 5307 urbanized-area formula); Section 5 Profile 3 (cross-jurisdictional commuter pathway); SD7 cross-reference on cross-jurisdictional coordination.
- *Representation implication:* Inter-district congressional delegation coordination on regional transit advocacy; cross-jurisdictional federal-funding engagement.

**G13-SD1-05: Federal operating support capacity below operational need.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (statutory restriction); [SD] Structurally derived (capacity gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the structural restriction; HIGH on the SEPTA deficit scale; MEDIUM on the precise interaction between federal operating eligibility and SEPTA's specific situation
- *Gap statement:* Section 5307 formula apportionment includes operating-support eligibility but with substantial restrictions for large urbanized areas (Philadelphia is among the largest). The federal share for capital projects is up to 80%; for operating expenses, up to 50% maximum. SEPTA's structural ~$213M FY26 operating deficit (~$192M FY27 projected per MC-13) is not directly addressable through federal operating support at IIJA-authorized levels.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (Section 5307 operating-eligibility restrictions); Section 4 Statistical Profile (SEPTA operating deficit scale); MC-13 (SEPTA FY27 budget proposed; second and final year of $394M capital-to-operating buffer).
- *Representation implication:* IIJA reauthorization could address operating-support eligibility but this would represent a substantive policy change rather than incremental program adjustment.

**G13-SD1-06: SEPTA CIG project advancement uncertainty (KOP Rail / Trolley Modernization).**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (project status per MC-12 verification); [SD] Structurally derived (advancement uncertainty)
- *Confidence:* HIGH that KOP Rail is the major CIG project; HIGH on Trolley Modernization funding mix; MEDIUM on current advancement status of either through 2026
- *Gap statement:* The major Section 5309 CIG project for SEPTA is **King of Prussia (KOP) Rail**, the four-mile Norristown High Speed Line extension, which has been in the CIG New Starts Project Development Phase since FTA approval in October 2021 (re-confirmed April 2025). SEPTA committed $390M in its FY23 capital budget toward KOP Rail and seeks up to 60% federal CIG share. **Trolley Modernization** is the larger transformative initiative ($714M Alstom contract for 130 ADA-compliant trolley cars; first delivery 2027; all in service by 2031) but is funded primarily through SEPTA capital, state Act 89, IIJA, and FTA pilot grants rather than as a single Section 5309 CIG project. CIG operates with significant administrative discretion at every stage of project advancement.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-12 (terminology/factual correction from prior-method Trolley Modernization attribution); Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (49 U.S.C. § 5309 architecture).
- *Representation implication:* CIG project advocacy is a direct federal House representation lever, particularly significant for KOP Rail given its discretionary status.

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

The Philadelphia transit system is the primary mobility infrastructure for most PA-3 residents who do not own a personal vehicle, and its formal architecture is federally-protected, state-financed, and locally-operated. (1) **What the legal framework provides or promises:** Federal Title VI service equity and fare equity protections for federally-funded transit (42 U.S.C. § 2000d; FTA Circular 4702.1B); ADA Title II accessible transit operations and complementary paratransit (42 U.S.C. § 12131 et seq.); federal funding through FTA Sections 5307, 5337, 5339, 5310, and 5309 supporting SEPTA's operating and capital expenditures; Pennsylvania Act 89 transit funding architecture channeling state revenue through dedicated Turnpike payments and state allocations; Philadelphia Home Rule 1% local sales tax allocation to SEPTA. (2) **What PA-3 constituents actually receive:** Service that varies by sub-area, time of day, and modal option. The June 2025 SEPTA board action — service cuts implemented August 24, fare increases effective September 1, court order reversal September 4, service restoration September 14, planned January 2026 second wave averted (per MC-04) — directly affected service availability and cost. Bus route eliminations imposed differential burden on bus-dependent populations concentrated in the North/Northwest Core and West Core; Regional Rail reverse-commute frequency reductions imposed differential burden on Northwest Philadelphia and on cross-jurisdictional commuters; the 21.5% fare increase persists and applies across all modes. Paratransit availability varies; ADA Title II's accessibility floor produces 46% station compliance. Fare burden as a share of household income varies by sub-area in inverse relation to income (per Section 5 illustrative calculations). (3) **What accounts for the gap:** The mismatch between formal provision and actual experience is structural, not incidental. SEPTA is a state-created regional authority — Philadelphia's leverage over SEPTA's policy is exercised through board appointments and intergovernmental coordination, not direct authority. Pennsylvania's Act 89 transit funding architecture, which moved the $400 million dedicated PA Turnpike payment from the dedicated stream to the General Fund beginning in fiscal 2022, created a structural state-match vulnerability that did not previously exist. When state operating support is squeezed (as in 2024-2026), SEPTA's structural fiscal crisis surfaces — and the response options (fare increases, service cuts) push the burden onto the populations most dependent on transit. Federal Section 5307 formula apportionment is a high-stability statutory entitlement that protects against complete federal collapse but does not substitute for state operating support. The result is an architecture in which federal protections are stable, state support is structurally vulnerable, and local leverage is indirect. (4) **What the gap means for the representation analysis:** PA-3 transit-dependent residents — disproportionately Black and Hispanic or Latino given PA-3's demographic composition — experience the most-acute consequences of the architecture's vulnerabilities. The lowest-income PA-3 constituents bear the post-September 2025 21.5% fare increase as a structurally larger share of household income than higher-income constituents bear it; the disparate-impact theory motivating the September 4, 2025 court order is the legal articulation of this structural feature. The structural representation question is not whether federal House representation cares about transit but whether federal representation engages the institutional architecture at the level where PA-3 residents experience it — bus route reliability, fare burden, station accessibility, paratransit availability — as opposed to the level at which federal program structure is most legible.
