# D13 Physical Infrastructure — Sub-Domain 2: Water and Stormwater Infrastructure

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain number:** SD2
**Sub-domain name:** Water and Stormwater Infrastructure
**Scope statement:** The federal/state/local mandate-stack architecture for drinking water, wastewater, and stormwater infrastructure serving PA-3, with primary institutional embodiment in the Philadelphia Water Department (PWD) as a municipal enterprise utility; the federal-floor protections through SDWA Lead and Copper Rule architecture (LCR 1991 → LCRR 2021 → LCRI 2024) and CWA NPDES + Section 404 + CWSRF authorities; the PA-specific Article I § 27 (Environmental Rights Amendment) substantive constitutional grounding for water resources; the 2011 Pennsylvania DEP Consent Order & Agreement (COA, not federal consent decree per MC-11) governing the 25-year Green City Clean Waters green stormwater infrastructure compliance program through 2036; PWD's lead service line inventory architecture (511,000 total service lines; 16,805 confirmed lead; 157,823 confirmed lead-free; 351,514 of unknown material as of December 2025; ~$500M estimated total replacement cost); and the 2026 pilot Service Line Replacement Program (~1,000 lines in North and West Philadelphia). Cross-references D6 verified file (2026-05-11) MC-06 LCRI federal-architecture principal anchor.

**Data provenance regime:** Internet-access regime. Track 1 architecture is directly documented from U.S. Code, PA statutes, PWD primary sources, and PADEP COA documentation. Track 2 outcomes draw on PWD FY 2026 budget testimony, WHYY December 2025 reporting on PWD lead service line architecture, Circle of Blue 2025 reporting on Green City Clean Waters pace, and the City of Philadelphia / PWD lead service line inventory.

**Data provenance scope statement:** Philadelphia-specific and directly documented: PWD lead service line inventory architecture as of December 2025 (511,000 total / 16,805 confirmed lead / 157,823 confirmed lead-free / 351,514 unknown / ~85,000 newly-determined since October 2024 / ~480,000 letters covering ~360,000 properties December 2025 / 2026 pilot ~1,000 lines / ~$500M total replacement cost / HELP loan program); Green City Clean Waters program (2011 PADEP COA; 2012 EPA AOCC layered September 21, 2012; 25-year program through 2036; ~$4.5B halfway-mark investment; Year-10 ~2,100 acres achieved by 2021; ~7,400 acres remaining; ~236 acres/year recent pace; ~5,700 acres projected by 2035 vs. ~9,500 target); PWD FY 2026 budget $640.5M; PWD service approximately 2 million customers across Philadelphia + 10 suburban wholesale customers. National benchmark applied by inference: LCRR/LCRI national compliance pace baseline applied to PWD operational architecture; CWSRF national allocation formula applied to PA share applied to PENNVEST distribution applied to PWD funding receipts. Structural inference: customer-side replacement pace constrained by homeowner financing capacity for 5% of properties (G13-SD2-01); tenure-based delay for rental segment of housing stock (G13-SD2-03). Held-open: precise rate at which PWD's customer-side replacement pace will track LCRI compliance trajectory (UV-D13-08); magnitude of Green City Clean Waters trajectory recovery if recent pace adjusts (G13-SD2-04).

## Section 2: Authority Chain

**Constitutional Foundation.**

*U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cl. 3 — Commerce Clause.* Grounds federal regulation of interstate water systems and water quality. SDWA and CWA both rest on this ground. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: N/A.*

*U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cl. 1 — Spending Clause.* Grounds federal financial assistance to state water programs (DWSRF, CWSRF capitalization grants; WIIN, WIFIA programs). *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: N/A.*

*U.S. Const. amend. XIV § 1 — Equal Protection Clause.* Grounds Title VI environmental justice review applied to federally-supported water infrastructure investment. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: HIGH (post-2025 federal EJ administrative architecture thinning per MC-03 affects implementation; statutory floor preserved per <a id="g13-sd2-07"></a>G13-SD2-07).*

*PA Const. art. I § 27 (Environmental Rights Amendment).* Ratified May 18, 1971. Provides three operative components: (1) the people's right to clean air, pure water, and preservation of environmental values; (2) the common-property status of public natural resources, including waters; (3) the Commonwealth as trustee of these resources, obligated to conserve and maintain them. After decades of dormancy under the *Payne v. Kassab* balancing-test doctrine, the Pennsylvania Supreme Court in *Robinson Township, Delaware Riverkeeper Network, et al. v. Commonwealth*, 83 A.3d 901 (Pa. 2013) reinvigorated Article I § 27 as a substantive constitutional limit on state action affecting environmental rights. For water infrastructure, this means that state action affecting drinking water quality, surface water quality, or stormwater management is subject to substantive constitutional review — not merely the procedural balancing of *Payne v. Kassab*. *Statutory stability: HIGH (constitutional provision). Administrative vulnerability: LOW for the trust doctrine itself; variable for specific applications.*

*Constitutional tension specific to PA-3 water infrastructure.* Federal law establishes minimum water quality standards under SDWA and CWA, but enforcement depends on Pennsylvania DEP's primacy and on Philadelphia Water Department's compliance — a layered enforcement architecture in which federal standards may go unenforced if state primacy implementation is slow or under-resourced. The Article I § 27 substantive constitutional grounding provides an additional layer of state-level constitutional review that exists only in Pennsylvania and a small number of other states with comparable amendments.

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

*Safe Drinking Water Act (SDWA), 42 U.S.C. §§ 300f through 300j-27.* Primary federal statute governing drinking water quality. EPA promulgates National Primary Drinking Water Regulations under SDWA authority; codified at 40 CFR Part 141. State primacy is the implementing mechanism: Pennsylvania, like most states, has been delegated primary enforcement authority by EPA. Federal direct enforcement authority is reserved for instances of state non-action. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: HIGH on state primacy implementation; MEDIUM on EPA enforcement posture post-2025.*

*Lead and Copper Rule architecture — three iterations.* (1) Original **Lead and Copper Rule (LCR)** in 1991 (action level 15 ppb); (2) **Lead and Copper Rule Revisions (LCRR)** in 2021, with compliance date October 16, 2024; (3) **Lead and Copper Rule Improvements (LCRI)**, final rule promulgated October 30, 2024, effective December 30, 2024, with compliance date November 1, 2027. LCRI removes the lead trigger level, reduces the lead action level from 15 ppb to 10 ppb (0.010 mg/L), strengthens tap sampling procedures (first-liter and fifth-liter sampling at sites with lead service lines), and requires water systems to develop service line replacement plans. The LCRR compliance date of October 16, 2024 was retained for initial service line inventory submission, public education for known or potential lead service lines, Tier 1 public notification of action level exceedance (within 24 hours), and associated reporting requirements. EPA's stated enforcement posture late 2024-2025 was that the agency "intends to take prompt, meaningful enforcement actions to address noncompliance with the retained requirements from the 2021 LCRR that occurs following the October 16, 2024 deadline." *Verification update (2026-05-11): Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) MC-06 — LCRI defended by EPA in AWWA v. EPA with Respondents' Brief filed February 20, 2026; D6 MC-06 is the cross-domain principal anchor for LCRI federal-architecture defense; D13 SD2 PWD operational status (per MC-08) aligns with D6 MC-06 LCRI compliance trajectory.* *Statutory stability: HIGH for LCRI as enacted; HIGH on AWWA v. EPA defense posture per D6 MC-06. Administrative vulnerability: HIGH on compliance pace; HIGH on customer-side replacement financing.*

*Clean Water Act (CWA), 33 U.S.C. §§ 1251 et seq.* Primary federal statute governing surface water quality, wastewater discharge, and stormwater. Key authorities: Section 402 NPDES permit program (33 U.S.C. § 1342); Section 404 dredge-and-fill permits (33 U.S.C. § 1344, jointly administered with USACE); Clean Water State Revolving Fund (33 U.S.C. § 1383). The MS4 (Municipal Separate Storm Sewer System) program operates as a sub-authority of NPDES. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (state delegation; EPA enforcement discretion).*

*Federal funding flows.* IIJA appropriated more than $40 billion ($43.4 billion) for the Drinking Water State Revolving Fund (DWSRF) and the Clean Water State Revolving Fund (CWSRF) over FY2022-2026. Total IIJA funding usable for lead service line replacements is over $26 billion over five years. The flow: EPA capitalizes state SRFs → Pennsylvania receives capitalization grants → **PENNVEST** (Pennsylvania Infrastructure Investment Authority) administers the SRFs as low-interest loans (and some principal forgiveness) to municipal water systems → PWD applies for and receives PENNVEST financing for specific water infrastructure projects. Beyond the SRFs, EPA has separate IIJA-funded programs including Water Infrastructure Improvements for the Nation Act (WIIN) grants and Water Infrastructure Finance and Innovation Act (WIFIA) loans. *Statutory stability: HIGH (IIJA authorization through September 30, 2026 per MC-02). Administrative vulnerability: HIGH on FY27 reauthorization outcomes.*

*EPA Region III.* Headquartered in Philadelphia, with jurisdiction over PA, DE, MD, VA, WV, and DC. EPA Region III's Water Division oversees PA DEP primacy under SDWA, NPDES permit compliance under CWA, and the Green City, Clean Waters compliance regime. **EPA national Administrator: Lee Zeldin** per MC-10 (*Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per EPA press communications*). *EPA Region III Regional Administrator name not surfaced in retrofit verification; carried in UV-D13-10.* **EPA Criminal Investigations Division (EPA-CID)** brings criminal enforcement actions partnering with U.S. Attorneys' Offices.

*Justice40 framework revocation note.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-03: Justice40 (EO 14008), EO 12898 (Clinton 1994 environmental justice mandate), and EO 14096 (Biden 2023) were all revoked through 2025. All 10 EPA regional environmental justice offices were closed; EJScreen tool was prohibited from use in enforcement and compliance activities; ~$1B in IRA EJ community grants was targeted. This affects the SDWA / DWSRF disadvantaged-community considerations: federal program design for water-system EJ targeting has retracted significantly since 2024, leaving statutory floors intact but administrative implementation thin. Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) MC-04 and MC-32 for environmental-justice operational architecture; D13 SD2 aligns with D6 Justice40 operational status without duplicating D6's principal-anchor environmental-justice content.*

**State Statutory Layer.**

*Pennsylvania Department of Environmental Protection (PA DEP).* Primary state agency for water infrastructure regulation. PA DEP's Bureau of Safe Drinking Water administers the state's SDWA primacy; PA DEP's Bureau of Clean Water administers state CWA delegated programs. The **Southeast Regional Office** in Norristown serves Philadelphia County (and the surrounding southeastern Pennsylvania counties). *PA DEP SE Regional Office Director name not surfaced in retrofit verification; carried in UV-D13-11.*

*Pennsylvania Safe Drinking Water Act*, 35 P.S. §§ 721.1 et seq. — authorizes state primacy over SDWA implementation. **Pennsylvania Clean Streams Law**, 35 P.S. §§ 691.1 et seq. — authorizes state implementation of CWA-delegated programs. **PA Storm Water Management Act of 1978**, 32 P.S. §§ 680.1-680.17 — authorizes PA DEP regulation of stormwater across the Commonwealth. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (PA DEP resource constraints).*

*Pennsylvania Infrastructure Investment Authority (PENNVEST).* Independent state authority administering the DWSRF and CWSRF. Receives EPA capitalization grants and provides low-interest loans (and some principal forgiveness) to municipal water systems. PENNVEST is the most consequential state-level water finance institution for PWD. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (loan award decisions involve discretion within formula state allocation).*

*Pennsylvania-specific features.* PA's combination of SDWA primacy, Clean Streams Law CWA implementation, and Article I § 27 substantive constitutional grounding produces a regulatory environment that is, in formal terms, more protective of water resources than many other states. The federal floor is supplemented by state and constitutional layers. The recurring gap is in implementation: regulatory authority is concentrated at PA DEP, and PA DEP's resources for monitoring, enforcement, and primacy implementation have not consistently kept pace with the regulatory framework's formal scope.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

*Philadelphia Water Department (PWD).* Municipal enterprise utility providing integrated water, wastewater, and stormwater services. PWD serves over 2 million people in Philadelphia, Montgomery, Delaware, and Bucks counties (Philadelphia plus 10 suburban wholesale customers). PWD operates three drinking water treatment plants, three wastewater treatment plants, and a vast system of water mains, sewers, pump stations, inlets, outfalls, fire hydrants, and other critical infrastructure.

**Water Commissioner: Benjamin C. Jewell** per MC-10 (*Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per PWD press release December 5, 2025*). Appointed by Mayor Cherelle L. Parker in October 2025; previously served as First Deputy Commissioner, Deputy Commissioner of Operations, and Director of Wastewater Operations with the Water Department; with PWD since 2007. Holds a degree in Environmental Engineering from Northwestern University. Replaced former Commissioner Randy Hayman, who departed in July 2025; Jewell served as Acting Water Commissioner during the transition. The Commissioner manages all units and a staff of approximately 2,200 at the Water Department, directing the department's capital and operating budgets totaling approximately $1.5 billion annually (FY 2026 budget per PWD testimony: $640.5M operating; balance capital).

*PWD's enterprise utility structure.* PWD is funded primarily by water and sewer rates paid by customers (residential, commercial, industrial), supplemented by federal and state capital grants and loans (PENNVEST, EPA programs, IIJA-specific programs). PWD does not draw from the City General Fund for operations. This structure has important implications for cross-domain analysis: the PILOET fiscal gap (D9 SD4) affects the School District's General Fund-equivalent fiscal capacity in a way it does not affect PWD's enterprise budget. Anchor institutions that are tax-exempt for property tax purposes pay PWD rates as customers; anchor exemption operates differently for water than for taxes. *Statutory stability: HIGH (Home Rule Charter). Administrative vulnerability: LOW for the enterprise structure itself; MEDIUM for rate-setting under City Council oversight.*

*Lead service line program — current status.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-08: As of WHYY reporting December 17, 2025, of Philadelphia's **511,000 total service lines**: **16,805 confirmed to contain lead**, **157,823 confirmed lead-free**, **351,514 of unknown material**. Approximately 1 in 20 (5%) of properties may have a lead service line. PWD has determined the materials of approximately 85,000 previously-unknown lines since October 2024 (when PWD launched its public Service Line Materials Map). Approximately 480,000 letters covering approximately 360,000 properties were sent in December 2025 (some properties receive multiple letters — landlord and occupant). The Service Line Verification Program limited targeted excavations to the first 400 customers responding spring/early summer 2025. **The 2026 pilot Service Line Replacement Program is the first time PWD will replace lines outside of planned water main construction or HELP loan requests; approximately 1,000 lead service lines are budgeted for replacement in North and West Philadelphia, beginning 2026.** The HELP loan program (Homeowners Emergency Loan Program — zero-interest loans for replacing lead/galvanized lines) remains the primary customer-side financing mechanism. Total estimated cost of replacing all known lead service lines is approximately $500 million.*

*Green City, Clean Waters — Consent Order & Agreement and pace concerns.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-11: The compliance instrument is a **Pennsylvania DEP Consent Order & Agreement (COA)**, not a federal consent decree. In 2011, PADEP and PWD entered into the COA to address Philadelphia's combined sewer overflows. The COA requires PWD to implement the Long-Term Control Plan Update (LTCPU), known as Green City, Clean Waters, which is a first-of-its-kind combined sewer overflow compliance approach based primarily on green stormwater infrastructure. EPA layered an Administrative Order for Compliance on Consent (AOCC) on top on September 21, 2012. The 25-year program runs through 2036; pollution-reduction targets aim for 85% mass-capture endpoint with Water Quality Based Effluent Limits (WQBELs) at 5-year intervals.* Performance through approximately the halfway mark: Philadelphia hit its target of about 2,100 acres at the ten-year mark (2021), but the remaining target requires approximately 7,400 additional greened acres by 2036; recent installation pace has averaged about 236 acres per year. At that pace, Philadelphia would install approximately 5,700 acres by 2035 — substantially short of the ~9,500 acres required for full compliance. PA DEP granted PWD a seven-month extension for Year 10 deliverables on April 13, 2021, citing COVID-19 impacts. PWD's own October 2025 reporting acknowledges the trajectory shortfall. The total city investment is now approximately $4.5 billion at the halfway mark (compared to original $2.1 billion 25-year estimate); design lifespan of green infrastructure has been observed to be shorter than original 25-40 year projections (under 20 years in some installations).

*Tiered Assistance Program (TAP) and PWD income-based assistance architecture.* TAP is a state-and-locally-authorized income-based water-affordability program self-financed through PWD ratepayer cross-subsidy. Approximately 40% of Philadelphia households had unpaid water bills or water debt during 2012-2018; PWD reports approximately 80,709 customers currently enrolled in income-based assistance programs (TAP + SCD + others). Pre-launch FY18 estimate of TAP revenue loss was $16.3M; post-launch FY18 estimate was $3.9M — implying substantially lower actual enrollment than projected eligibility (G13-SD2-06). *Statutory stability: HIGH (locally enacted; not federally required). Administrative vulnerability: HIGH on enrollment outreach; HIGH on rate-base capacity.*

**Local Agency Layer.**

*Philadelphia Water Department.* Operational agency; see above.

*Philadelphia Streets Department.* Some stormwater catch-basin and inlet maintenance overlaps with Streets Department road operations.

*City of Philadelphia Office of Sustainability.* Coordinates city climate-resilience planning, including Eastwick flood resilience strategy.

*Home Rule authority and limits.* Philadelphia's Home Rule Charter authorizes PWD as a municipal enterprise utility; rate-setting requires City Council oversight; PWD's operations are bounded by state primacy frameworks (SDWA/PA DEP) and federal-floor compliance (LCRR/LCRI; CWA NPDES).

**Constituent Layer.** The aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5; placeholder reference here.

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

**Triggering event 1 — Homeowner notification of potential lead service line.**

*Step 1.* Property owner receives PWD notification letter (one of approximately 480,000 letters covering 360,000 properties sent December 2025) advising of potential lead service line status; property owner accesses PWD Service Line Materials Map online to confirm property-specific service line classification. *Decision point:* property owner awareness of letter content; willingness to engage with PWD verification process.

*Step 2.* If service line classification is "unknown material" (351,514 of 511,000 properties), property owner may request PWD verification via Service Line Verification Program (limited to first 400 customers spring/early summer 2025 — capacity constrained). If classification is confirmed lead, property owner faces customer-side replacement decision. *Decision point:* willingness to authorize PWD-side verification; capacity to authorize customer-side replacement.

*Step 3.* For customer-side replacement, property owner has three pathways: (a) HELP loan program (zero-interest loans for replacement; historical enrollment small relative to potential demand); (b) coordination with planned PWD water main construction (replacement paired with public-side work); (c) 2026 pilot Service Line Replacement Program (~1,000 lines in North and West Philadelphia, first PWD program to replace lines outside of planned construction or HELP loan). *Decision point:* HELP eligibility application; willingness to wait for pilot or water main work; capacity for self-financing if outside these pathways.

*Step 4.* For rental properties (51% of Philadelphia housing per D1 demographic context; G13-SD2-03), the property owner (landlord) holds the replacement decision; tenant occupants cannot directly authorize replacement; absentee landlords have weaker incentive to replace. *Pathway breakdown:* tenure-based delay compounds the customer-side financing gap (G13-SD2-01) for the rental segment of the housing stock.

**Triggering event 2 — Low-income household facing water bill affordability.**

*Step 1.* Household income at or below PWD's TAP eligibility threshold (approximately 150% FPL for full discount; tiered up to 250% FPL); receives or accumulates water bill payment difficulty. *Decision point:* awareness of TAP and related assistance programs.

*Step 2.* Household applies for TAP enrollment through PWD's customer service channels; eligibility verification involves income documentation; approved enrollees receive tiered discount on monthly water and sewer bill. *Decision point:* application completion; income-verification documentation availability.

*Step 3.* Once enrolled, household pays reduced monthly bill calibrated to income; SDWA does not federally require water-affordability programs (G13-SD2-02), so the protection is locally-authorized and operates within PWD's rate structure rather than as a federal floor. Federal LIHWAP (Low-Income Household Water Assistance Program) existed during the COVID period but lacks permanent authorization. *Pathway breakdown:* approximately 80,709 enrolled across full set of PWD income-based programs but FY18 pre-launch vs. post-launch revenue-loss data implies substantial enrollment-eligibility gap (G13-SD2-06); structural reliance on affirmative household application against limited outreach capacity.

**Triggering event 3 — Eastwick / Cobbs Creek flood-vulnerable resident.**

*Step 1.* Resident in Eastwick or proximate Cobbs Creek watershed area faces recurring flood risk from combined sources: Cobbs Creek overflow; Schuylkill backflow; combined sewer system overflow; precipitation events exceeding green stormwater infrastructure capacity. *Decision point:* property-level flood preparedness; access to flood insurance (NFIP coverage status).

*Step 2.* Interim resilience infrastructure includes Eastwick Near-term Flood Barrier Project (HESCO barriers, $1,383,069 federal funding via PA-5 Scanlon, October 2024 — *Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: Rep. Scanlon in legislative role per federal funding announcement*). Long-term Cobbs Creek levee architecture is in flux: USACE 15-foot proposal was scrapped due to induced flooding for up to 328 Delaware County structures; revised 8-foot design is under reconsideration; the Eastwick Flood Resilience Strategy has been pending release. *Decision point:* USACE design timeline; cross-district coordination outcomes.

*Step 3.* Federal floor protections (NFIP, FEMA, CDBG-DR) operate on declared-disaster and project-specific bases; long-term climate-driven flood risk requires coordination across multiple agencies (USACE, FEMA, EPA, HUD, PWD, Office of Sustainability) and across jurisdictional boundaries (Cobbs Creek = PA-3/PA-5 boundary). *Pathway breakdown:* no single federal program is designed to address the cumulative outcome — Eastwick's flood vulnerability intersects with Lower Darby Creek Superfund adjacency (cross-reference SD4), anchor institutional gaps, and absent comprehensive federal climate-flood program (G13-SD2-05).

**Pathway breakdown patterns in practice.** The documented breakdown points for PA-3 SD2 constituents concentrate at the customer-side replacement financing gap (Triggering event 1, Step 3) where the property-owner-owned portion of the service line is structurally outside PWD's direct regulatory authority; the LCRI federal-floor compliance trajectory depends on homeowner financing capacity for 5% of Philadelphia properties. Secondary breakdown at TAP enrollment-vs-eligibility gap (Triggering event 2, Step 1-2) where the structural reliance on affirmative household application produces enrollment substantially below eligibility. Tertiary breakdown at the Eastwick / Cobbs Creek flood-vulnerability pathway (Triggering event 3) where the cumulative federal-floor program architecture does not match the structural risk profile.

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

*Data provenance note:* PWD lead service line inventory figures are Philadelphia-specific and directly documented from PWD via WHYY December 2025 reporting (per MC-08). Green City Clean Waters pace and trajectory figures are directly documented from PWD October 2025 reporting and Circle of Blue 2025 analysis. PWD service area and enrollment figures are directly documented from PWD FY 2026 budget testimony. Sub-area-specific lead service line concentration is structurally inferred from documented citywide LSL distribution applied to PA-3 housing-stock age data (D7 sub-domain).

**PA-3 statistical profile.**

- PWD service area: approximately 2 million customers; Philadelphia plus 10 suburban wholesale customers (Montgomery, Delaware, Bucks counties).
- Lead service line inventory as of December 2025: **511,000 total service lines; 16,805 confirmed lead; 157,823 confirmed lead-free; 351,514 of unknown material**. Approximately 1 in 20 (5%) of properties may have a lead service line. ~85,000 lines determined since October 2024 launch of public Service Line Materials Map. ~480,000 letters covering ~360,000 properties sent December 2025 per MC-08.
- 2026 pilot Service Line Replacement Program: ~1,000 lead service lines budgeted for replacement in North and West Philadelphia.
- Estimated total replacement cost: ~$500 million.
- Green City Clean Waters program: 25-year compliance program 2011-2036; ~7,400 acres remaining to greened-acre target; recent pace ~236 acres/year; projected ~5,700 acres by 2035 against ~9,500 acres target — substantial trajectory shortfall (G13-SD2-04).
- PWD FY 2026 operating budget: $640.5M; total operating + capital ~$1.5B annually.
- PWD staff: ~2,200 employees.
- TAP and income-based assistance enrollment: ~80,709 customers across TAP + Senior Citizen Discount + Special Hardship + others; FY18 estimated revenue loss pre-launch $16.3M, post-launch $3.9M, implying substantial enrollment-eligibility gap per G13-SD2-06.
- Combined sewer system: serves ~60% of Philadelphia's land area (the older urban core); generates the CSO compliance obligation under the 2011 PA DEP COA.
- 40% of Philadelphia households had unpaid water bills or water debt during 2012-2018 (Pew analysis).

**Geographic variation across four sub-areas.**

- *North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.* Concentrated lead service line probability given housing-stock age (D7 cross-reference: pre-1986 housing stock predominantly in this sub-area, age-correlated with lead service line probability). The 2026 pilot replacement program targets North Philadelphia as one of two pilot geographies. Combined sewer area covers most of this sub-area. PWD water main replacement and CSO infrastructure investment concentrated here.

- *West Philadelphia Core.* The second 2026 pilot geography; substantial pre-1986 housing stock. Cobbs Creek watershed runs through southern portion (cross-reference Section 3 Triggering event 3 and SD2 G13-SD2-05).

- *Northwest Philadelphia.* Mixed housing-stock age; some neighborhoods (Manayunk, East Falls, Roxborough) along Schuylkill River with separate sewer architecture in newer development areas; older sections within combined sewer area. Wissahickon Creek subwatershed runs through this sub-area (cross-reference D6 SD6 conservation-partnership architecture).

- *South/Southwest Philadelphia.* Eastwick / Cobbs Creek flood-vulnerable zone in southwestern portion (G13-SD2-05). Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site adjacency (cross-reference SD4). Sports Complex and Navy Yard areas have specific stormwater management features. Schuylkill River frontage; FDR Park Phase 1 ($263M) and Phase 2 includes stormwater management infrastructure.

The geographic variation pattern is characterized by lead service line probability concentrated where housing stock is oldest (North/Northwest and West Cores); Green City Clean Waters greened-acre installations distributed across combined-sewer areas; flood-vulnerability concentration in Eastwick / Cobbs Creek / Schuylkill-adjacent zones.

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

**Profile 1: Owner-occupant homeowner with confirmed lead service line — North Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Homeowner age 55, single-family rowhouse purchased 1995, residing in Hunting Park, household earning approximately $42,000, no mortgage on the property.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Receives December 2025 PWD notification letter identifying property as confirmed lead service line; advised of HELP loan eligibility for customer-side replacement.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 1 — receives notification; verifies property classification via PWD Service Line Materials Map; considers HELP loan application; assesses whether property is on the 2026 pilot program list for North Philadelphia.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* Customer-side replacement cost typically $3,000-$8,000 depending on service line length and street conditions. *Illustrative range from PWD cost guidance and Greater Philadelphia Coalition Against Hunger summary materials; verify current PWD customer-side replacement cost guidance at phila.gov/water before public use — F13-SD2-01.* At $42,000 annual income (~$3,500 monthly gross; ~$2,800 monthly take-home), a $5,000 self-financed replacement would represent approximately 1.8 months of take-home pay. HELP loan zero-interest financing over typical 5-year term would convert this to approximately $83/month (~3% of monthly take-home). The federal SDWA LCRI compliance trajectory by November 1, 2027 depends on owner participation in pathways like these; structural finding (compliance pace constrained by customer-side financing capacity per G13-SD2-01) holds at any plausible specific cost figure.

*Outcome:* The owner-occupant homeowner faces a customer-side replacement decision that the federal SDWA LCRI architecture does not fully resource. The 2026 pilot program is a partial response (~1,000 lines vs. 16,805 confirmed lead lines). HELP loan participation is one option but historical enrollment has been small relative to potential demand. Federal floor (LCRI) sets a compliance trajectory; PWD operational architecture (2026 pilot + HELP + planned water main replacement) provides partial pathways; the structural gap between formal protection and household-level resource is the structural mismatch this profile encounters.

**Profile 2: Tenant in rental property with unknown service line — West Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Single adult tenant household earning approximately $32,000, residing in Cobbs Creek as renter of a multi-unit rowhouse owned by an absentee landlord based in suburban Pennsylvania.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* West Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Receives PWD notification letter (sent to occupant in addition to landlord) advising property is of unknown material; receives water service from PWD; pays utilities directly under lease terms.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 1, Step 4 — tenant cannot directly authorize replacement decision; landlord holds property-owner status; absentee landlord has weaker incentive to undertake the customer-side replacement than would an owner-occupant. Federal floor: SDWA does not impose specific tenure-based replacement requirements; LCRI service line replacement plans assume property-owner action. *Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* Rental segment of Philadelphia housing stock is approximately 51% per D1 demographic context; tenant population in PA-3 is structurally exposed to the property-owner-decision pathway without direct authority over the decision. Verification of current Philadelphia rental housing share at acs.gov before public use — F13-SD2-02.

*Outcome:* The tenant in the rental property faces a structural delay in resolving the customer-side portion of the service line replacement architecture — not because of insufficient federal-floor protection, but because the architecture's allocation of replacement authority to the property owner mismatches with tenure status. G13-SD2-03 captures this gap.

**Profile 3: Low-income household at TAP eligibility threshold — South Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Single-parent household with two children, earning approximately $34,000 (~150% FPL for family of three), residing in South Philadelphia near Point Breeze; tenant.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* South/Southwest Philadelphia.

*Triggering event:* Receives monthly PWD water and sewer bill; household budget tight; learns of TAP through community organization or PWD outreach.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 2 — household applies for TAP enrollment; provides income documentation; receives tiered discount calibrated to ~150% FPL eligibility level.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* Typical Philadelphia residential water and sewer bill at average usage is approximately $70-90/month. TAP enrollees at 150% FPL pay a tiered discount substantially below the standard rate (specific discount tier varies; some enrollees pay a flat $12/month at the deepest discount level). *Illustrative figures from PWD TAP program documentation; verify current TAP eligibility thresholds and discount tier structure at phila.gov/water before public use — F13-SD2-03.* At ~$34,000 household income (~$2,830 monthly gross; ~$2,400 monthly take-home), the difference between standard bill (~$80/month, ~3.3% of take-home) and deepest TAP tier (~$12/month, ~0.5% of take-home) is meaningful. The TAP enrollment-eligibility gap (G13-SD2-06) means many households at this income level may not be enrolled.

*Outcome:* The low-income household accesses locally-authorized water-affordability assistance not federally required. SDWA does not mandate water-affordability programs (G13-SD2-02); LIHWAP COVID-era program lacks permanent authorization. The protection is real but is local and operates within PWD's enterprise rate structure. The TAP enrollment-vs-eligibility gap is the structural mismatch this profile encounters.

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The most consequential thing to understand about Philadelphia water infrastructure from a PA-3 representation perspective is that the institutional architecture is in many respects well-developed — and that the residual gaps are concentrated where the architecture's formal scope leaves the resolution to private property-owner action.

PWD as a municipal enterprise utility has the regulatory authority, the technical expertise, and the federal-state funding access to operate one of the more capable urban water systems in the country. The federal floor under SDWA's lead and copper architecture has tightened significantly: LCRI promulgated October 30, 2024 with November 1, 2027 compliance date; lead action level reduced from 15 ppb to 10 ppb; lead trigger level removed; service line replacement plans required. The federal floor under CWA NPDES and the Pennsylvania DEP Consent Order & Agreement governing Green City, Clean Waters is similarly substantial: 25-year compliance program through 2036; ~$4.5B halfway-mark investment; the most ambitious green-stormwater-infrastructure compliance program in the country.

What the architecture does not resolve, and what the 2024-2026 verification cycle has surfaced, is the customer-side portion. The lead service line, as a physical asset, has two segments: the public-side portion (PWD's responsibility) and the customer-side portion (property owner's responsibility, from the property line to the building). PWD can replace the public-side portion through capital projects and pilot programs. PWD cannot, under current authority, compel property owners to replace the customer-side portion. The federal LCRI compliance trajectory by November 1, 2027 depends on customer-side replacement pace — which depends on property-owner financing capacity, which depends on tenure status, which depends on absentee-landlord incentive, and which is unaffected by IIJA DWSRF/CWSRF capitalization grant flow except where those funds are programmatically directed to subsidize customer-side work.

The Green City, Clean Waters trajectory shortfall is a different kind of structural problem. PWD installed ~2,100 greened acres by 2021 (the 10-year mark target). Remaining target ~7,400 acres by 2036; recent pace ~236 acres/year would yield ~5,700 acres by 2035 — substantially short. PA DEP granted a seven-month extension for Year 10 deliverables citing COVID; total city investment is now ~$4.5B at the halfway mark (more than twice the original 25-year estimate); design lifespan of green infrastructure has been observed shorter than original projections. PWD's October 2025 reporting acknowledges the trajectory shortfall. The structural question is not whether the program is operating — it manifestly is — but whether the architecture can recover the trajectory at sufficient pace to meet the 85% mass-capture endpoint by 2036.

The Eastwick / Cobbs Creek flood-vulnerability pathway is a third structural problem with a different mismatch. The federal program architecture — NFIP, FEMA, CDBG-DR, USACE flood-control authority — operates on declared-disaster, project-specific, or geographic-eligibility bases. The cumulative pathway through which an Eastwick household experiences flood risk crosses multiple federal program lines, multiple jurisdictional boundaries (Cobbs Creek = PA-3/PA-5), multiple anchor-institutional gaps (cross-reference D6 SD3 industrial-disinvestment landscape), and the absence of a comprehensive federal climate-flood program designed for this kind of cumulative-exposure profile. The Eastwick HESCO barrier project (PA-5 Scanlon, October 2024) is a meaningful interim resilience instrument; the long-term Cobbs Creek levee architecture is in flux pending USACE design reconsideration.

The TAP enrollment-vs-eligibility gap is the affordability dimension of the same general pattern. The protection (locally-authorized income-based water affordability assistance) is real and operates; the enrollment outreach architecture (affirmative household application; limited outreach capacity) produces enrollment substantially below structural eligibility.

For the homeowner with a confirmed lead service line in Hunting Park, the tenant in the unknown-material rental in Cobbs Creek, and the low-income household in Point Breeze who appear in this sub-domain's aggregate profiles, the experience of PWD as an institution is mediated by these architectural features in ways that the formal regulatory architecture only partially addresses. The structural representation question for SD2 is whether federal House representation engages the customer-side / property-owner-decision portion of the lead service line architecture (where the federal floor's compliance trajectory depends on private resource that is not federally provisioned), the Green City Clean Waters trajectory recovery (where federal CWA program support and PA DEP COA structure operate alongside but do not control PWD's installation pace), the Eastwick / Cobbs Creek federal climate-flood-program gap, and the TAP enrollment-vs-eligibility gap — or whether the engagement remains at the higher levels at which federal program structure is most legible. Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) MC-06 is the principal anchor for the LCRI federal-architecture component; D13 SD2's contribution is the PWD operational architecture against the federal floor.

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G13-SD2-01: LCRI customer-side replacement financing gap.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (inventory and program parameters); [SD] Structurally derived (financing-pace gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the inventory and program parameters; HIGH on the structural financing gap; MEDIUM on the precise pace at which customer-side replacement will track LCRI trajectory (UV-D13-08)
- *Gap statement:* SDWA LCRI compliance trajectory by November 1, 2027 requires replacement of lead service lines. Of Philadelphia's 511,000 service lines, 16,805 are confirmed lead and 351,514 are of unknown material (per MC-08). The customer-side portion of replacement is owned by the property owner and structurally outside PWD's direct regulatory authority. The 2026 pilot Service Line Replacement Program (~1,000 lines in North and West Philadelphia) is a partial response; HELP loan zero-interest financing is the primary customer-side mechanism; total estimated $500M replacement cost. The federal floor sets the trajectory; the property-owner-financing dependency is the structural gap.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 (LCRI architecture and PWD lead service line inventory); MC-08 (LSL inventory status); Section 3 Triggering event 1 (homeowner notification pathway); Section 5 Profile 1 (homeowner illustrative calculation).
- *Representation implication:* Federal House representation has direct levers on LCRI compliance support (DWSRF programmatic targeting toward customer-side; LIHWAP-equivalent permanent authorization; IIJA reauthorization on customer-side eligibility).

**G13-SD2-02: SDWA absence of water-affordability federal mandate.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (statutory absence)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the statutory absence; HIGH on PWD TAP architecture
- *Gap statement:* SDWA does not federally mandate water-affordability assistance programs. Federal LIHWAP existed during COVID but lacks permanent authorization. PA does not statutorily mandate water-affordability programs. Philadelphia's TAP and related programs are locally-authorized, self-financed through PWD ratepayer cross-subsidy. The protection is real but is structurally below the federal-floor level of energy assistance (LIHEAP federally authorized and recurring) — water affordability operates as a local supplement to a federal-floor that does not exist for water.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (SDWA scope); Section 2 Local Statutory Layer (TAP architecture); Section 5 Profile 3 (low-income household pathway).
- *Representation implication:* Permanent LIHWAP authorization is a direct federal legislative lever; SDWA amendment to incorporate affordability requirements is a substantive policy lever.

**G13-SD2-03: Tenure-based delay in customer-side replacement (rental segment).**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (housing tenure data); [SD] Structurally derived (delay mechanism)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the structural feature; MEDIUM on the precise delay magnitude
- *Gap statement:* Philadelphia rental housing share is approximately 51% per D1 demographic context. Customer-side service line replacement authority rests with the property owner; in rental properties, the landlord holds the decision; absentee landlord has weaker incentive to undertake replacement than would an owner-occupant. The rental segment of Philadelphia's housing stock structurally delays customer-side replacement pace, with the compliance-trajectory consequence depending on the precise share of rental properties with confirmed lead service lines.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 3 Triggering event 1 Step 4 (tenure decision-point); Section 5 Profile 2 (tenant pathway); G13-SD2-01 (financing gap framework).
- *Representation implication:* SDWA amendment to address tenure-status replacement-authority architecture is a substantive policy lever; HUD-PWD coordination on rental-property service line replacement is an administrative lever.

**G13-SD2-04: Green City Clean Waters trajectory recovery uncertainty.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (pace and target data); [SD] Structurally derived (trajectory gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the trajectory shortfall against the 2036 compliance endpoint; MEDIUM on the magnitude of trajectory recovery if pace adjusts
- *Gap statement:* PWD's Green City, Clean Waters program is approximately at the halfway mark of its 25-year PADEP COA compliance program (2011-2036). Year-10 target ~2,100 acres achieved by 2021; remaining target ~7,400 acres by 2036; recent installation pace ~236 acres/year. At that pace, ~5,700 acres by 2035 against ~9,500 target — substantial trajectory shortfall. PA DEP granted 7-month extension for Year 10 deliverables in April 2021 citing COVID. Total city investment ~$4.5B at halfway mark vs. original $2.1B 25-year estimate. PWD October 2025 reporting acknowledges the shortfall.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Local Statutory Layer (GCCW architecture); MC-11 (COA terminology clarification); Section 4 Statistical Profile (pace and target figures); UV-D13-09 (trajectory recovery magnitude held open).
- *Representation implication:* Federal CWA CWSRF programmatic support, IIJA reauthorization on stormwater eligibility, EPA AOCC compliance attention are levers.

**G13-SD2-05: Eastwick / Cobbs Creek federal climate-flood program gap.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (project status and architecture); [SD] Structurally derived (cumulative-program gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the documented project status; HIGH on the structural cumulative-gap finding
- *Gap statement:* Eastwick and proximate Cobbs Creek-watershed residents face recurring flood risk from combined sources (Cobbs Creek, Schuylkill, CSO, precipitation). Federal program architecture (NFIP, FEMA, CDBG-DR, USACE) operates on disaster-declared, project-specific, or geographic-eligibility bases — no single federal program is designed for the cumulative climate-flood-vulnerability profile this geography presents. Eastwick HESCO barrier project ($1,383,069 federal funding via PA-5 Rep. Scanlon October 2024) is meaningful interim resilience; USACE Cobbs Creek levee architecture in flux (15-foot scrapped; 8-foot under reconsideration); Eastwick Flood Resilience Strategy pending release. Cobbs Creek = PA-3 / PA-5 boundary requires cross-district coordination.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (CWA); Section 3 Triggering event 3 (flood-vulnerable resident pathway); Section 4 (sub-area geographic variation).
- *Representation implication:* Federal House representation has levers on USACE design coordination, CDBG-DR programmatic support, NFIP reauthorization with climate-risk integration, and inter-district congressional delegation coordination.

**G13-SD2-06: TAP enrollment-vs-eligibility gap.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (enrollment data); [SD] Structurally derived (eligibility gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the enrollment data and revenue-loss comparison; MEDIUM on the precise eligibility-vs-enrollment ratio
- *Gap statement:* PWD reports approximately 80,709 customers enrolled in income-based assistance programs (TAP + Senior Citizen Discount + Special Hardship + others). Pre-launch FY18 estimate of TAP revenue loss was $16.3M; post-launch FY18 estimate was $3.9M — implying substantially lower actual enrollment than projected eligibility. Approximately 40% of Philadelphia households had unpaid water bills or water debt during 2012-2018. The structural reliance on affirmative household application against limited outreach capacity produces enrollment substantially below structural eligibility.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Local Statutory Layer (TAP architecture); Section 4 Statistical Profile (enrollment figures); Section 5 Profile 3 (low-income household pathway).
- *Representation implication:* Federal LIHWAP-permanent-authorization is the closest direct federal lever; HUD-PWD coordination on rental-property outreach is administrative; structural finding stands at any plausible enrollment-eligibility ratio.

**G13-SD2-07: Post-Justice40 federal disadvantaged-community targeting retraction.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (federal architecture change); [SD] Structurally derived (programmatic targeting consequence)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the federal-architecture change; HIGH on the affected program identification; MEDIUM on the precise effect on PA-3 water infrastructure investment
- *Gap statement:* Justice40 framework (EO 14008), EO 12898 (1994), and EO 14096 (2023) were revoked through 2025 per MC-03. All 10 EPA regional environmental justice offices closed; EJScreen tool prohibited from enforcement use; ~$1B IRA EJ community grants targeted. SDWA / DWSRF disadvantaged-community considerations and CWA / CWSRF EJ programmatic targeting that operated through these frameworks have retracted significantly. Statutory floors (SDWA, CWA) remain; administrative implementation has thinned. Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) MC-04 and MC-32 for environmental-justice principal-anchor content.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-03 (Justice40 broader revocation); Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (Justice40 framework revocation note); D6 cross-reference.
- *Representation implication:* Federal House representation has levers on statutory EJ codification (legislative response to administrative revocation), DWSRF/CWSRF programmatic targeting through appropriations, and EPA program oversight.

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

Philadelphia water infrastructure operates within a federal-state-local architecture in which (1) **what the legal framework provides or promises:** federal SDWA Lead and Copper architecture (LCR 1991 → LCRR 2021 → LCRI 2024 with November 1, 2027 compliance date; 10 ppb action level; service line replacement plan requirements); federal CWA NPDES + Section 404 + CWSRF authorities; IIJA-authorized DWSRF/CWSRF capitalization $43.4B FY22-26 with state pass-through to PENNVEST and ultimate award to PWD; PA Article I § 27 substantive constitutional grounding for water resources; PA Safe Drinking Water Act and Clean Streams Law state-implementation; 2011 PA DEP COA governing Green City Clean Waters 25-year compliance program through 2036; PWD as municipal enterprise utility serving ~2M customers under City Council rate-setting oversight; locally-authorized TAP and income-based assistance architecture. (2) **What PA-3 constituents actually receive:** PWD operational architecture that has tightened substantially in 2024-2026 (lead service line inventory complete to 31% confirmed-lead-or-confirmed-lead-free; 2026 pilot replacement program; ~480,000 December 2025 notification letters; continued Green City Clean Waters investment; ~80,709 income-based assistance enrollees) within a structural compliance trajectory shortfall (Green City Clean Waters ~236 acres/year vs. ~9,500-acre 2036 target); the customer-side lead service line replacement portion structurally outside PWD's direct authority; the TAP enrollment-vs-eligibility gap; the Eastwick / Cobbs Creek cumulative federal-program gap. (3) **What accounts for the gap:** The architecture's allocation of customer-side authority to property owners produces a property-owner-financing dependency that the federal SDWA architecture does not resource; the Green City Clean Waters compliance instrument is a state COA (not federal consent decree, per MC-11) with PA DEP discretion on extension and modification operating within EPA AOCC oversight; the 2011-2024 federal EJ administrative architecture that supported DWSRF/CWSRF disadvantaged-community targeting has retracted per MC-03 while statutory floors remain; PA's combination of SDWA primacy and Article I § 27 substantive grounding produces a formal regulatory environment more protective than many other states, while PA DEP's resource constraints concentrate implementation gaps at the state-primacy level; the cumulative federal climate-flood-program architecture does not match the structural risk profile of the Eastwick / Cobbs Creek geography. (4) **What the gap means for the representation analysis:** PA-3 water-infrastructure-vulnerable residents — concentrated in oldest-housing-stock sub-areas (North/Northwest and West Cores) for lead service line probability and in Eastwick / Cobbs Creek geography for flood vulnerability, and disproportionately Black, Hispanic or Latino given PA-3's demographic composition — experience the most-acute consequences of the architecture's customer-side / property-owner-decision / federal-program-gap features. Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) MC-06 (LCRI federal-architecture principal anchor) and MC-04/MC-32 (environmental-justice principal anchors); D13 SD2's contribution is the PWD operational architecture against the federal floor, the Green City Clean Waters trajectory question, the customer-side / tenure / financing architecture, and the Eastwick / Cobbs Creek cumulative-program gap.
