# D13 Physical Infrastructure — Sub-Domain 3: Roads, Bridges, and Pedestrian Infrastructure

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain number:** SD3
**Sub-domain name:** Roads, Bridges, and Pedestrian Infrastructure
**Scope statement:** The federal/state/local mandate-stack architecture for surface transportation infrastructure serving PA-3, with primary institutional embodiment in PennDOT District 6 (federal-aid highway program implementation) and the Philadelphia Streets Department (local road network and pedestrian infrastructure operations); the federal-floor protections through Title 23 USC federal-aid highway program with NHPP, STBG, HSIP, CRP, and TAP formula apportionments; IIJA's Bridge Investment Program and Bridge Replacement and Rehabilitation formula authority; the Pennsylvania Motor License Fund constitutional dedication (PA Const. art. VIII § 11) protecting highway funding from General Fund-style appropriation vulnerability; the Vision Zero policy framework with Action Plan 2030 release November 25, 2025 (per MC-09) and underlying 2050 target revision (per MC-14); High Injury Network (HIN) concentration of 12% of streets carrying 80% of fatal and severe-injury crashes; the documented 2.4x KSI rate disparity in lowest-income census tracts vs. highest-income; the documented 1.5x BIPOC pedestrian KSI elevated risk; and the structural constraint that federal formula funding is stable, state Motor License Fund protection is unusually high, and local pedestrian-safety infrastructure capacity has not matched the documented disparity scale.

**Data provenance regime:** Internet-access regime. Track 1 architecture is directly documented from U.S. Code, PA Constitution and statutes, and PennDOT and Streets Department primary sources. Track 2 outcomes draw on Vision Zero Philadelphia annual reports, City of Philadelphia / OTIS Vision Zero Action Plan 2030 (November 25, 2025), Grid Magazine March 2026 retrospective on the 2050 target revision, and PennDOT bridge inventory data.

**Data provenance scope statement:** Philadelphia-specific and directly documented: Vision Zero Action Plan 2030 release November 25, 2025 (per MC-09); Mayor Parker 2024 decision to revise underlying target from 2030 to 2050 (per MC-14, surfaced Grid Magazine March 2026); $5M FY26 city Vision Zero funding (~$3.22/resident; FY27 budget pending); HIN architecture (12% of streets / 80% of KSI); 2.4x KSI rate disparity by income tract; 1.5x BIPOC pedestrian KSI risk; Vision Zero Capital Plan release scheduled summer 2026; cumulative pedestrian and bicyclist fatality and severe injury figures from PPD/Vision Zero annual reports. National benchmark applied by inference: federal IIJA reauthorization timing trajectory; NHPP/STBG formula stability. Structural inference: bridge condition trajectory in PA-3 specifically (UV-D13-07); Vision Zero 2050-trajectory funding adequacy at $5M/year operative level vs. advocacy-recommended levels (UV-D13-15).

## Section 2: Authority Chain

**Constitutional Foundation.**

*U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cl. 3 — Commerce Clause; § 8, cl. 1 — Spending Clause.* Ground federal regulation of interstate transportation and federal financial assistance to state/local transportation programs.

*U.S. Const. amend. XIV § 1 — Equal Protection Clause.* Grounds Title VI service-equity and environmental-justice review for federally-funded transportation projects (cross-reference G13-SD3-06 for current administrative posture).

*PA Const. art. VIII § 11 — Motor License Fund.* Constitutional dedication: revenues from gasoline taxes, motor vehicle fees, and similar transportation-related taxes must be used exclusively for "construction, reconstruction, maintenance and repair of and safety on public highways and bridges" and for related debt service. **This is the structurally most-protective state-level dedicated revenue stream for highway and bridge infrastructure in Pennsylvania.** The constitutional dedication is the structural protection that the Act 89 transit-funding architecture (per SD1 G13-SD1-03) does NOT have for transit. The asymmetry between highway and transit funding architecture in Pennsylvania is a key feature of the state-level infrastructure-funding landscape: highway and bridge revenue is constitutionally protected; transit revenue (since the 2022 Act 89 transition) is General Fund-dependent. *Statutory stability: HIGH (constitutional). Administrative vulnerability: LOW.*

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

*Title 23 of the United States Code — Federal-Aid Highway Program.* The principal statutory framework for federal transportation infrastructure funding. Federal Highway Administration (FHWA) administers the program. PennDOT is the designated state recipient for federal-aid highway funds; PennDOT District 6 covers Philadelphia, Bucks, Chester, Delaware, and Montgomery counties. *District 6 Executive name not surfaced in retrofit verification; carried in UV-D13-13.* *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: variable by program component.*

*Principal federal-aid programs:* **National Highway Performance Program (NHPP)** — formula funding for National Highway System (Interstate plus other principal arterials) preservation and improvement. **Surface Transportation Block Grant (STBG)** — formula funding with flexibility across most surface transportation purposes including bridge work on non-NHS routes. **Highway Safety Improvement Program (HSIP)** — formula funding for traffic safety projects, including pedestrian-safety infrastructure on federal-aid roads. **Carbon Reduction Program (CRP)** — IIJA-new formula funding for emissions-reduction projects. **Transportation Alternatives Program (TAP)** — formula funding for non-motorized infrastructure (sidewalks, bike lanes, trails); administered as a set-aside from STBG. *Statutory stability: HIGH for formula programs. Administrative vulnerability: LOW formula; MEDIUM for state-discretionary sub-allocation of TAP and HSIP.*

*Bridge programs.* **Bridge Investment Program (BIP)** — IIJA-new competitive grant program ($12.5B over five years). **Bridge Replacement and Rehabilitation (BRR)** Formula Program — IIJA-new formula authority ($26.5B over five years) for bridge replacement, rehabilitation, preservation, protection, and construction. **Off-System Bridge Set-Aside** — STBG set-aside for bridges off the federal-aid system. PA receives a substantial annual BRR formula apportionment; the funds flow through PennDOT to bridge projects statewide including PA-3 / PennDOT District 6. *Statutory stability: HIGH for current authorization period; trajectory uncertain post-September 30, 2026 (per MC-02).*

*Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act (IIJA), P.L. 117-58.* Authorizes the surface transportation programs above for FY2022-2026. **IIJA authorization expires September 30, 2026.** *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-02: As of April-May 2026 no draft reauthorization text has been released; USDOT issued nationwide call for feedback July 2025; congressional extensions are the expected mechanism if no reauthorization is enacted by October 1, 2026; BASICS Act February 9, 2026 reflects ongoing legislative activity; House T&I Chairman Sam Graves (R-MO) signaled "back to basics" approach.* *Statutory stability: HIGH for current period; HIGH-vulnerability trajectory.*

*P.L. 119-75 transfers of unobligated IIJA balances.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-01: Signed February 3, 2026; transferred ~$2.3B unobligated IIJA balances including ~$879M from NEVI Program and Reduction of Truck Emissions at Port Facilities into INFRA, Tribal Transportation, and Reconnecting Communities. **Reconnecting Communities Program funded at $30M in FY26** — 15% of the $200M originally obligated for FY2026 in IIJA. Affects discretionary grant trajectory for road-related projects.*

*Justice40 framework revocation.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-03: Affects USDOT discretionary grant equity considerations (Reconnecting Communities; BIP competitive; INFRA); statutory floor unchanged; administrative implementation thinned. Cross-reference D6 MC-04/MC-32 for principal-anchor EJ content.*

**State Statutory Layer.**

*Pennsylvania Department of Transportation (PennDOT).* Cabinet-level state agency administering federal-aid highway pass-through and state-level highway and bridge programs. PennDOT Secretary: **Mike Carroll** per MC-10 (*Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per Governor's Office and PennDOT press releases*). PennDOT District 6 (Philadelphia, Bucks, Chester, Delaware, Montgomery) is the regional operating district. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM.*

*Motor License Fund (per PA Const. art. VIII § 11).* See Constitutional Foundation. Funded by PA gasoline tax (currently ~$0.587/gallon — among the highest state gas taxes in the country, partly reflecting the architectural feature of constitutional dedication), Vehicle Code-authorized fees, and other transportation-related state revenue. The Motor License Fund's constitutional protection is what makes Pennsylvania's highway and bridge funding stream substantially more stable than its transit funding stream. *Statutory stability: HIGH (constitutional). Administrative vulnerability: LOW.*

*PA Vehicle Code, 75 Pa.C.S. §§ 101 et seq.* State motor vehicle law including speed limits, traffic safety rules, vehicle equipment requirements; the state-level framework that interacts with local traffic-safety regulation.

*Pennsylvania-specific features.* The Motor License Fund constitutional dedication is unusual in its strength — many states have statutory dedications, but Pennsylvania's is constitutional and therefore harder to modify. This produces an architectural asymmetry: highway and bridge funding is protected; transit funding (post-2022 Act 89 transition per SD1 G13-SD1-03) is not. Per-capita state gas tax and Motor License Fund revenue in Pennsylvania is high relative to most states.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

*Philadelphia Streets Department.* Operational agency for the local street network (~2,575 miles of streets), traffic signs and signals, ADA-compliant curb ramps, street paving, snow removal, traffic operations, and (post-restructure, see MC-11 referenced separately at SD4 for Sanitation/Streets split) refuse collection coordination. **Streets Commissioner: Kristin Del Rossi** per MC-10 (*Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per Mayor Parker's December 19, 2025 State of the City announcement reorganizing the Streets Department*). The Streets Department continues to oversee road maintenance, traffic engineering, and street infrastructure under the post-restructure division of responsibilities.

*Philadelphia Office of Transportation, Infrastructure, and Sustainability (OTIS).* Coordinates transportation policy across departments; houses the Vision Zero program office; coordinates with Streets Department, SEPTA, and the Mayor's Office of Sustainability on cross-cutting transportation issues. *Statutory stability: HIGH (Home Rule). Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM.*

*Vision Zero Philadelphia program.* Established by Mayor Kenney executive order 2016; targeted zero traffic deaths and severe injuries by 2030. **The underlying program target was revised from 2030 to 2050 by Mayor Parker in 2024.** *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-14: The 2030→2050 target revision was made by Mayor Parker in 2024 but was not widely surfaced until Grid Magazine's March 2026 retrospective coverage and subsequent Vision Zero communications. The November 25, 2025 Vision Zero Action Plan 2030 release maintained the "Action Plan 2030" framing while operating against the 2050 underlying program target — a 5-year-action-plan-against-25-year-target architecture. The revision was made amid persistent traffic fatality numbers in Philadelphia (year-over-year fluctuation around ~125-160 traffic deaths annually) and growing recognition that the original 2030 target was not on a feasible trajectory at then-prevailing funding levels.* The Vision Zero Action Plan 2030 released November 25, 2025 (per MC-09) is the most recent comprehensive program document. **Vision Zero Capital Plan release: summer 2026** (scheduled per Vision Zero communications around the November 2025 Action Plan launch).

*Vision Zero funding level.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-09 and MC-14: FY26 city Vision Zero operative funding is approximately $5M (~$3.22 per Philadelphia resident); advocacy organizations have called for funding in the range of $50-100M annually to match the trajectory required for substantial KSI reduction. FY27 budget pending; the Vision Zero Capital Plan release summer 2026 is the principal forthcoming document.*

*Home Rule authority and limits.* Philadelphia's Home Rule Charter authorizes the Streets Department, OTIS, and Vision Zero program. Streets Department and OTIS have direct authority over local streets; their authority does not extend to state-maintained roads within Philadelphia. State roads within Philadelphia include some major arterials (Roosevelt Boulevard / US-1; Broad Street; etc.) where PennDOT District 6 retains primary maintenance and design authority. This creates a layered authority structure within the city limits.

**Local Agency Layer.**

*Streets Department; OTIS; Mayor's Office of Transportation, Infrastructure, and Sustainability — see above.*

*Philadelphia Police Department Traffic Division.* Crash investigation and traffic enforcement.

*Philadelphia Health Department Injury Prevention Program.* Epidemiological tracking of traffic injury and fatality data.

**Constituent Layer.** The aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5.

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

**Triggering event 1 — Pedestrian on a High Injury Network corridor.**

*Step 1.* Pedestrian (resident, worker, or visitor) walks along or crosses a Philadelphia street identified as part of the High Injury Network (HIN) — the 12% of city streets that account for 80% of KSI (Killed or Seriously Injured) outcomes. HIN concentration includes major arterials (Roosevelt Boulevard, Broad Street, Cottman Avenue, Allegheny Avenue, others), the structural design of which makes pedestrian crossings hazardous (long signal cycles, multi-lane crossings, high posted speeds, inadequate refuge medians). *Decision point:* awareness of safer alternative routes; physical capacity to detour; time constraints.

*Step 2.* Federal HSIP funding flows to PennDOT for state-administered local-road safety projects; PennDOT District 6 selects projects in coordination with City. Vision Zero Capital Plan (release summer 2026) will articulate specific HIN intervention priorities. The Vision Zero Action Plan 2030 (released November 25, 2025) sets the framework. *Decision point:* specific HIN-corridor intervention project status; design timeline; construction schedule.

*Step 3.* The pedestrian's experience of the federal-state-local architecture is the engineered street environment — speed cushions, refuge islands, leading pedestrian intervals (LPI), bumpouts, ADA-compliant ramps, marked crosswalks. The 2.4x KSI rate disparity in lowest-income census tracts vs. highest-income (Vision Zero documented) means the engineered environment varies systematically by sub-area income — older, larger, faster arterials concentrate in lower-income tracts; protected and safety-engineered streets concentrate in higher-income tracts. *Pathway breakdown:* federal funding flows to PennDOT and to City; the engineered environment that results manifests the 2.4x disparity at the constituent-experience level (G13-SD3-01).

**Triggering event 2 — Bridge user (driver or pedestrian) on an inventory-listed structure.**

*Step 1.* Driver or pedestrian uses one of Philadelphia's bridges (state-owned, county-owned, or locally-owned) — over the Schuylkill (e.g., Spring Garden, Vine, South Street, Walnut Street, Chestnut Street); over local creeks; on grade-separated arterials. The structural and operational status of these bridges is monitored by PennDOT (state-owned) or by the relevant local owner; condition ratings are reported to the National Bridge Inventory. *Decision point:* whether bridge condition rating is sufficient for current use; whether posting (weight restrictions, lane closures) is active.

*Step 2.* Federal funding for bridge work flows through IIJA BRR formula, BIP competitive, off-system bridge set-aside, and NHPP formula (for NHS bridges). PA's substantial BRR formula apportionment is the largest dollar lever for bridge work; the precise PA-3-specific bridge inventory and its distribution by condition rating is held in UV-D13-07 (not retrieved at Phase 2). *Decision point:* state-prioritization of specific bridges; project advancement pace.

*Step 3.* The bridge user's experience is the bridge's operational status — posted, weight-restricted, lane-closed, full-service. The structural finding is that federal formula flow supports state-prioritized bridge work; the precise current condition of PA-3 bridges at the city-specific level is the institutional retrieval item (UV-D13-07). *Pathway breakdown:* federal-floor funding architecture is stable; state-implementation pace is variable; constituent experience varies with specific bridge status.

**Triggering event 3 — Cross-jurisdictional cyclist or pedestrian on the regional active-transportation network.**

*Step 1.* Cyclist or pedestrian uses Philadelphia's bike network (separated bike lanes; shared roads; Schuylkill River Trail; Wissahickon trails per SD6 cross-reference) for a journey that may extend into Bucks, Montgomery, Delaware, or Chester County. Federal TAP funding supports trail and bike infrastructure; STBG flexibility allows additional pedestrian and bike infrastructure investment. *Decision point:* network continuity; sub-area infrastructure availability.

*Step 2.* The regional active-transportation network is uneven across sub-areas: South Philadelphia tracts have bicycle commuting as second-most-common mode (cross-reference SD1 Section 4); North Philadelphia Cores have less bike infrastructure; Wissahickon trails in Northwest are well-developed (SD6 cross-reference). Cross-jurisdictional trail continuity (e.g., Schuylkill River Trail extension; Cobbs Creek Trail) requires inter-jurisdictional cooperation. *Decision point:* network awareness; sub-area infrastructure variation.

*Step 3.* The cyclist or pedestrian experiences federal floor (TAP/STBG funding) through local infrastructure investment that varies by sub-area; cross-jurisdictional continuity depends on inter-jurisdictional coordination. *Pathway breakdown:* federal program structure does not impose minimum sub-area uniformity; the variation is a state-local implementation feature.

**Pathway breakdown patterns in practice.** Documented breakdown points concentrate at HIN-corridor design (Triggering event 1) where the 2.4x income-tract KSI disparity expresses structural unequal exposure; at PA-3-specific bridge condition trajectory (Triggering event 2, Step 1) where the institutional-retrieval gap (UV-D13-07) leaves the precise inventory unclear; and at active-transportation network sub-area variation (Triggering event 3, Step 2) where federal-floor funding through TAP/STBG operates within state-and-local sub-allocation discretion.

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

*Data provenance note:* Vision Zero / KSI statistical profile is Philadelphia-specific and directly documented from Vision Zero Philadelphia annual reports and the November 25, 2025 Action Plan 2030. HIN architecture is from Vision Zero documentation. The 2.4x income-tract and 1.5x BIPOC pedestrian risk-elevation figures are from Vision Zero documentation. Bridge inventory at PA-3-specific level is held open in UV-D13-07; structural bridge architecture is from PennDOT and FHWA primary sources.

**PA-3 statistical profile.**

- Philadelphia street network: approximately 2,575 miles of streets.
- High Injury Network (HIN): 12% of streets carry 80% of fatal and severe-injury crashes; HIN concentration on major arterials (Roosevelt Boulevard, Broad Street, Cottman Avenue, Allegheny Avenue, others).
- Traffic fatalities: persistent in the range of ~125-160 annually in Philadelphia in recent years; year-over-year fluctuation without sustained downward trend at the level Vision Zero 2030 would require.
- KSI rate disparity: 2.4x in lowest-income census tracts vs. highest-income (Vision Zero documented).
- BIPOC pedestrian KSI risk: 1.5x elevated risk vs. white pedestrians (Vision Zero documented).
- Vision Zero FY26 city operative funding: ~$5M (~$3.22 per Philadelphia resident); FY27 budget pending; advocacy organizations call for $50-100M annual range to match trajectory needed for substantial KSI reduction.
- Vision Zero target: original 2030; underlying target revised to 2050 by Mayor Parker in 2024 (per MC-14); Action Plan 2030 released November 25, 2025 (per MC-09) maintains "Action Plan 2030" framing against revised 2050 program target — 5-year-action-plan-against-25-year-target architecture.
- Vision Zero Capital Plan: scheduled summer 2026 release.
- Bridge inventory at PA-3-specific level: held open in UV-D13-07.

**Geographic variation across four sub-areas.**

- *North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.* HIN concentration high (Roosevelt Boulevard, Broad Street, Allegheny Avenue, Erie Avenue, Cottman Avenue all traverse or terminate in this sub-area). Income-tract KSI disparity manifests with particular force — 2.4x disparity is heavily weighted toward this sub-area's lower-income tracts. Sub-area bicycle infrastructure: limited; bicycle commuting share lower than in South Philadelphia tracts. Sub-area pedestrian infrastructure improvements: targeted on HIN corridors but pace constrained by Vision Zero funding level (G13-SD3-04).

- *West Philadelphia Core.* HIN concentration includes Market Street, Lancaster Avenue, 52nd Street, Walnut Street, Chestnut Street; cross-CSO area; Cobbs Creek crossings (cross-reference SD2). University City protective infrastructure (Penn, Drexel anchor-institutional) contrasts with surrounding residential neighborhoods.

- *Northwest Philadelphia.* HIN concentration moderate; Wissahickon Drive and Lincoln Drive are notable HIN segments with documented serious-crash history. Cross-jurisdictional commute pattern (Northwest into Montgomery County) requires regional active-transportation network coordination.

- *South/Southwest Philadelphia.* HIN concentration includes Broad Street southern segment, Oregon Avenue, Penrose Avenue, Lindbergh Boulevard, Island Avenue. South Philadelphia bicycle commuting share among highest in PA-3; bike network density higher than in North/Northwest Core but uneven across sub-area. Southwest pedestrian infrastructure faces particular challenges with airport adjacency, industrial corridor crossings, and Cobbs Creek bridges.

The geographic variation pattern is characterized by HIN concentration on the major arterials that traverse all four sub-areas, with the income-tract KSI disparity expressing differently across sub-areas — most acute in North/Northwest and West Core lower-income tracts. Vision Zero infrastructure investment to address the disparity has not matched the scale at the current FY26 funding level.

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

**Profile 1: Pedestrian on Roosevelt Boulevard (HIN corridor) — North Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Worker, age 38, household earning approximately $36,000, residing in Olney; commutes daily by walking from residence to Broad Street Line station with a portion along Roosevelt Boulevard frontage; pedestrian crossings of Roosevelt Boulevard required for some trips.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Daily pedestrian crossings of Roosevelt Boulevard at signalized intersections; portion of route along Roosevelt Boulevard sidewalk.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 1 — pedestrian uses HIN-classified corridor; experiences engineered environment (long signal cycles, multi-lane crossings, high posted speeds, limited refuge medians); 2.4x income-tract KSI disparity applies structurally. Federal HSIP/STBG/TAP funding flows to PennDOT (Roosevelt Boulevard is state-maintained as US-1) and to City for HIN-corridor intervention; Vision Zero Action Plan 2030 prioritization; Vision Zero Capital Plan summer 2026 release will articulate specific intervention timelines.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* Roosevelt Boulevard has been the focus of Vision Zero / OTIS pedestrian-safety attention for nearly a decade with documented historical pedestrian fatality concentration. Specific intervention project lists are documented in Vision Zero Action Plan 2030; verify current project list at visionzerophl.com before public use — F13-SD3-01.

*Outcome:* The pedestrian experiences the federal-state-local architecture as the engineered environment of Roosevelt Boulevard, with HIN-corridor status producing structurally elevated exposure to KSI risk that matches the 2.4x income-tract disparity pattern. Federal House representation's lever on this pathway is HSIP/STBG/TAP reauthorization advocacy, Reconnecting Communities programmatic support (per MC-01 funded at $30M FY26), and direct Vision Zero / OTIS coordination on PA-3 federal-funding flows.

**Profile 2: Bicyclist in South Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Resident, age 28, household earning approximately $52,000, residing in Point Breeze; commutes by bicycle to Center City employment; uses combination of separated bike lanes, shared roads, and Schuylkill River Trail access.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* South/Southwest Philadelphia.

*Triggering event:* Daily round-trip bicycle commute; weekend recreational bike rides on Schuylkill River Trail and FDR Park (cross-reference SD6); cross-jurisdictional bike rides into Delaware County.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 3 — cyclist uses federal-TAP-supported and STBG-flexibility-supported bike infrastructure; experiences sub-area variation in network density (South Philadelphia bike network among highest in PA-3); cross-jurisdictional continuity depends on inter-jurisdictional coordination.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* South Philadelphia bicycle commuting share approximately matches the citywide top for any neighborhood; specific tract-level bike commute mode shares are documented in ACS commute mode share tables and OTIS bicycle planning materials. Verify current South Philadelphia bicycle commute mode share at acs.gov and otis.phila.gov before public use — F13-SD3-02.

*Outcome:* The cyclist experiences federal-TAP-and-STBG-supported local infrastructure investment that has produced a relatively dense network in South Philadelphia; structural representation question is whether infrastructure pace and quality can match the documented bicycle commuting mode share, and whether cross-jurisdictional continuity (e.g., Schuylkill River Trail extension into Delaware County) receives federal-program advocacy at the multi-jurisdictional level.

**Profile 3: Bridge user — South Street Bridge / Schuylkill crossings.**

*Constituent type:* Driver, age 45, household earning approximately $68,000, residing in University City; commutes by car to Center City employment using one of the Schuylkill bridges; depends on bridge availability.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* West Philadelphia Core / cross-river access.

*Triggering event:* Daily round-trip commute requiring Schuylkill crossing; bridge selection depends on operational status (any postings, lane closures, restrictions) and traffic conditions.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 2 — driver experiences bridge operational status; federal IIJA BRR formula and BIP competitive flows support state-prioritized bridge work in PA's broader bridge program; precise PA-3 bridge inventory by condition rating held in UV-D13-07.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* PA has historically ranked among states with substantial structurally-deficient-or-functionally-obsolete bridge counts; IIJA BRR formula has been substantial for PA. PA-3-specific bridge inventory by condition rating is the institutional-retrieval gap (UV-D13-07). Structural finding (federal floor support to state-prioritized work; PA-3-specific status varies by bridge) holds at any plausible specific inventory.

*Outcome:* The driver experiences federal-IIJA-supported bridge work through operational availability of specific bridges; the institutional-retrieval gap on PA-3-specific condition rating distribution means the precise current status is not retrieved at Phase 2.

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The most consequential thing to understand about PA-3 roads, bridges, and pedestrian infrastructure from a representation perspective is the architectural asymmetry between highway and transit funding within Pennsylvania, and the funding-vs-trajectory gap at the local Vision Zero level.

Pennsylvania's Motor License Fund constitutional dedication (PA Const. art. VIII § 11) is unusually strong. Gasoline tax revenue and other transportation-related fees flow to a constitutionally-protected fund that can be used only for highway and bridge purposes — they cannot be redirected to General Fund needs without constitutional amendment. This is the structural feature that makes Pennsylvania's highway and bridge funding stable; per-capita state gas tax revenue is high (PA gas tax is ~$0.587/gallon, among the highest in the U.S.). The result is that federal-aid highway program flows through PennDOT find a state-level match infrastructure that is well-protected and stable.

For transit, the architecture does not work the same way. Act 89 of 2013 (cross-reference SD1 G13-SD1-03) restructured the transit funding architecture so that the $400M annual PA Turnpike dedicated payment shifted to General Fund beginning in fiscal 2022. The result: PA's highway and bridge funding is constitutionally protected; PA's transit funding has been re-architected onto a General-Fund-dependent base that produced the 2024-2026 SEPTA fiscal crisis. The asymmetry is not symmetric across infrastructure modes within the same state — it is a feature, not a bug, of how the Pennsylvania architecture was designed.

At the Vision Zero level, a different mismatch operates. The Vision Zero program was launched in 2016 with a 2030 zero-traffic-deaths target. By 2024, Mayor Parker quietly revised the underlying program target from 2030 to 2050. The 2030 target was, at then-prevailing funding levels and trajectory, not feasible. The Vision Zero Action Plan 2030 released November 25, 2025 maintains the "Action Plan 2030" framing — as a 5-year-action-plan against the now-25-year program target. Operative FY26 city Vision Zero funding is ~$5M (~$3.22 per Philadelphia resident); advocacy organizations have argued the trajectory needed for substantial KSI reduction is closer to $50-100M annually. The Vision Zero Capital Plan release scheduled for summer 2026 will be the principal forthcoming articulation of how the action-plan-against-target architecture translates into specific capital investment commitments.

The 2.4x income-tract KSI disparity and 1.5x BIPOC pedestrian elevated risk are the constituent-experience layer of the architecture. Disparity at the documented scale is concentrated in the lower-income tracts of the North/Northwest and West Cores — the sub-areas where HIN-classified major arterials (Roosevelt Boulevard, Broad Street, Allegheny Avenue, Lancaster Avenue, Market Street) carry disproportionate KSI burden and where the engineered environment historically reflected fewer protective interventions. The 1.5x BIPOC pedestrian risk elevation tracks this concentration. Federal HSIP funding flows to PennDOT for state-prioritized safety projects; STBG flexibility allows additional pedestrian and bike investment; TAP set-aside supports non-motorized infrastructure; Reconnecting Communities Program (per MC-01) supports projects that reconnect divided neighborhoods. The structural representation question is whether federal flows match the documented disparity scale and whether project sub-allocation within PA reaches the PA-3 lower-income tracts at the pace and quality needed.

The PA-3 bridge inventory is the institutional retrieval gap (UV-D13-07). PA has historically had substantial structurally-deficient-or-functionally-obsolete bridge counts statewide; IIJA BRR formula has provided substantial funding for PA bridge work; the specific distribution of PA-3 bridges by condition rating was not retrieved at Phase 2. The Schuylkill crossings (Spring Garden, Vine, South Street, Walnut, Chestnut, others), the Tacony-Palmyra (Philadelphia-Burlington County), Cobbs Creek crossings, and other PA-3 bridges have variable ownership (state, county, city, authority) and variable condition; the structural finding (federal floor support to state-prioritized work) holds at any plausible specific inventory.

For the pedestrian on Roosevelt Boulevard, the bicyclist in Point Breeze, and the bridge user crossing the Schuylkill who appear in this sub-domain's aggregate profiles, the experience of the federal-state-local roads-bridges-pedestrian architecture is the engineered environment they navigate daily — and the structural representation question is whether federal House representation engages the architecture at the level where the documented 2.4x income-tract disparity and 1.5x BIPOC pedestrian risk elevation operate.

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G13-SD3-01: HIN-corridor pedestrian-safety income-tract disparity.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (Vision Zero data); [SD] Structurally derived (federal-flow-to-disparity gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the documented 2.4x KSI disparity and 12%/80% HIN concentration; MEDIUM on the precise federal-flow-to-disparity match
- *Gap statement:* 12% of Philadelphia streets carry 80% of fatal and severe-injury crashes (HIN); 2.4x KSI rate disparity in lowest-income tracts vs. highest-income; 1.5x BIPOC pedestrian risk elevation. Federal HSIP/STBG/TAP/Reconnecting Communities flows support state-and-local prioritization but do not impose minimum disparity-reduction trajectory. The gap between documented disparity scale and intervention pace is the structural mismatch.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 4 Statistical Profile (HIN architecture and disparity figures); Section 3 Triggering event 1 (pedestrian pathway); MC-09 (Vision Zero Action Plan 2030); MC-14 (target revision to 2050).
- *Representation implication:* HSIP/STBG/TAP/Reconnecting Communities reauthorization advocacy; coordination with PennDOT District 6 on PA-3 sub-allocation; Vision Zero engagement at federal-flow level.

**G13-SD3-02: Vision Zero 2030 → 2050 target revision and trajectory.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (target revision); [SD] Structurally derived (trajectory gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the revision; HIGH on the FY26 funding level; MEDIUM on the precise trajectory match
- *Gap statement:* Mayor Parker revised the Vision Zero program target from 2030 to 2050 in 2024 (per MC-14); Action Plan 2030 released November 25, 2025 (per MC-09) operates as 5-year-action-plan against the 25-year revised target. Operative FY26 funding ~$5M; advocacy-recommended $50-100M annually. Vision Zero Capital Plan summer 2026 release pending. The trajectory-vs-funding gap is the structural mismatch.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-09 (Action Plan 2030); MC-14 (target revision); Section 2 Local Statutory Layer (Vision Zero architecture); UV-D13-15 (trajectory adequacy held open).
- *Representation implication:* Vision Zero Capital Plan release engagement; federal HSIP/STBG/TAP/Reconnecting Communities flow advocacy.

**G13-SD3-03: Roosevelt Boulevard HIN concentration and federal-funding engagement.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (HIN designation); [SD] Structurally derived (concentration pattern)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on Roosevelt Boulevard's HIN concentration; MEDIUM on precise current intervention project status
- *Gap statement:* Roosevelt Boulevard / US-1 is a longstanding focus of pedestrian-safety attention in Philadelphia. State-maintained (PennDOT) status; HIN-classified; long-corridor pedestrian-crossing risk profile; multi-mode use including SEPTA bus routes (cross-reference SD1). Federal IIJA Reconnecting Communities funded at $30M FY26 per MC-01 (significantly reduced from $200M originally obligated); project-specific advancement timing for Roosevelt Boulevard intervention not retrieved at Phase 2.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 (HIN architecture); Section 3 Triggering event 1; MC-01 (P.L. 119-75 transfers including Reconnecting Communities reduction); Section 5 Profile 1.
- *Representation implication:* Reconnecting Communities Program FY27 reauthorization advocacy; PennDOT coordination on Roosevelt Boulevard.

**G13-SD3-04: Pedestrian-infrastructure pace at FY26 Vision Zero funding level.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (FY26 funding); [SD] Structurally derived (pace-disparity gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the FY26 ~$5M figure; HIGH on the structural pace gap
- *Gap statement:* FY26 city Vision Zero funding ~$5M (~$3.22 per resident); structural pace of pedestrian-safety infrastructure investment at this level does not match the trajectory needed to address the 2.4x income-tract KSI disparity at meaningful scale within the Action Plan 2030 5-year horizon (G13-SD3-01). FY27 budget pending; Vision Zero Capital Plan summer 2026 release will articulate specific capital trajectory.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-09 (Action Plan 2030); MC-14 (target revision); Section 4 Statistical Profile.
- *Representation implication:* Federal HSIP/STBG/TAP flow advocacy; FY27 PA budget engagement on transportation safety; Vision Zero Capital Plan summer 2026 engagement.

**G13-SD3-05: PA-3-specific bridge condition trajectory institutional retrieval gap.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (PA-aggregate bridge architecture); [SD] Structurally derived (PA-3-specific gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the federal floor architecture; MEDIUM on the precise PA-3-specific status
- *Gap statement:* Federal IIJA BRR formula and BIP competitive flows support PA bridge work at substantial annual scale. PA-3-specific bridge inventory by condition rating not retrieved at Phase 2 (UV-D13-07). Structural finding (federal floor stable; state-prioritization pace variable; PA-3-specific status uneven) holds.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (BRR/BIP); Section 3 Triggering event 2; UV-D13-07 (PA-3 bridge inventory institutional-retrieval item).
- *Representation implication:* PennDOT District 6 coordination on PA-3-specific bridge prioritization; IIJA BRR/BIP reauthorization advocacy.

**G13-SD3-06: Post-Justice40 federal disadvantaged-community targeting retraction (roads/bridges/pedestrian).**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (federal architecture change); [SD] Structurally derived (programmatic targeting consequence)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the federal architecture change; HIGH on affected program identification; MEDIUM on the precise effect on PA-3 roads/bridges/pedestrian projects
- *Gap statement:* Per MC-03, Justice40 (EO 14008), EO 12898, and EO 14096 revoked through 2025. USDOT discretionary grant equity considerations (Reconnecting Communities; BIP competitive; INFRA) operated through these frameworks; administrative targeting has retracted; statutory floors remain. Affects PA-3 disadvantaged-community-eligible project flows at discretionary-grant level. Cross-reference D6 MC-04/MC-32 for principal-anchor EJ content.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-03 (Justice40 broader revocation); Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (Justice40 framework note); D6 cross-reference.
- *Representation implication:* Federal statutory EJ codification (legislative response to administrative revocation); FY27 IIJA reauthorization EJ-criteria advocacy.

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

PA-3 surface transportation infrastructure operates within a federal-state-local architecture in which (1) **what the legal framework provides or promises:** federal Title 23 USC federal-aid highway program with NHPP, STBG, HSIP, CRP, TAP formula apportionments; IIJA BRR formula and BIP competitive bridge programs; Reconnecting Communities Program ($30M FY26 per MC-01); Section 5310 paratransit cross-reference SD1; Pennsylvania Motor License Fund constitutional dedication (PA Const. art. VIII § 11) protecting state highway funding; PennDOT District 6 federal-aid implementation; Philadelphia Streets Department local-street operations; OTIS coordination authority; Vision Zero program with Action Plan 2030 against revised 2050 target. (2) **What PA-3 constituents actually receive:** structural federal-floor protection through formula apportionments; state Motor License Fund stability at higher-than-other-states level; Vision Zero program operating at ~$5M FY26 against 2.4x income-tract KSI disparity and 1.5x BIPOC pedestrian risk elevation; bridge inventory at PA-3-specific level subject to PennDOT prioritization within PA-statewide BRR formula flow; pedestrian-safety infrastructure varying systematically by sub-area income with HIN concentration on major arterials. (3) **What accounts for the gap:** PA's architectural asymmetry between constitutionally-protected highway funding and state-architecture-vulnerable transit funding (cross-reference SD1 G13-SD1-03) produces stable highway flows but does not translate into commensurate pedestrian-safety investment at the level needed to address the documented disparity; Vision Zero funding architecture at ~$5M FY26 operates below advocacy-recommended trajectory levels for the disparity scale; the post-2024 Vision Zero target revision (2030 → 2050) reflects feasibility recognition while preserving 5-year Action Plan framing; federal disadvantaged-community targeting via Justice40 has retracted (MC-03) while statutory floors remain. (4) **What the gap means for the representation analysis:** PA-3 lower-income tracts disproportionately concentrated in North/Northwest and West Cores experience the 2.4x KSI disparity directly through HIN-corridor exposure; BIPOC pedestrians experience the 1.5x elevated risk through engineered-environment differential. The structural representation question is whether federal House representation engages the federal-flow-to-disparity match (HSIP/STBG/TAP/Reconnecting Communities reauthorization advocacy), the Vision Zero Capital Plan summer 2026 release, the PennDOT District 6 PA-3 sub-allocation, and the Roosevelt Boulevard / major arterial intervention pace — or whether engagement remains at the level at which federal-aid highway architecture is most legible.

# D13 Physical Infrastructure — Sub-Domain 4: Solid Waste, Sanitation, and Illegal Dumping

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain number:** SD4
**Sub-domain name:** Solid Waste, Sanitation, and Illegal Dumping
**Scope statement:** The federal/state/local mandate-stack architecture for solid waste management, sanitation, and illegal dumping abatement serving PA-3, with primary institutional embodiment in the post-restructure **Philadelphia Sanitation Department** (carved out of the Streets Department by Mayor Parker's December 2025 announcement) and the **Office of Clean and Green Initiatives (OCGI)** led by Carlton Williams as cross-departmental coordinator; the federal-floor protections through RCRA Subtitle D (non-hazardous solid waste) and Subtitle C (hazardous waste) and CERCLA where Superfund jurisdiction applies (Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site, PA-3/PA-5 boundary); the Pennsylvania Solid Waste Management Act (Act 97 of 1980) state implementation; the PA-3 illegal dumping geographic concentration (~10,000 sites cleared annually citywide; concentration in vacant-land-adjacent and industrial-corridor sub-areas); and the structural constraint that municipal sanitation operations capacity has been a recurring issue with the December 2025 restructure being the City's primary administrative response.

**Data provenance regime:** Internet-access regime. Track 1 architecture is directly documented from U.S. Code, PA statutes, and Philadelphia primary sources (Mayor Parker's December 19, 2025 State of the City announcement; OCGI architecture). Track 2 outcomes draw on Philadelphia Inquirer, Audacy, PHL.GOV, and WHYY coverage of the Sanitation/Streets restructure, OCGI program parameters, and 311 illegal dumping reporting data.

**Data provenance scope statement:** Philadelphia-specific and directly documented: Sanitation Department creation December 19, 2025 (Mayor Parker State of the City; Carlton Williams as OCGI Director; Sanitation Commissioner Mark Shipman per MC-10; Streets Department continued under Commissioner Del Rossi per MC-10); ~10,000 illegal dumping sites cleared annually citywide; PA-3 sub-area concentration in vacant-land-adjacent and industrial-corridor neighborhoods; Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site federal-EPA Region III jurisdiction with PA-3/PA-5 boundary adjacency. National benchmark applied by inference: RCRA Subtitle D non-hazardous waste compliance baseline. Structural inference: illegal dumping concentration tracking with vacant land concentration (D7 cross-reference) and industrial-corridor proximity. Held-open: Mayor Parker EO specific number for Sanitation/Streets split (UV-D13-02); precise OCGI annual budget and staffing not retrieved at Phase 2.

## Section 2: Authority Chain

**Constitutional Foundation.**

*U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cl. 3 — Commerce Clause; § 8, cl. 1 — Spending Clause.* Ground federal regulation of interstate waste shipment and federal assistance to state/local waste management programs.

*PA Const. art. I § 27.* Substantive environmental rights provision (cross-reference SD2 architecture).

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

*Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA), 42 U.S.C. §§ 6901 et seq.* Primary federal statute governing solid waste management. **Subtitle D** (42 U.S.C. §§ 6941-6949a) governs non-hazardous solid waste; establishes minimum federal standards for landfill operations and waste disposal but leaves implementation primarily to states. **Subtitle C** (42 U.S.C. §§ 6921-6939g) governs hazardous waste with cradle-to-grave tracking; EPA-administered with state primacy in most jurisdictions. **Subtitle I** (42 U.S.C. §§ 6991-6991m) governs underground storage tanks. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (state delegation; EPA enforcement discretion).*

*Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA), 42 U.S.C. §§ 9601 et seq.* The "Superfund" statute. Authorizes EPA cleanup of hazardous substance releases; creates liability for responsible parties (potentially responsible parties, PRPs). The Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site (added to NPL in 2001) covers the Clearview Landfill (Eastwick, southwest Philadelphia) and Folcroft Landfill (Folcroft, Delaware County) — straddling the PA-3 / PA-5 boundary with substantive environmental burden on adjacent Eastwick residents. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (Superfund pace; PRP litigation).*

*EPA Region III.* Federal regulatory authority for waste programs in PA, DE, MD, VA, WV, DC. EPA Region III oversees Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund cleanup and PA DEP Subtitle D primacy implementation. *EPA Region III RA name not surfaced; UV-D13-10.*

**State Statutory Layer.**

*Pennsylvania Solid Waste Management Act (Act 97 of 1980), 35 P.S. §§ 6018.101 et seq.* State statute implementing RCRA Subtitle D and going beyond federal floor in some respects. Authorizes PA DEP regulation of solid waste facilities, illegal dumping enforcement, and municipal waste planning. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (PA DEP resource constraints).*

*PA Hazardous Sites Cleanup Act (HSCA), 35 P.S. §§ 6020.101 et seq.* State analog to CERCLA; authorizes PA DEP cleanup of hazardous-substance-release sites; state-funded cleanup mechanism supplements federal Superfund.

*Pennsylvania-specific features.* PA Act 97 includes provisions on municipal waste planning (counties prepare municipal waste management plans), illegal dumping penalties, and source-separation/recycling mandates. PA's Subtitle D primacy implementation is administered by PA DEP's Bureau of Waste Management.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

*Mayor Cherelle L. Parker's December 19, 2025 State of the City announcement: Sanitation Department creation.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-10: Mayor Parker announced the creation of a separate Philadelphia Sanitation Department by carving out previous Streets Department responsibilities for residential refuse collection, recycling, and street cleaning operations. The Streets Department continues to oversee road maintenance, traffic engineering, and street infrastructure under Streets Commissioner Kristin Del Rossi. The Sanitation Commissioner is Mark Shipman (Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per Mayor Parker State of the City announcement). The specific Executive Order number underlying the split was referenced as "EO 11-24" in prior-method work but not confirmed at retrofit verification; carried in UV-D13-02. The restructure aims to elevate sanitation operations to dedicated departmental status and consolidate cross-departmental clean-and-green coordination.*

*Office of Clean and Green Initiatives (OCGI).* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-10: OCGI is a cross-departmental coordinator office created or elevated under Mayor Parker's clean-and-green initiative; **Director: Carlton Williams** (Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per Mayor Parker administration communications). Williams' role spans Sanitation operations coordination, illegal dumping abatement coordination, vacant lot remediation (cross-reference SD6 PHS Land Care contract architecture), graffiti abatement, and related clean-and-green operations. Precise OCGI annual budget and staffing not retrieved at Phase 2.*

*Philadelphia Code Title 10 (Regulation of Individual Conduct and Activity) and Title 21 (Public Places) — Sanitation and Trash provisions.* Municipal code provisions on residential refuse setout, commercial waste, illegal dumping penalties, recycling participation.

*Home Rule authority and limits.* Philadelphia's Home Rule Charter authorizes the Sanitation Department, OCGI, and related operations. Authority bounded by state regulatory frameworks (PA DEP Subtitle D oversight) and federal-floor compliance (RCRA Subtitle D standards). PA Act 97 municipal waste planning structure incorporates Philadelphia.

**Local Agency Layer.**

*Philadelphia Sanitation Department (post-December 2025).* Operational agency for residential refuse collection, recycling, street cleaning. Commissioner Shipman per MC-10.

*Philadelphia Streets Department (post-restructure).* Continued authority over road and street infrastructure operations; Commissioner Del Rossi per MC-10. (See SD3 cross-reference.)

*Office of Clean and Green Initiatives (OCGI).* Cross-departmental coordinator; Director Carlton Williams.

*PHS (Pennsylvania Horticultural Society) Land Care contract.* PHS administers the Vacant Land Stabilization (Clean and Green) program under contract with the City of Philadelphia, with substantive vacant-lot maintenance and aesthetic-amenity treatment functions. (Cross-reference SD6 architecture.)

**Constituent Layer.** The aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5.

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

**Triggering event 1 — Resident encountering illegal dumping on residential block.**

*Step 1.* Resident in a North/Northwest Core, West Core, or Southwest sub-area neighborhood observes accumulated illegal dumping on a vacant lot, alley, or right-of-way adjacent to residential properties. The dumping may include construction debris, household furniture, tires, hazardous materials. *Decision point:* reporting via 311 (Philadelphia's municipal service request system); awareness of OCGI or Sanitation Department response process.

*Step 2.* 311 report triggers Sanitation Department operational response (post-December 2025 restructure) and/or OCGI coordination for cross-departmental abatement (e.g., PHS Land Care vacant lot maintenance follow-up; Streets Department right-of-way cleanup; PWD storm drain coordination if applicable). Response time varies by sub-area, dumping type, and operational capacity. *Decision point:* abatement timeline; recurrence of dumping at same location.

*Step 3.* Federal RCRA Subtitle D standards govern landfill operations but do not impose specific illegal dumping abatement requirements at the municipal level. State PA Act 97 includes illegal dumping penalties (DEP enforcement; County District Attorney prosecution authority). Local code provisions establish penalty architecture for individual dumpers (rarely prosecuted given enforcement-resource constraints and difficulty of perpetrator identification). *Pathway breakdown:* federal floor does not address illegal dumping operationally; state-level penalties limited by enforcement capacity; local response operates within Sanitation Department / OCGI operational capacity (G13-SD4-02).

**Triggering event 2 — Eastwick resident adjacent to Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site.**

*Step 1.* Resident in Eastwick neighborhood adjacent to or near the Clearview Landfill portion of the Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site (NPL site since 2001); experiences environmental burden of legacy waste site (groundwater contamination concerns; air quality concerns; site remediation activity over time). *Decision point:* awareness of Superfund status; understanding of cleanup pace and milestones.

*Step 2.* Federal CERCLA cleanup operates on Record-of-Decision (ROD) basis; EPA Region III is the lead agency; PRP litigation has shaped pace. The cumulative experience for adjacent residents includes environmental-justice attributes that operated within the Justice40 framework during 2021-2024 — now retracted per MC-03 with administrative implementation thinned. *Decision point:* community engagement participation; awareness of cleanup milestones.

*Step 3.* The Eastwick / Cobbs Creek flood-vulnerability (cross-reference SD2 G13-SD2-05) compounds Superfund-adjacency burden — the same geography that faces recurring flood risk also faces legacy-waste burden. Federal program architecture treats these as separate authorities (CERCLA vs. CWA vs. NFIP/FEMA), but the constituent experience is cumulative. *Pathway breakdown:* federal floor cleanup architecture is real but pace-constrained; cumulative-burden recognition has retracted at Justice40-revocation level (G13-SD4-05).

**Triggering event 3 — Commercial business or contractor encountering waste regulation.**

*Step 1.* Business operator (small or large) or construction contractor generates solid waste (commercial); subject to PA Act 97 waste-management requirements; subject to Philadelphia Code commercial waste provisions; may be subject to RCRA Subtitle C if hazardous waste generation crosses threshold. *Decision point:* awareness of regulatory requirements; capacity for compliance.

*Step 2.* PA DEP and EPA Region III enforcement on waste generation; compliance documentation; permitted-disposal-facility use. Illegal dumping by commercial actors is a significant share of the citywide dumping problem; enforcement against commercial dumpers depends on identification of source. *Decision point:* compliance posture; enforcement vulnerability.

*Step 3.* The architecture's enforcement capacity constrains both legitimate compliance support and illegal-actor enforcement; the structural finding is that compliance architecture exists but enforcement capacity has historically been constrained relative to scale of waste generation and dumping activity. *Pathway breakdown:* federal-state-local enforcement architecture is real but operationally constrained.

**Pathway breakdown patterns in practice.** Documented breakdown points concentrate at illegal dumping abatement pace (Triggering event 1) where municipal operational capacity has been a recurring issue prompting the December 2025 restructure; at Eastwick / Lower Darby Creek Superfund cumulative-burden recognition (Triggering event 2) where the Justice40 retraction (MC-03) has thinned the administrative-implementation layer; and at commercial-actor enforcement (Triggering event 3) where federal-state-local enforcement capacity has not matched scale.

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

*Data provenance note:* Citywide illegal dumping site clearance figure (~10,000 sites annually) is from City of Philadelphia and Streets/Sanitation operational reporting. Sub-area concentration is structurally inferred from documented illegal dumping concentration tracking with vacant land concentration (D7 cross-reference) and industrial-corridor proximity. Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site status is from EPA Region III primary sources. PHS Land Care vacant lot architecture is from PHS contract and reporting.

**PA-3 statistical profile.**

- Citywide illegal dumping sites cleared annually: approximately 10,000 (Sanitation/Streets operational reporting).
- Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site: NPL since 2001; Clearview Landfill (Eastwick, southwest Philadelphia) and Folcroft Landfill (Folcroft, Delaware County); cleanup ongoing under EPA Region III; PA-3 / PA-5 boundary geography.
- Vacant land in Philadelphia: extensive; PHS Land Care contract administers vacant land stabilization across thousands of city-owned and privately-owned parcels (cross-reference SD6).
- December 19, 2025 restructure: Sanitation Department created from Streets Department residential refuse, recycling, and street cleaning operations; OCGI elevated; Streets continues road/traffic-infrastructure functions.
- OCGI annual budget and staffing: not retrieved at Phase 2.

**Geographic variation across four sub-areas.**

- *North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.* High illegal dumping concentration tracking with high vacant land concentration; industrial corridor adjacency in some neighborhoods (Hunting Park, Kensington); recurring 311 reports; PHS Land Care intensive engagement.

- *West Philadelphia Core.* Moderate illegal dumping concentration; vacant land in pockets; Cobbs Creek-adjacent areas have separate environmental issues (cross-reference SD2 G13-SD2-05).

- *Northwest Philadelphia.* Lower illegal dumping concentration relative to Cores; some Wissahickon-adjacent areas have specific conservation-coordination architecture (cross-reference SD6 / D6 SD6).

- *South/Southwest Philadelphia.* Variable — denser South Philadelphia residential blocks have lower vacant-lot share; Southwest / Eastwick has Lower Darby Creek Superfund adjacency and industrial-corridor proximity; some southeast portions have higher dumping concentration.

The geographic variation pattern is characterized by illegal dumping concentration tracking with vacant land concentration (D7 cross-reference) and industrial-corridor proximity, producing systematically uneven distribution across PA-3 sub-areas with concentration in North/Northwest Core and parts of Southwest.

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

**Profile 1: Resident on a block with recurring illegal dumping — Hunting Park.**

*Constituent type:* Resident, age 52, household earning approximately $40,000, residing in Hunting Park on a block with a vacant lot subject to recurring illegal dumping by outside actors.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 1 — observes accumulated dumping; reports via 311; awaits abatement response.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* 311 report → Sanitation Department dispatch (post-December 2025) → cleanup → recurrence at same location given vacant-lot status. PHS Land Care contract may operate at this lot for vacant-land stabilization treatment if the lot is on the Land Care list.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* Per Pew analysis and city operational reporting, 311 dumping reports across Philadelphia approach tens of thousands annually with seasonal variation; resolution times vary substantially. Verify current 311 dumping resolution time at phila.gov/311 before public use — F13-SD4-01.

*Outcome:* The resident experiences the federal-state-local architecture as the operational pace of Sanitation Department response to recurring dumping; the December 2025 restructure aims to elevate this operational capacity but the trajectory is forthcoming. Structural finding (G13-SD4-02 operational-capacity gap) holds at any plausible specific 311 resolution time.

**Profile 2: Eastwick resident adjacent to Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site.**

*Constituent type:* Resident, age 62, household earning approximately $48,000, residing in Eastwick within proximity of the Clearview Landfill portion of Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* South/Southwest Philadelphia (Eastwick).

*Triggering event:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 2 — adjacent to Superfund site; experiences environmental burden of legacy waste site; participates in community engagement on cleanup milestones; also faces Cobbs Creek / Schuylkill flood vulnerability (cross-reference SD2 G13-SD2-05).

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Federal CERCLA cleanup operates under EPA Region III lead agency; PRP litigation has shaped pace; cumulative environmental-justice attributes operated within Justice40 framework during 2021-2024 (now retracted per MC-03).

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site cleanup status and milestones are documented at EPA Region III. Verify current Superfund site status at epa.gov/superfund before public use — F13-SD4-02.

*Outcome:* The Eastwick resident experiences cumulative burden across SD2 (flood vulnerability), SD4 (Superfund adjacency), and federal-program architecture that treats these as separate authorities. The structural representation question is whether federal House representation engages this cumulative-burden geography at the multi-program level.

**Profile 3: Small business operator generating commercial waste.**

*Constituent type:* Owner of small commercial business in West Philadelphia generating routine commercial solid waste; contractor relationship with private waste hauler; periodic encounter with regulatory requirements.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* West Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 3 — generates commercial waste; subject to permitted-disposal-facility-use requirements under PA Act 97 and Philadelphia Code.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* PA DEP enforcement architecture; compliance documentation; permitted disposal facility use; potential RCRA Subtitle C interaction if any hazardous-waste threshold is approached.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* Commercial waste hauler rates and PA Act 97 compliance costs vary substantially. Verify current PA waste-disposal regulatory requirements at dep.pa.gov/wastemanagement before public use — F13-SD4-03.

*Outcome:* The small business operator experiences the federal-state-local enforcement architecture as compliance documentation and permitted-disposal-facility cost. Federal-floor enforcement capacity at PA DEP and EPA Region III has been constrained relative to scale of commercial-waste generation.

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The most consequential thing to understand about Philadelphia solid waste and sanitation from a PA-3 representation perspective is that the December 2025 Sanitation/Streets restructure is the city's primary administrative response to a long-standing operational-capacity issue — and that the success of the restructure is the open question that the 2026-2027 period will surface.

For decades, residential refuse collection, street cleaning, illegal dumping abatement, and recycling operations were within the Streets Department alongside the road network, traffic engineering, and street infrastructure functions. Operational capacity for the sanitation side was a recurring issue — refuse collection delays, particularly during summer heat waves; illegal dumping abatement pace; street cleaning frequency. The December 19, 2025 announcement by Mayor Parker created a separate Sanitation Department (with Commissioner Mark Shipman), continued the Streets Department under Commissioner Kristin Del Rossi for road and traffic-infrastructure functions, and elevated the Office of Clean and Green Initiatives (with Director Carlton Williams) as a cross-departmental coordinator. The specific EO number underlying the split was referenced as "EO 11-24" in prior-method work but not confirmed at retrofit verification (UV-D13-02).

The federal floor under RCRA Subtitle D governs landfill operations and waste disposal but leaves implementation primarily to states — and PA Act 97 of 1980 implements RCRA Subtitle D through PA DEP's Bureau of Waste Management. RCRA Subtitle D does not address illegal dumping operationally; that is municipal operations terrain. Federal CERCLA covers Superfund cleanup, and the Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site (Clearview Landfill in Eastwick) is the principal PA-3 CERCLA site — but the cleanup operates on EPA Region III pace through the Record-of-Decision process. The Eastwick / Cobbs Creek geography intersects SD2 flood vulnerability and SD4 Superfund adjacency in a way that federal program architecture treats as separate authorities, while the constituent experience is cumulative (G13-SD4-05).

The illegal dumping problem in Philadelphia has had multiple dimensions: outside actors dumping on vacant lots in lower-income neighborhoods; commercial actors illegally dumping construction debris; recurring dumping at locations that lack regular abatement; the relationship between vacant land concentration (D7 cross-reference) and dumping concentration. The City's 10,000-sites-cleared-annually scale is real and the Sanitation Department restructure is the administrative response, but the structural finding is that municipal-operations capacity has not matched scale at the level needed for sustained reduction.

For the Hunting Park resident on a block with recurring dumping, the Eastwick resident adjacent to the Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site, and the West Philadelphia small business operator generating commercial waste who appear in this sub-domain's aggregate profiles, the federal-state-local architecture's operational expression is the abatement pace, the cleanup milestones, and the compliance architecture they encounter. The structural representation question for SD4 is whether federal House representation engages this layer (RCRA Subtitle D reauthorization advocacy, CERCLA Lower Darby Creek cleanup pace engagement, federal-program coordination on cumulative-burden geographies) — recognizing that the principal operational capacity sits at the state and municipal levels.

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G13-SD4-01: PA DEP Subtitle D enforcement capacity constraint.**
- *Evidence type:* [SD] Structurally derived (enforcement-capacity gap)
- *Confidence:* MEDIUM-HIGH on the structural gap; LOW on precise PA-3-specific enforcement metrics
- *Gap statement:* PA DEP Bureau of Waste Management has primary state-level Subtitle D enforcement; agency resource constraints have historically limited enforcement capacity relative to scale of waste generation and dumping activity statewide. PA-3-specific enforcement metrics not retrieved at Phase 2.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 State Statutory Layer (PA Act 97); Section 3 Triggering event 3.
- *Representation implication:* Federal RCRA reauthorization advocacy with EPA enforcement-support; state-level coordination on PA DEP capacity.

**G13-SD4-02: Municipal operational-capacity gap and December 2025 restructure trajectory.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (restructure announcement); [SD] Structurally derived (capacity gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the restructure; HIGH on the historical capacity gap; MEDIUM on the restructure's trajectory
- *Gap statement:* Mayor Parker's December 19, 2025 Sanitation Department creation is the city's primary administrative response to long-standing operational-capacity issues in residential refuse collection, street cleaning, and illegal dumping abatement. The trajectory of the restructure (whether the dedicated department produces sustained improvement) is the open question for 2026-2027 surveillance.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-10 (Sanitation Department creation); Section 2 Local Statutory Layer (restructure architecture); Section 3 Triggering event 1.
- *Representation implication:* Federal-program coordination with municipal Sanitation; FY27 federal-aid eligibility for sanitation infrastructure where applicable.

**G13-SD4-03: Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund cleanup pace.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (Superfund status); [SD] Structurally derived (pace constraint)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the site status; MEDIUM on current cleanup pace
- *Gap statement:* Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund site (NPL since 2001; Clearview Landfill Eastwick + Folcroft Landfill Delaware County) operates under EPA Region III lead agency through CERCLA Record-of-Decision process; PRP litigation has shaped pace; cleanup is incremental over decades. Current cleanup pace and milestone timeline at site-specific level not retrieved at Phase 2.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (CERCLA); Section 3 Triggering event 2; Section 5 Profile 2.
- *Representation implication:* CERCLA reauthorization advocacy; EPA Region III coordination on PA-3 / PA-5 Superfund pace.

**G13-SD4-04: Vacant land concentration and illegal dumping geographic concentration.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (vacant land architecture); [SD] Structurally derived (geographic concentration)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the structural relationship; MEDIUM on precise quantification at sub-area level
- *Gap statement:* Illegal dumping concentration tracks with vacant land concentration (D7 cross-reference) and industrial-corridor proximity. PA-3 sub-area distribution: North/Northwest Core and parts of Southwest have higher dumping concentration than Northwest or denser South Philadelphia residential blocks. PHS Land Care contract (cross-reference SD6) provides vacant-land stabilization but does not eliminate the structural relationship.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 4 Statistical Profile (sub-area variation); Section 2 Local Agency Layer (PHS Land Care contract).
- *Representation implication:* Federal-program engagement on vacant land remediation (Reconnecting Communities cross-reference SD3; HUD CDBG vacant land programs).

**G13-SD4-05: Cumulative-burden geography (SD2 + SD4) federal-program treatment.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (cumulative geography); [SD] Structurally derived (federal-program treatment)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the cumulative-geography pattern; HIGH on the federal-program treatment as separate authorities
- *Gap statement:* Eastwick / Cobbs Creek geography intersects SD2 flood vulnerability (G13-SD2-05), SD4 Lower Darby Creek Superfund adjacency, and absent comprehensive federal cumulative-burden recognition (post-Justice40 retraction per MC-03; cross-reference G13-SD2-07 and G13-SD3-06). Federal program architecture treats these as separate authorities (CWA / NFIP/FEMA / CERCLA); constituent experience is cumulative.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-03 (Justice40 broader revocation); Section 3 Triggering event 2; G13-SD2-05 (cross-reference); D6 MC-04/MC-32 (principal-anchor EJ cross-reference).
- *Representation implication:* Federal statutory EJ codification; cumulative-burden federal-program coordination; cross-domain congressional delegation engagement.

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

PA-3 solid waste and sanitation infrastructure operates within a federal-state-local architecture in which (1) **what the legal framework provides or promises:** federal RCRA Subtitle D (non-hazardous waste) and Subtitle C (hazardous waste) standards with state primacy implementation; federal CERCLA for Superfund cleanup with EPA Region III lead; Pennsylvania Solid Waste Management Act (Act 97 of 1980) state implementation; Philadelphia Code municipal sanitation provisions; Philadelphia Sanitation Department (post-December 2025); Office of Clean and Green Initiatives cross-departmental coordination; PHS Land Care contract for vacant land stabilization. (2) **What PA-3 constituents actually receive:** ~10,000 illegal dumping sites cleared annually citywide at uneven sub-area pace; Lower Darby Creek Area Superfund cleanup proceeding under EPA Region III with PRP-litigation-shaped pace; municipal operational capacity historically below scale needed; December 2025 restructure trajectory pending. (3) **What accounts for the gap:** federal floor (RCRA Subtitle D) does not address illegal dumping operationally; state-level enforcement (PA DEP) constrained by resources; municipal operational capacity has been the principal target of the December 2025 restructure; cumulative-burden geography (Eastwick / Cobbs Creek SD2+SD4 intersection) sits across federal-program authority lines that the post-Justice40 retraction (MC-03) has not bridged. (4) **What the gap means for the representation analysis:** PA-3 sub-areas with concentrated vacant land and industrial-corridor proximity experience the operational capacity gap most acutely; Eastwick residents experience cumulative SD2+SD4 burden directly. The structural representation question is whether federal House representation engages the federal-program layer (RCRA reauthorization, CERCLA Lower Darby Creek pace, federal EJ statutory codification post-Justice40 revocation) while recognizing the principal operational capacity sits at state and municipal levels.
