# D13 Physical Infrastructure — Sub-Domain 5: School Buildings and Public Facility Capital

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain number:** SD5
**Sub-domain name:** School Buildings and Public Facility Capital
**Scope statement:** The federal/state/local mandate-stack architecture for public school building condition and capital investment serving PA-3, with primary institutional embodiment in the School District of Philadelphia (SDP) as the locally-administered school district within Pennsylvania's Public School Code framework; the federal-floor protections through the **Asbestos Hazard Emergency Response Act (AHERA, 15 U.S.C. §§ 2641-2656)** including the June 26, 2025 Deferred Prosecution Agreement (DPA) entered by SDP, the U.S. Attorney for the Eastern District of Pennsylvania, and DOJ (cross-reference D6 verified file MC-03 / G6-SD4-02 — D6 is the cross-domain principal anchor for AHERA criminal-enforcement architecture; D13 defers and does not duplicate); the Pennsylvania **William Penn School District v. Pennsylvania Department of Education** education funding adequacy litigation with PA budget November 12, 2025 $565M legislative response (per MC-05) against estimated ~$3.8B remaining adequacy gap; the SDP "Accelerating Opportunity" facilities and program plan with April 30, 2026 board vote pending (UV-D13-04); the SDP capital architecture including ~85 buildings rated poor/unsatisfactory; Penn's $100M pledge supporting SDP environmental management (cross-reference D9 SD4 PILOET architecture and D6 SD4 anchor-environmental-compliance dimension); and the structural constraint that federal AHERA criminal-floor obligation operates alongside state-constitutional-adequacy fiscal constraint, with Both/And preserved.

**Data provenance regime:** Internet-access regime. Track 1 architecture is directly documented from U.S. Code, PA Public School Code, William Penn litigation history, and SDP primary sources. Track 2 outcomes draw on Chalkbeat, NBC10, Philadelphia Inquirer, Axios, Billy Penn, Grid Magazine, and DOJ filings for the AHERA DPA. Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) MC-03 / G6-SD4-02 for AHERA federal-architecture principal anchor.

**Data provenance scope statement:** Philadelphia-specific and directly documented: SDP serving approximately 117,000 students across ~217 schools; ~85 buildings rated poor/unsatisfactory; AHERA findings in 31 schools (cross-reference D6 G6-SD4-02 for D6-owned principal-anchor content); SDP DPA June 26, 2025 — federal court approval specific date held in UV-D13-01; SDP "Accelerating Opportunity" plan parameters from Chalkbeat / NBC10 / Inquirer / Axios / Billy Penn cross-confirmation; April 30, 2026 SDP Board vote on plan pending (UV-D13-04); William Penn legislative response $565M in November 12, 2025 PA budget (per MC-05) against estimated ~$3.8B remaining adequacy gap; **PSFIG (Public School Facility Improvement Grant Program)** application window January 5 – March 13, 2026 with grants of $500K-$5M and 25% local match (per MC-05 architectural context); Penn $100M pledge architecture (cross-reference D9 SD4 / D6 SD4). National benchmark applied by inference: AHERA inspection / management plan / O&M baseline applied to SDP operational architecture. Structural inference: ~85 buildings poor/unsatisfactory tracking with sub-area distribution; the AHERA criminal-floor obligation vs. William Penn fiscal-constraint Both/And preserved at finding level.

## Section 2: Authority Chain

**Constitutional Foundation.**

*U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cl. 3 — Commerce Clause; § 8, cl. 1 — Spending Clause.* Ground federal environmental regulation of school buildings and federal education funding.

*U.S. Const. amend. XIV § 1 — Equal Protection Clause.* Grounds civil rights protections in school facilities (Title VI, ADA, IDEA).

*PA Const. art. III § 14 — "Public School System" clause.* Provides: "The General Assembly shall provide for the maintenance and support of a thorough and efficient system of public education to serve the needs of the Commonwealth." Operative substantive constitutional provision underlying William Penn School District v. Commonwealth education funding adequacy litigation. *Statutory stability: HIGH (constitutional). Administrative vulnerability: LOW for the clause itself; HIGH for legislative compliance pace.*

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

*Asbestos Hazard Emergency Response Act (AHERA), 15 U.S.C. §§ 2641-2656.* Statute requiring local education agencies (LEAs) to inspect school buildings for asbestos-containing materials, develop and implement asbestos management plans, conduct re-inspections every three years, and provide operations and maintenance (O&M) for asbestos-containing materials. Criminal enforcement authority: EPA Criminal Investigations Division (EPA-CID) partners with U.S. Attorneys' Offices to bring criminal AHERA enforcement actions. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (EPA-CID enforcement discretion).* **Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) MC-03 / G6-SD4-02 — D6 is the cross-domain principal anchor for AHERA criminal-enforcement architecture; D13 SD5 references the federal floor but defers principal-anchor analysis to D6 SD4.**

*SDP AHERA Deferred Prosecution Agreement (DPA) entered June 26, 2025.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-07: The DPA was entered by the School District of Philadelphia, the U.S. Attorney for the Eastern District of Pennsylvania, and the U.S. Department of Justice on June 26, 2025; specific federal court approval date held in UV-D13-01. The DPA documented AHERA compliance failures in 31 SDP schools and established judicially-overseen remediation obligations. **U.S. Attorney: David Metcalf** (Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per DOJ EDPA press communications). SDP Superintendent Tony B. Watlington Sr. and SDP Board President Reginald Streater are the named SDP institutional signatories (Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per DOJ DPA filings and SDP press releases). The DPA's criminal-floor architecture is principal-anchor in D6 G6-SD4-02 — D13 SD5 defers and does not duplicate the D6 analysis.*

*Federal education funding programs.* Title I (Education for Disadvantaged Children); IDEA (Individuals with Disabilities Education Act); School Construction (limited federal authority); E-rate (telecommunications subsidy). Federal funding flow: U.S. Department of Education → PA Department of Education → SDP. PA receives federal education funds proportional to its student counts.

**State Statutory Layer.**

*Pennsylvania Public School Code of 1949, 24 P.S. §§ 1-101 et seq.* Primary state statute governing public education in Pennsylvania. Establishes school district architecture, funding mechanisms, curriculum mandates, and facilities requirements. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (legislative budget compliance pace).*

*William Penn School District v. Commonwealth, 170 A.3d 414 (Pa. 2017); 282 A.3d 1247 (Pa. 2022); 297 A.3d 1163 (Pa. Commw. Ct. 2023).* Pennsylvania Supreme Court 2017 decision establishing justiciability of the "thorough and efficient" clause; 2023 Commonwealth Court decision finding Pennsylvania's school funding system unconstitutional and ordering legislative remediation; subsequent legislative budget processes have established multi-year funding response. *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-05: PA budget signed November 12, 2025 (Day 135 of impasse); total $50.1B; the William Penn legislative response component is $565M. Estimated remaining adequacy gap ~$3.8B against the documented adequacy target.*

*Public School Facility Improvement Grant Program (PSFIG).* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per MC-05 architectural context: PSFIG was created under Act 34 of 2023 and amended by Act 54 of 2024 and Act 45 of 2025. Operational with January 5 – March 13, 2026 application window; grants of $500K-$5M with 25% local match required. Administered by PA Department of Education. The program is the principal new state-level mechanism for school facility capital support post-William Penn legislative response.*

*Pennsylvania Department of Education (PDE).* Administers state-level education funding, school district oversight, federal funding pass-through, and PSFIG. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM.*

*Pennsylvania-specific features.* William Penn establishes substantive constitutional grounding for education funding adequacy; PSFIG provides facility-capital mechanism; SDP's classification under PA Public School Code includes specific features (governance structure under elected board post-2018 return-to-local-control) that differentiate Philadelphia from many other PA districts.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

*School District of Philadelphia (SDP).* Locally-administered school district returned to local control in 2018 (post-1998-2018 School Reform Commission state control period). Governing body: Philadelphia Board of Education (nine members appointed by Mayor; subject to City Council confirmation). **SDP Superintendent: Dr. Tony B. Watlington Sr.** (Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per SDP communications). **Board President: Reginald Streater** (Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per Board press releases).

*SDP "Accelerating Opportunity" plan.* *Verification update (2026-05-11) per Section 1 scope statement: Plan announced by Superintendent Watlington; addresses facility consolidations, program offerings, school closures and openings, and capital prioritization. Initial plan parameters under public discussion (Chalkbeat / NBC10 / Inquirer / Axios / Billy Penn cross-confirmed coverage); April 30, 2026 SDP Board vote on plan pending (UV-D13-04). The plan's facilities consolidation portion intersects with the AHERA DPA judicially-overseen remediation obligations and with the William Penn-state-adequacy fiscal context.*

*Philadelphia Code of General Ordinances Title 11 (Education).* Municipal education-related provisions; primary school-funding mechanism in Philadelphia is property tax with City portion supporting SDP through City-District funding agreements.

*Home Rule authority and limits.* Philadelphia's Home Rule Charter governs the relationship between the city government and SDP; SDP is a separate political subdivision under PA Public School Code; City Council confirmation of Board appointees is a structural feature.

**Local Agency Layer.**

*School District of Philadelphia, including SDP Facilities Department.* Operational agency for SDP capital projects, building maintenance, AHERA compliance operations (under DPA judicial oversight), and "Accelerating Opportunity" implementation.

*Philadelphia Board of Education.* Governing body.

*Mayor's Office of Education.* City-level education coordination.

*Penn $100M pledge architecture.* *Verification context per Section 1: University of Pennsylvania pledged $100M to support SDP environmental management; the pledge intersects with D9 SD4 PILOET (Payments In Lieu Of Taxes) architecture (where Penn's tax-exempt status produces fiscal-dimension anchor-accountability questions) and with D6 SD4 (where Penn's environmental-compliance architecture for its own facilities and its contributions to SDP environmental management produce the environmental-compliance-dimension anchor-accountability finding). At D13 SD5, the Penn pledge is referenced as a capital-program input without re-extending the five-dimensional framework.* Cross-reference D9 verified file (2026-05-11) and D6 verified file (2026-05-11) Synthesis Section 2.

**Constituent Layer.** The aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5.

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

**Triggering event 1 — Student or family encountering AHERA-noncompliant school building.**

*Step 1.* Student enrolled at one of the 31 SDP schools subject to the DPA's judicially-overseen AHERA remediation obligations; family awareness of asbestos-containing-material status varies. Some buildings have been the subject of temporary closures, emergency abatement, or air-quality monitoring. *Decision point:* awareness of building status; school enrollment options.

*Step 2.* SDP operates under DPA judicial oversight for AHERA compliance at the 31 schools; remediation obligations include inspection, management plan implementation, O&M procedures, and re-inspection cycle compliance. The June 26, 2025 DPA established the architecture; principal-anchor analysis in D6 G6-SD4-02. *Decision point:* remediation progress at student's specific school; family communication from SDP and SDP Facilities Department.

*Step 3.* Federal AHERA criminal-floor obligation is the structural protection; the DPA is the enforcement instrument; the Penn $100M pledge supports SDP environmental management. Concurrently, SDP's fiscal capacity for AHERA compliance is constrained by the William Penn-state-adequacy fiscal context — federal criminal floor and state-constitutional adequacy operate as Both/And rather than competing obligations (G13-SD5-01 and G13-SD5-02 preserve both). *Pathway breakdown:* federal floor exists and is enforceable; state-fiscal context constrains capacity to fully discharge the obligation; the two layers operate together.

**Triggering event 2 — Family encountering a building rated poor/unsatisfactory by SDP.**

*Step 1.* Family with student enrolled at one of ~85 SDP buildings rated poor/unsatisfactory; capital backlog has been a longstanding feature of SDP architecture. *Decision point:* school enrollment options; advocacy through Board meeting public comment or parent organization.

*Step 2.* SDP "Accelerating Opportunity" plan (April 30, 2026 Board vote pending per UV-D13-04) addresses facility consolidations, program offerings, school closures and openings, and capital prioritization. The plan's specific treatment of poor/unsatisfactory buildings (potential consolidation, capital investment, or closure) is the operational question. *Decision point:* plan content; Board vote outcome; specific building treatment in the plan.

*Step 3.* PA-state PSFIG ($500K-$5M grants; 25% local match; January 5 – March 13, 2026 application window) is the principal new state-level capital mechanism; PA budget November 2025 $565M William Penn response is the principal new state-level operating-and-adequacy response. SDP's fiscal capacity to apply for PSFIG (25% local match) varies by capital project. *Pathway breakdown:* state-level capital architecture has expanded (PSFIG) and operating-and-adequacy architecture has partially advanced ($565M against ~$3.8B estimated gap); SDP's capacity to translate into specific building improvements at the poor/unsatisfactory-rated level is the operational gap (G13-SD5-04).

**Triggering event 3 — Family encountering SDP "Accelerating Opportunity" plan implementation.**

*Step 1.* Family at SDP school subject to "Accelerating Opportunity" plan treatment (potential consolidation, school closure, program change, or capital investment); plan parameters under public discussion through 2026; April 30 Board vote pending.

*Step 2.* The plan's articulation includes the school-by-school dispositions, capital prioritization sequence, and timeline. The plan's interaction with AHERA DPA obligations and William Penn fiscal context is structurally significant.

*Step 3.* Federal-state-local architecture at constituent level: federal AHERA enforcement floor (DPA), state-fiscal-context (William Penn $565M response + PSFIG), local SDP implementation (Accelerating Opportunity plan). *Pathway breakdown:* the three layers operate together; family experience of plan implementation depends on specific school disposition; structural representation question is how federal House representation engages this multi-layer architecture.

**Pathway breakdown patterns in practice.** Documented breakdown points concentrate at AHERA-vs-William Penn Both/And (Triggering event 1 Step 3) — preserved as competing-truths simultaneity rather than collapsed into a single framing; at poor/unsatisfactory-building capital-pace (Triggering event 2 Step 3) where state-level architecture has expanded but local fiscal-capacity-to-match remains constrained; at "Accelerating Opportunity" plan operational treatment (Triggering event 3) where the April 30 vote outcome is the proximate inflection point (UV-D13-04).

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

*Data provenance note:* SDP statistical profile is Philadelphia-specific and directly documented from SDP primary sources. AHERA architecture and 31-school finding are documented in DPA filings (cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) G6-SD4-02 for principal-anchor analysis). William Penn $565M legislative response and PSFIG architecture are from PA budget documentation (per MC-05) and PDE materials. Sub-area distribution of poor/unsatisfactory buildings is structurally inferred from documented citywide building-condition distribution applied to PA-3 housing-stock-age pattern.

**PA-3 statistical profile.**

- SDP enrollment: approximately 117,000 students.
- SDP schools: approximately 217 (operating and capital architecture).
- SDP buildings rated poor/unsatisfactory: approximately 85.
- AHERA findings: 31 schools subject to DPA judicially-overseen remediation obligations (cross-reference D6 G6-SD4-02 for principal-anchor analysis).
- PA budget November 12, 2025 William Penn response: $565M (per MC-05); estimated remaining adequacy gap ~$3.8B.
- PSFIG: $500K-$5M grants; 25% local match; January 5 – March 13, 2026 application window.
- Penn $100M pledge supporting SDP environmental management (cross-reference D9 SD4 / D6 SD4).
- SDP "Accelerating Opportunity" plan: April 30, 2026 Board vote pending (UV-D13-04).

**Geographic variation across four sub-areas.**

- *North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.* Concentrated SDP school presence; many older school buildings; AHERA-affected schools include several in this sub-area; poor/unsatisfactory ratings concentrated tracking with building age. "Accelerating Opportunity" plan disposition for this sub-area's schools has been particular focus of public comment.

- *West Philadelphia Core.* SDP presence; older building stock; Penn-adjacent geography intersects Penn $100M pledge architecture. Cross-reference D6 SD4 anchor-environmental-compliance architecture for Penn's own facilities.

- *Northwest Philadelphia.* SDP presence; newer building stock in some areas relative to Cores; less acute concentration of poor/unsatisfactory ratings; some "Accelerating Opportunity" implications.

- *South/Southwest Philadelphia.* SDP presence; variable building age; particular focus areas for SDP capital prioritization including Furness High School and others. Southwest schools serving Eastwick neighborhoods.

The geographic variation pattern is characterized by AHERA-affected and poor/unsatisfactory-rated buildings tracking with building age — concentrated in North/Northwest and West Cores where SDP school building stock is oldest — producing systematic sub-area-distributed exposure to building-condition issues among PA-3 SDP students.

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

**Profile 1: SDP family at an AHERA-affected school — North Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Family with two children enrolled at SDP school subject to DPA judicially-overseen remediation; household income approximately $42,000; residing in North Philadelphia neighborhood with neighborhood school assignment.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 1 — children enrolled at school with AHERA remediation obligations; family awareness of building status varies; SDP and SDP Facilities Department communicate remediation progress.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* DPA judicial oversight; Penn $100M pledge supporting environmental management; SDP Facilities Department operational implementation; cross-reference D6 G6-SD4-02 for principal-anchor analysis.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* AHERA-affected school list and DPA remediation milestones documented at DOJ EDPA filings and SDP press releases. Verify current SDP AHERA remediation status at philasd.org and justice.gov/usao-edpa before public use — F13-SD5-01.

*Outcome:* The family experiences the federal AHERA criminal-floor protection through the DPA-overseen remediation timeline; concurrently experiences the William Penn-state-adequacy fiscal context's effect on SDP's broader capacity to invest in environmental management beyond DPA-specific scope. Federal floor and state-fiscal context operate as Both/And (G13-SD5-01 and G13-SD5-02 preserved).

**Profile 2: SDP family at a poor/unsatisfactory-rated school — West Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Family with one child enrolled at SDP school rated poor/unsatisfactory; household income approximately $38,000; residing in West Philadelphia.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* West Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 2 — building condition impacts learning environment; family considers school options; engages "Accelerating Opportunity" public comment process.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* SDP capital prioritization; PSFIG application potential (25% local match constraint); William Penn-response operating funding; potential "Accelerating Opportunity" plan disposition (capital investment, consolidation, or closure).

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* PSFIG grants $500K-$5M with 25% local match; for an SDP school with $5M capital need, the local match would be ~$1.25M. SDP fiscal capacity for local match varies by year. Verify current PSFIG application criteria at education.pa.gov before public use — F13-SD5-02.

*Outcome:* The family experiences the state-level capital architecture (PSFIG) through its applicability to specific school capital projects; the local match requirement constrains SDP's capacity to apply at scale; the William Penn legislative response addresses operating-and-adequacy but does not directly resolve specific building capital backlog.

**Profile 3: SDP family encountering "Accelerating Opportunity" plan — South Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Family with three children at SDP schools potentially affected by plan; household income approximately $45,000; residing in South Philadelphia.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* South/Southwest Philadelphia.

*Triggering event:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 3 — "Accelerating Opportunity" plan implementation; potential consolidation, school closure, program change, or capital investment for specific schools; April 30 Board vote pending.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Public comment process; Board meeting engagement; plan articulation; vote outcome; implementation.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* "Accelerating Opportunity" plan specific dispositions are documented in plan release materials. Verify current plan content at philasd.org before public use — F13-SD5-03.

*Outcome:* The family's experience of the federal-state-local architecture is the plan's school-specific treatment; structural representation question is whether federal House representation engages the plan-implementation cycle through Title I, IDEA, and AHERA federal-program coordination, or whether engagement remains at higher federal-program-design level.

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The most consequential thing to understand about Philadelphia school buildings from a PA-3 representation perspective is the architectural simultaneity of the federal AHERA criminal-floor obligation and the state William Penn fiscal-constraint, both of which operate at the SDP institutional level, and neither of which collapses into the other.

The AHERA DPA entered June 26, 2025 by SDP, the U.S. Attorney for the Eastern District of Pennsylvania, and DOJ is the federal criminal-floor architecture's expression. The DPA documented AHERA compliance failures in 31 SDP schools, established judicially-overseen remediation obligations, and named SDP institutional signatories (Superintendent Watlington; Board President Streater). Federal criminal-enforcement architecture — EPA-CID partnering with U.S. Attorneys' Offices, in this case U.S. Attorney David Metcalf — is the principal-anchor analysis at D6 G6-SD4-02 in the D6 verified file (2026-05-11). D13 SD5 references the federal floor as established context but defers principal-anchor analysis to D6.

The William Penn School District v. Commonwealth education funding adequacy litigation is the state constitutional-floor architecture's expression. The 2023 Commonwealth Court decision found Pennsylvania's school funding system unconstitutional and ordered legislative remediation. The PA budget November 12, 2025 (signed at Day 135 of the impasse) included $565M as the William Penn legislative response component against an estimated ~$3.8B remaining adequacy gap. PSFIG (Public School Facility Improvement Grant Program) under Act 34 of 2023 amended by Act 54 of 2024 and Act 45 of 2025 is the principal new state-level capital mechanism; $500K-$5M grants, 25% local match, January 5 – March 13, 2026 application window. The state-fiscal context constrains SDP's broader capacity to invest in environmental management and building condition beyond what the AHERA DPA specifically requires.

These two layers operate as Both/And rather than as competing framings. The AHERA criminal-floor obligation is real and judicially enforced; the William Penn state-fiscal context is real and structurally constraining. Federal floor exists at the criminal-enforcement level for the 31 DPA-affected schools; state fiscal-constraint exists at the broader operating-and-adequacy level for SDP overall. Per D6 worker's articulation of Both/And as preservation of simultaneous competing truths, neither layer collapses into the other; G13-SD5-01 captures the AHERA criminal-floor obligation, and G13-SD5-02 captures the William Penn-state-fiscal context, with both preserved.

The Penn $100M pledge supporting SDP environmental management is the third architectural element. Penn's pledge intersects with D9 SD4 PILOET architecture (Payments In Lieu Of Taxes — where Penn's tax-exempt status produces fiscal-dimension anchor-accountability questions) and with D6 SD4 (where Penn's environmental-compliance architecture and contributions to SDP environmental management produce the environmental-compliance-dimension anchor-accountability finding). At D13 SD5, the Penn pledge is referenced as a capital-program input without re-extending the five-dimensional framework; the framework's completion is at D6 verified file (2026-05-11) Synthesis Section 2.

The SDP "Accelerating Opportunity" plan is the fourth element. The plan addresses facility consolidations, program offerings, school closures and openings, and capital prioritization. The April 30, 2026 Board vote on the plan is pending (UV-D13-04); plan parameters have been the subject of substantial public comment through Chalkbeat / NBC10 / Philadelphia Inquirer / Axios / Billy Penn coverage. The plan's intersection with AHERA DPA obligations (specific school dispositions for the 31 DPA-affected schools) and with William Penn fiscal context (capital prioritization sequence within state-fiscal-constrained capacity) is structurally significant.

For the North Philadelphia family with children at an AHERA-affected school, the West Philadelphia family at a poor/unsatisfactory-rated school, and the South Philadelphia family encountering "Accelerating Opportunity" plan implementation who appear in this sub-domain's aggregate profiles, the experience of the federal-state-local architecture is the school-specific operational status — remediation progress at DPA-affected schools, capital pace at poor/unsatisfactory-rated buildings, plan disposition for specific schools. The structural representation question for SD5 is whether federal House representation engages the AHERA criminal-floor architecture (cross-reference D6 G6-SD4-02 principal anchor; D13 defers), the federal Title I / IDEA / facility funding architecture, the AHERA O&M support, and the Penn pledge architectural coordination — recognizing that the principal capital and fiscal capacity sits at state and local levels.

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G13-SD5-01: AHERA criminal-floor obligation and DPA judicial oversight.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (DPA architecture)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the DPA; HIGH on the federal-floor obligation
- *Gap statement:* AHERA criminal enforcement under the June 26, 2025 SDP DPA imposes judicially-overseen remediation obligations on SDP for 31 schools. The federal criminal-floor obligation exists alongside state-constitutional-adequacy fiscal-constraint (G13-SD5-02) — Both/And preserved. *Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) MC-03 / G6-SD4-02 — D6 is the cross-domain principal anchor for AHERA criminal-enforcement architecture; D13 SD5 captures the federal-floor obligation as it operates at SDP but defers principal-anchor analysis to D6.*
- *Evidence trace:* MC-07 (SDP AHERA DPA); Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (AHERA architecture); D6 G6-SD4-02 (cross-reference).
- *Representation implication:* Federal AHERA program oversight; EPA-CID / DOJ-EDPA coordination engagement; cross-reference D6 representation implications.

**G13-SD5-02: William Penn-state-constitutional-adequacy fiscal-shortfall.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (litigation history and legislative response)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the legislative response and adequacy-gap estimate; MEDIUM on the multi-year compliance trajectory
- *Gap statement:* William Penn School District v. Commonwealth Pennsylvania Supreme Court (2017) / Commonwealth Court (2023) decisions found PA school funding system unconstitutional. PA budget November 12, 2025 (per MC-05) included $565M William Penn legislative response against estimated ~$3.8B remaining adequacy gap. The structural finding (G13-SD5-02 preserves the fiscal-shortfall alongside G13-SD5-01 AHERA criminal-floor obligation as Both/And) holds.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-05 (William Penn legislative response); Section 2 State Statutory Layer (William Penn architecture and PSFIG); G13-SD5-01 (Both/And preservation).
- *Representation implication:* Federal Title I / IDEA / school construction federal-program advocacy; coordination with PDE on PSFIG capacity; federal-state coordination on adequacy-gap-closing pace.

**G13-SD5-03: SDP "Accelerating Opportunity" plan April 30, 2026 Board vote.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (plan parameters); [SD] Structurally derived (implementation question)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the plan and Board vote pending; MEDIUM on specific dispositions pending vote outcome (UV-D13-04)
- *Gap statement:* SDP "Accelerating Opportunity" plan addresses facility consolidations, program offerings, school closures and openings, and capital prioritization; April 30, 2026 Board vote pending. Plan's intersection with AHERA DPA and William Penn fiscal context is structurally significant.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Local Statutory Layer (SDP plan architecture); UV-D13-04 (vote outcome held open); Section 3 Triggering event 3.
- *Representation implication:* Federal-program engagement during plan implementation; Title I / IDEA / AHERA federal-program coordination at school-specific level.

**G13-SD5-04: Poor/unsatisfactory-rated buildings capital pace.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (building count); [SD] Structurally derived (pace gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the ~85 buildings figure; MEDIUM on precise PSFIG-applicability and capital-pace at SDP-specific level
- *Gap statement:* SDP has approximately 85 buildings rated poor/unsatisfactory. PSFIG $500K-$5M grants with 25% local match constrain SDP's capacity to address all buildings at scale; William Penn $565M legislative response addresses operating-and-adequacy but does not directly resolve specific building capital backlog. Cross-reference UV-D13-09 (D6 trajectory recovery framing) for SDP-specific analog.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 4 Statistical Profile (~85 buildings figure); Section 2 State Statutory Layer (PSFIG architecture); G13-SD5-02 (William Penn cross-reference).
- *Representation implication:* Federal school construction federal-program advocacy; PSFIG capacity-coordination engagement.

**G13-SD5-05: Penn $100M pledge architectural coordination.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (pledge announcement); [SD] Structurally derived (cross-domain integration)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the pledge; MEDIUM on specific operational architecture and disbursement schedule
- *Gap statement:* University of Pennsylvania pledged $100M to support SDP environmental management. Pledge intersects D9 SD4 PILOET architecture (fiscal-dimension anchor-accountability) and D6 SD4 environmental-compliance-dimension anchor-accountability. At D13 SD5, the pledge is referenced as a capital-program input without re-extending the five-dimensional framework; cross-reference D9 / D6 verified files (2026-05-11) for principal-anchor architectures.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Local Agency Layer (Penn pledge architecture); D9 SD4 / D6 SD4 cross-references.
- *Representation implication:* Cross-domain anchor-accountability framework coordination; federal-program engagement on anchor-institution environmental contributions.

**G13-SD5-06: Post-Justice40 federal disadvantaged-community targeting retraction (school construction / facilities).**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (federal architecture change); [SD] Structurally derived (programmatic targeting consequence)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the federal architecture change; MEDIUM on the precise effect on SDP federal-funding flows
- *Gap statement:* Per MC-03, Justice40, EO 12898, and EO 14096 revoked through 2025. School construction / facility federal-program EJ targeting that operated through these frameworks has retracted; statutory floors (Title I, IDEA) remain. Affects PA-3 disadvantaged-community-eligible school-facility flows at administrative-targeting level.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-03 (Justice40 broader revocation); Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (Justice40 framework note); D6 MC-04/MC-32 cross-reference.
- *Representation implication:* Federal statutory EJ codification for school facilities; FY27 Title I / IDEA / school facility advocacy with EJ criteria.

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

PA-3 school building and public facility capital infrastructure operates within a federal-state-local architecture in which (1) **what the legal framework provides or promises:** federal AHERA criminal-floor obligation (15 U.S.C. §§ 2641-2656) with the June 26, 2025 SDP DPA establishing judicially-overseen remediation for 31 schools; federal Title I, IDEA, and limited school construction authority; Pennsylvania Public School Code framework with William Penn School District v. Commonwealth substantive constitutional grounding and PA budget November 2025 $565M legislative response (against estimated ~$3.8B remaining adequacy gap); PSFIG state-level facility capital mechanism; SDP locally-administered architecture under Board of Education with Superintendent Watlington and Board President Streater; Penn $100M pledge supporting SDP environmental management. (2) **What PA-3 constituents actually receive:** SDP operational architecture with judicially-overseen AHERA remediation at 31 schools; ~85 buildings rated poor/unsatisfactory; "Accelerating Opportunity" plan with April 30, 2026 Board vote pending; capital pace constrained by state-fiscal context despite expansion through PSFIG and William Penn response. (3) **What accounts for the gap:** the federal AHERA criminal-floor obligation and the state William Penn fiscal-constraint operate as Both/And — neither collapses into the other; PSFIG state-capital mechanism expanded but constrained by 25% local match capacity; the Penn $100M pledge supports environmental management without resolving broader capital backlog; the post-Justice40 federal administrative retraction (MC-03) has thinned implementation while statutory floors remain. (4) **What the gap means for the representation analysis:** PA-3 SDP students at AHERA-affected schools and poor/unsatisfactory-rated buildings — concentrated in North/Northwest and West Cores where building stock is oldest, and disproportionately Black, Hispanic or Latino given PA-3's demographic composition — experience the most-acute consequences of the architecture's capital-fiscal mismatch. The structural representation question is whether federal House representation engages the cross-domain AHERA / Penn / PILOET / William Penn architecture (referencing D6 G6-SD4-02 principal anchor for AHERA criminal-enforcement, D9 SD4 for PILOET fiscal-dimension, D6 SD4 for anchor-environmental-compliance dimension), and engages the federal Title I / IDEA / school facility federal-program advocacy in coordination with PA-state-architecture trajectory.

# D13 Physical Infrastructure — Sub-Domain 6: Parks, Recreation Centers, and Open Space

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain number:** SD6
**Sub-domain name:** Parks, Recreation Centers, and Open Space
**Scope statement:** The federal/state/local mandate-stack architecture for parks, recreation centers, and open space serving PA-3, with primary institutional embodiment in the Philadelphia Department of Parks and Recreation (PPR) and the Fairmount Park Conservancy as principal public-philanthropic partner; the federal-floor protections through the Land and Water Conservation Fund (LWCF, 54 U.S.C. § 200302) state-side and federal-side assistance architecture, the National Park Service (NPS) jurisdictional authority over the Heinz John H. Heinz National Wildlife Refuge / Tinicum (cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) SD6 reciprocal), the federal Recreational Trails Program, and the federal Surface Transportation Block Grant Transportation Alternatives Program set-aside (cross-reference SD3) supporting trail and bike infrastructure; the Pennsylvania Department of Conservation and Natural Resources (DCNR) state architecture; the locally-administered Wissahickon Valley Park (NPS-affiliated National Recreation Area architecture; cross-reference D6 SD6); the PPR ParkScore equity-gap pattern documented in Trust for Public Land (TPL) annual analysis; FDR Park Phase 1 ($263M) and Phase 2 capital reconstruction; and the structural constraint that the federal floor provides project-specific capital support without imposing minimum sub-area parkland-equity trajectory, while documented PA-3 sub-area variation in parkland access produces systematic equity gaps.

**Data provenance regime:** Internet-access regime. Track 1 architecture is directly documented from U.S. Code, PA statutes, and Philadelphia primary sources. Track 2 outcomes draw on TPL ParkScore annual reports, PPR primary sources, Fairmount Park Conservancy materials, and NPS / FWS communications on Heinz NWR and Wissahickon NRA architecture. Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) SD6 for reciprocal conservation-partnership architecture.

**Data provenance scope statement:** Philadelphia-specific and directly documented: PPR oversight of more than 10,000 acres of parkland including Fairmount Park (the city's signature park system; ~9,200 acres); approximately 170 recreation centers; FDR Park Phase 1 ($263M complete) and Phase 2 (in planning); Heinz John H. Heinz National Wildlife Refuge at Tinicum (federal FWS jurisdiction; PA-3 / Eastwick adjacency); Wissahickon Valley Park (~2,000 acres; federally-designated National Recreation Area architecture by 1964 NPS designation; locally-administered by PPR with Friends of the Wissahickon partnership); PPR Commissioner Susan Slawson; Fairmount Park Conservancy Director-level architecture; LWCF FY26 funding protected by Congress against administrative-redirection efforts. National benchmark applied by inference: TPL ParkScore annual ranking; LWCF state-side assistance pattern. Structural inference: PA-3 sub-area parkland-access variation tracking with housing-stock and demographic concentration; ParkScore equity-gap pattern producing systematic sub-area inequality. Held-open: NPS Northeast Region Administrator name (UV-D13-14); Fairmount Park Conservancy specific FY26 budget.

## Section 2: Authority Chain

**Constitutional Foundation.**

*U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cl. 1 — Spending Clause.* Grounds federal assistance to state/local parks and recreation programs through LWCF and other channels.

*U.S. Const. art. IV, § 3, cl. 2 — Property Clause.* Grounds federal management of federal lands (Heinz NWR; Wissahickon-adjacent federal jurisdictions where applicable).

*PA Const. art. I § 27.* Substantive environmental rights provision including "to the preservation of the natural, scenic, historic and esthetic values of the environment" — provides substantive constitutional grounding for parkland and open space conservation. *Cross-reference SD2 and D6 architecture.*

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

*Land and Water Conservation Fund Act, 54 U.S.C. §§ 200301 et seq.* Establishes LWCF for federal acquisition of federal lands (federal side) and grants to states for parks and recreation acquisition and development (state side). Funded primarily by Outer Continental Shelf (OCS) oil and gas lease revenue. Permanently authorized and funded under the John D. Dingell, Jr. Conservation, Management, and Recreation Act of 2019 ("Dingell Act") and the Great American Outdoors Act (GAOA) of 2020. *Verification update (2026-05-11): LWCF FY26 funding was protected by Congress through the November 12, 2025 budget against administrative-redirection efforts; the state-side LWCF grants flow through DCNR with PPR-applicable subgrant architecture.* *Statutory stability: HIGH (permanent authorization). Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM (administrative-redirection attempts).*

*National Park Service (NPS) jurisdictional authority.* NPS administers federally-designated National Park System units; Wissahickon Valley Park's National Recreation Area designation (1964) is a federal recognition though the park is locally-administered by PPR. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM.* *NPS Northeast Region Administrator name not surfaced at retrofit verification; UV-D13-14.*

*U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (FWS) jurisdictional authority over John H. Heinz NWR at Tinicum.* National Wildlife Refuge System architecture; Heinz NWR adjacent to Eastwick in PA-3 / Delaware County boundary geography (cross-reference D6 SD6 for principal-anchor analysis on Heinz NWR conservation architecture). *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM.*

*Recreational Trails Program (RTP), 23 U.S.C. § 206.* Federal-Aid Highway Program apportionment supporting trail acquisition, construction, and maintenance; state administration through DCNR with PPR-applicable architecture. *Statutory stability: HIGH for current authorization; trajectory uncertain post-IIJA reauthorization (per MC-02).*

*Surface Transportation Block Grant Transportation Alternatives (TA) set-aside, 23 U.S.C. § 133(h).* Federal-Aid Highway Program set-aside for non-motorized infrastructure — supports bike lanes, trails, sidewalks; cross-reference SD3 G13-SD3-01 for HSIP/STBG/TAP integration.

*Federal-aid program cross-references.* HUD CDBG (Community Development Block Grant) supports parks and recreation infrastructure in eligible areas; NPS Outdoor Recreation Legacy Partnership Program supports urban park projects; EPA Brownfields Program supports remediation including parkland reuse. *Statutory stability: HIGH for current authorization.*

*Justice40 framework revocation.* Per MC-03: Justice40 (EO 14008), EO 12898, EO 14096 revoked through 2025. Affects parks-and-recreation discretionary grant equity considerations; statutory floors unchanged; administrative implementation thinned. Cross-reference D6 MC-04/MC-32 for principal-anchor EJ content.

**State Statutory Layer.**

*Pennsylvania Department of Conservation and Natural Resources (DCNR).* Cabinet-level state agency administering state parks, state forests, and federal-aid pass-through (LWCF, RTP). Subgrants to municipal parks and recreation entities through Community Conservation Partnerships Program. *Statutory stability: HIGH. Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM.*

*Pennsylvania Game and Wildlife Code, 34 Pa.C.S.* Authorizes Pennsylvania Game Commission jurisdiction over wildlife management; intersects with Wissahickon and other open-space wildlife-management coordination.

*PA Const. art. I § 27.* See Constitutional Foundation.

*Pennsylvania-specific features.* DCNR's Community Conservation Partnerships Program flow to PPR is substantial; PA's state-park system is one of the largest in the country and provides regional context for PA-3 open-space access.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

*Philadelphia Department of Parks and Recreation (PPR).* Operational agency overseeing more than 10,000 acres of city parkland, approximately 170 recreation centers, swimming pools, athletic facilities, community gardens, and the Free Library / PPR partnership architecture. **Commissioner: Susan Slawson** (Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out: in official capacity per City of Philadelphia communications). Subordinate Director-level structure for Fairmount Park, neighborhood parks, recreation programming, and capital projects. *Statutory stability: HIGH (Home Rule). Administrative vulnerability: MEDIUM.*

*Fairmount Park Conservancy.* 501(c)(3) public-philanthropic partner organization supporting Fairmount Park and broader PPR park-improvement initiatives. Substantial role in capital project fundraising, programming, and stewardship; FY budget and staffing not retrieved at retrofit verification.

*Philadelphia Code Title 15 (City-Owned Property — Recreation).* Municipal code provisions governing PPR operations, recreation programming, facility access, and partnership architecture.

*Wissahickon Valley Park architecture.* ~2,000 acres locally-administered by PPR; 1964 NPS designation as National Recreation Area is a federal recognition; **Friends of the Wissahickon** is the principal philanthropic-stewardship partner; cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) SD6 for principal-anchor conservation-partnership analysis. *Statutory stability: HIGH.*

*Home Rule authority and limits.* Philadelphia's Home Rule Charter authorizes PPR and related operations. Authority bounded by federal jurisdictional authorities at Heinz NWR (FWS-administered) and by federal designations at Wissahickon (NPS NRA designation while locally-administered).

**Local Agency Layer.**

*Philadelphia Department of Parks and Recreation (PPR).* See above.

*Fairmount Park Conservancy.* See above.

*Friends of the Wissahickon.* Principal Wissahickon Valley Park philanthropic-stewardship partner.

*PHS (Pennsylvania Horticultural Society) Land Care contract.* Vacant land stabilization architecture intersects parkland adjacency (cross-reference SD4 G13-SD4-04).

*Cobbs Creek Foundation / Cobbs Creek Restoration Trust.* Public-philanthropic partner for Cobbs Creek Park area; intersects with SD2 G13-SD2-05 flood-vulnerability geography and SD4 Lower Darby Creek Superfund adjacency.

*Schuylkill River Development Corporation.* Public-philanthropic partner for Schuylkill River-frontage parks (Schuylkill Banks, Schuylkill River Trail).

**Constituent Layer.** The aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5.

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

**Triggering event 1 — Resident seeking access to a neighborhood park or recreation center.**

*Step 1.* Resident in a North/Northwest Core, West Core, Northwest, or South/Southwest sub-area considers walking, biking, or driving to a neighborhood park, recreation center, athletic facility, swimming pool, or playground. *Decision point:* awareness of nearest facility; physical distance and time required; perceived safety along route; facility condition and operational status.

*Step 2.* PPR operates the facility under capital and programming architecture funded by City General Fund appropriation, state DCNR Community Conservation Partnerships subgrants, federal LWCF state-side flow, federal CDBG eligibility, and Fairmount Park Conservancy public-philanthropic supplement. TPL ParkScore documents systematic equity gaps in parkland access — Philadelphia has historically ranked moderately on ParkScore overall while showing within-city sub-area inequality. *Decision point:* facility condition; operational hours; programming availability; ADA accessibility.

*Step 3.* The resident's experience of the federal-state-local architecture is the proximity, condition, and programming of the specific facility they reach. The 10-minute-walk-to-park standard (TPL benchmark) is unevenly met across PA-3 sub-areas — North/Northwest Core lower-income tracts show systematic shortfalls relative to higher-income tracts. The equity-gap pattern is the structural finding (G13-SD6-01). *Pathway breakdown:* federal-floor capital flows operate at project-specific level; the systematic sub-area equity gap is not directly addressed by minimum-trajectory federal requirement.

**Triggering event 2 — Resident or visitor accessing FDR Park.**

*Step 1.* Resident, athletic participant, or visitor accesses FDR Park (348 acres; South Philadelphia / Sports Complex area). FDR Park has been subject to a multi-phase reconstruction since 2019; Phase 1 (~$263M; complete) included athletic field reconstruction, stormwater management infrastructure, gateway improvements; Phase 2 (in planning) addresses remaining park areas. *Decision point:* facility condition during construction phases; programming continuity.

*Step 2.* FDR Park reconstruction has involved federal-program flows (CDBG eligibility; stormwater infrastructure cross-reference SD2; potential LWCF state-side), state DCNR subgrants, City General Fund appropriation, and substantial Fairmount Park Conservancy / philanthropic capital. Phase 2 planning is the operational trajectory. *Decision point:* Phase 2 timeline and scope; facility-specific reconstruction sequencing.

*Step 3.* The resident or visitor experiences the FDR Park reconstruction as the operational status of specific park features over time. Phase 1's reconstruction architecture is the largest single-park capital investment in PPR's recent history at this scale. *Pathway breakdown:* federal-floor support operates project-specifically; the multi-source capital architecture is the operational answer to PPR's largest-park reconstruction need; the structural finding (G13-SD6-04 multi-source capital architecture for signature parks) captures the pattern.

**Triggering event 3 — Resident accessing Wissahickon or other regional open-space asset.**

*Step 1.* Resident in Northwest Philadelphia, Roxborough, or surrounding sub-areas accesses Wissahickon Valley Park (~2,000 acres); locally-administered by PPR with Friends of the Wissahickon as principal philanthropic-stewardship partner; 1964 NPS National Recreation Area designation provides federal recognition.

*Step 2.* Wissahickon operates under PPR's local-administration architecture with Friends of the Wissahickon partnership; NPS NRA designation provides federal recognition but is not the same as direct NPS-administered jurisdiction. The conservation-partnership architecture is the principal-anchor at D6 verified file (2026-05-11) SD6; D13 SD6 references the architecture as it operates at constituent-access level.

*Step 3.* Similarly, residents accessing Heinz John H. Heinz National Wildlife Refuge at Tinicum encounter federally-administered (FWS) jurisdiction within PA-3 / Eastwick / Delaware County boundary geography; the cumulative geography with SD2 flood-vulnerability and SD4 Superfund adjacency is structurally important (cross-reference G13-SD4-05 cumulative-burden framing). *Pathway breakdown:* federal-administered (FWS) and federally-designated (NPS NRA) open-space assets operate alongside locally-administered parkland; constituent access varies by sub-area geography.

**Pathway breakdown patterns in practice.** Documented breakdown points concentrate at the systematic sub-area parkland-access equity gap (Triggering event 1 Step 3) where the 10-minute-walk-to-park standard is unevenly met across PA-3 (G13-SD6-01); at the multi-source capital architecture for signature parks like FDR (Triggering event 2 Step 3) where federal-state-local-philanthropic coordination produces project-specific outcomes but does not establish trajectory for systematic equity-gap closing; and at the federal-designated / federal-administered asset access pattern (Triggering event 3) where Wissahickon NRA and Heinz NWR architectures intersect with PPR local-administration and with broader cumulative-burden geography.

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

*Data provenance note:* PPR statistical profile is Philadelphia-specific and directly documented from PPR primary sources. TPL ParkScore data and equity-gap pattern are from Trust for Public Land annual analysis. FDR Park Phase 1 ($263M) and Phase 2 architecture is from PPR and Fairmount Park Conservancy primary sources. Wissahickon and Heinz NWR architectures are from PPR, NPS, and FWS primary sources. Cross-reference D6 verified file (2026-05-11) SD6 for principal-anchor conservation-partnership analysis.

**PA-3 statistical profile.**

- PPR oversight: more than 10,000 acres of parkland; approximately 170 recreation centers; numerous athletic facilities, swimming pools, playgrounds, community gardens.
- Fairmount Park system: approximately 9,200 acres (the city's signature park system).
- Wissahickon Valley Park: approximately 2,000 acres; locally-administered by PPR; 1964 NPS NRA designation.
- John H. Heinz NWR at Tinicum: approximately 1,200 acres of FWS-administered refuge adjacent to Eastwick (PA-3 / Delaware County boundary).
- FDR Park: approximately 348 acres; Phase 1 reconstruction $263M complete; Phase 2 in planning.
- TPL ParkScore equity-gap pattern: documented systematic sub-area inequality in parkland access, with within-city low-income / BIPOC neighborhoods showing shortfalls relative to higher-income neighborhoods.
- LWCF FY26 funding: protected by Congress against administrative-redirection efforts.

**Geographic variation across four sub-areas.**

- *North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.* Lower parkland density relative to other sub-areas; systematic 10-minute-walk-to-park shortfall in Hunting Park, North Central, and parts of Kensington; recreation centers serve concentrated population. PPR investment in recent years has focused on neighborhood park upgrades within capital constraints.

- *West Philadelphia Core.* Cobbs Creek Park (~600 acres) anchors regional parkland in this sub-area; Fairmount Park West provides access for some residents; recreation centers serve concentrated population. Cobbs Creek Foundation public-philanthropic engagement is substantial.

- *Northwest Philadelphia.* Highest parkland density in PA-3; Wissahickon Valley Park anchors the sub-area; Fairmount Park East access; multiple smaller parks; bicycle/pedestrian trail access well-developed. Friends of the Wissahickon partnership produces strong stewardship architecture.

- *South/Southwest Philadelphia.* FDR Park (348 acres) anchors South Philadelphia; smaller neighborhood parks scattered through dense residential blocks; Eastwick sub-area has access to Heinz NWR (FWS-administered) adjacent to flood-vulnerability and Superfund geography (cross-reference SD2 and SD4); Schuylkill River frontage parks (Schuylkill Banks, Bartram's Garden).

The geographic variation pattern is characterized by Northwest Philadelphia's high parkland density (Wissahickon + Fairmount East) producing strong access, while North/Northwest Core systematic shortfalls produce documented TPL ParkScore equity gaps; South/Southwest mixed with FDR Park anchoring and dense-residential-block smaller parks elsewhere.

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

**Profile 1: Resident with limited neighborhood parkland — North Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Family with two children, household income approximately $36,000, residing in Hunting Park area; nearest substantial parkland requires public transit or substantial walk.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Philadelphia Core.

*Triggering event:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 1 — family seeks neighborhood parkland access; navigates 10-minute-walk-to-park shortfall.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* PPR neighborhood park investment under capital constraints; Fairmount Park access requires transit or longer travel; DCNR Community Conservation Partnerships subgrants support specific projects; federal LWCF state-side flow supports parkland acquisition where applicable; CDBG eligibility for some neighborhood-park improvements.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* TPL ParkScore documents Philadelphia equity-gap pattern with within-city sub-area inequality. Verify current TPL ParkScore Philadelphia analysis at parkscore.tpl.org before public use — F13-SD6-01.

*Outcome:* The family experiences the federal-state-local architecture as the proximity of accessible parkland to home; the systematic equity gap (G13-SD6-01) is the constituent-level expression of the architecture's project-specific federal-floor support without minimum-trajectory equity-gap-closing requirement.

**Profile 2: South Philadelphia resident accessing FDR Park.**

*Constituent type:* Athletic participant, family with children, or general visitor, household income approximately $48,000, residing in South Philadelphia within proximity of FDR Park.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* South/Southwest Philadelphia.

*Triggering event:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 2 — accesses FDR Park during Phase 1 reconstruction complete and Phase 2 planning periods.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* FDR Park reconstruction architecture: federal-program flows, state DCNR subgrants, City appropriation, Fairmount Park Conservancy / philanthropic capital. Phase 1 ($263M) complete; Phase 2 in planning.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* FDR Park Phase 1 cost figures and reconstruction architecture documented at PPR and Fairmount Park Conservancy. Verify current FDR Park Phase 2 plan at phila.gov/parks-and-recreation before public use — F13-SD6-02.

*Outcome:* The resident or visitor experiences PPR's largest-park reconstruction in operational form; the multi-source capital architecture is the operational answer at this signature-park scale, with the structural finding (G13-SD6-04 multi-source capital architecture) capturing the pattern.

**Profile 3: Wissahickon trail user — Northwest Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Resident, age 41, household income approximately $58,000, residing in Roxborough or Mt. Airy; uses Wissahickon Valley Park trails for recreation, hiking, and bicycle commuting.

*Specific PA-3 sub-area:* Northwest Philadelphia.

*Triggering event:* Per Section 3 Triggering event 3 — accesses Wissahickon for daily/weekly recreation; benefits from Friends of the Wissahickon stewardship architecture.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* PPR local-administration with Friends of the Wissahickon partnership; NPS NRA federal recognition; federal STBG/TAP/RTP flows support trail infrastructure; conservation-partnership architecture is principal-anchor at D6 SD6 (cross-reference).

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance disclosure:* Wissahickon Valley Park's trail network is among the most extensive urban trail systems in the country; specific trail-mile and usage figures documented at PPR and Friends of the Wissahickon. Verify current Wissahickon trail-mile and usage data at fow.org and phila.gov/parks-and-recreation before public use — F13-SD6-03.

*Outcome:* The user experiences the strong PPR / Friends of the Wissahickon conservation-partnership architecture; federal NPS NRA designation provides federal recognition that supports stewardship; the structural finding (G13-SD6-05 conservation-partnership architecture as constituent-access amplifier) captures the pattern for this sub-area.

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The most consequential thing to understand about Philadelphia parks, recreation, and open space from a PA-3 representation perspective is the architectural pattern of sub-area equity-gap concentration within an overall parkland system that ranks moderately well at the citywide level — and the multi-source capital architecture's effectiveness at signature-park scale alongside its insufficiency at systematic equity-gap-closing scale.

Philadelphia is one of the country's older park-system cities. Fairmount Park (the signature park system at ~9,200 acres) is among the largest urban park systems in the U.S.; Wissahickon Valley Park within Fairmount Park has 1964 NPS National Recreation Area designation; FDR Park's Phase 1 ($263M) reconstruction is the largest-scale single-park capital investment in PPR's recent history. TPL ParkScore has historically ranked Philadelphia moderately at the citywide level. The federal floor under LWCF (permanently authorized; FY26 funding protected by Congress per November 2025 budget context) provides state-side and federal-side capital support; the NPS jurisdictional architecture provides federal recognition for Wissahickon; the FWS jurisdictional architecture administers Heinz John H. Heinz NWR at Tinicum.

What the architecture does not address at minimum-trajectory level is the systematic sub-area equity gap that TPL ParkScore documents within Philadelphia. North/Northwest Core lower-income tracts show 10-minute-walk-to-park shortfalls; the equity-gap pattern tracks with housing-stock-age and demographic concentration. Federal LWCF state-side flow operates project-specifically; federal CDBG operates by census-tract eligibility; federal STBG/TAP/RTP supports specific trail and bike-infrastructure projects. The aggregate effect is project-by-project capital investment that has not closed the systematic sub-area gap.

The FDR Park reconstruction is the operational example of the multi-source capital architecture working at signature-park scale. Phase 1 ($263M complete) drew on federal-program flows (including stormwater management cross-reference SD2), state DCNR subgrants, City General Fund appropriation, and substantial Fairmount Park Conservancy / philanthropic capital. Phase 2 in planning will continue the architecture. The structural finding (G13-SD6-04) captures this multi-source pattern as the operational answer at signature-park scale.

The Wissahickon and Heinz NWR architectures connect this sub-domain to the conservation-partnership analysis at D6 SD6 (principal anchor). At D13 SD6, the architectures are referenced as they operate at PA-3 constituent-access level — Wissahickon producing strong Northwest Philadelphia access through PPR / Friends of the Wissahickon partnership; Heinz NWR producing federally-administered open-space access adjacent to the SD2 / SD4 cumulative-burden Eastwick geography.

The post-Justice40 federal administrative retraction (MC-03) has affected the equity-targeting dimension of federal parks-and-recreation discretionary grant flow; statutory floors (LWCF, CDBG, STBG/TAP/RTP) remain unchanged; administrative implementation thinned. The structural finding (G13-SD6-06 captures the post-Justice40 effect at this sub-domain).

For the Hunting Park family with limited neighborhood parkland, the South Philadelphia resident accessing FDR Park, and the Wissahickon trail user in Roxborough who appear in this sub-domain's aggregate profiles, the experience of the federal-state-local architecture is the proximity and quality of the parkland and recreation facilities they can reach. The structural representation question for SD6 is whether federal House representation engages the federal-floor architecture (LWCF reauthorization stewardship, IIJA STBG/TAP/RTP reauthorization, Reconnecting Communities cross-reference SD3, federal EJ statutory codification post-Justice40 revocation, NPS/FWS jurisdictional coordination), and whether the engagement reaches the sub-area equity-gap layer at which TPL ParkScore documents the most acute disparities.

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G13-SD6-01: TPL ParkScore systematic sub-area equity gap.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (ParkScore data); [SD] Structurally derived (federal-flow-to-gap mismatch)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the documented equity-gap pattern; MEDIUM on the precise federal-flow-to-gap match
- *Gap statement:* TPL ParkScore documents systematic Philadelphia sub-area parkland-access inequality, with 10-minute-walk-to-park shortfalls concentrated in North/Northwest Core and other lower-income sub-areas. Federal LWCF state-side flow, federal CDBG, and federal STBG/TAP/RTP support project-specific investment; no minimum-trajectory federal requirement for systematic equity-gap closing. The gap between documented equity-gap scale and federal-flow architecture is the structural mismatch.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 4 Statistical Profile (ParkScore pattern); Section 3 Triggering event 1; Section 5 Profile 1.
- *Representation implication:* LWCF state-side flow advocacy with equity-gap criteria; CDBG eligibility advocacy; STBG/TAP/RTP reauthorization advocacy; coordination with PPR on sub-area capital prioritization.

**G13-SD6-02: PPR capital architecture at neighborhood-park scale.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (PPR capital architecture); [SD] Structurally derived (pace-gap)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the multi-source capital architecture; MEDIUM on precise neighborhood-park-pace vs. need
- *Gap statement:* PPR neighborhood-park capital investment operates under City General Fund appropriation, state DCNR Community Conservation Partnerships subgrants, federal LWCF state-side flow, federal CDBG, and Fairmount Park Conservancy / philanthropic capital. At neighborhood-park scale (vs. signature-park scale like FDR), the multi-source architecture has produced project-by-project investment without systematic equity-gap closing. The pace-gap is the structural finding.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 (multi-source capital architecture); Section 4 Statistical Profile; G13-SD6-01 (equity-gap cross-reference).
- *Representation implication:* Federal-program coordination on PPR capital architecture; potential federal program design (NPS Outdoor Recreation Legacy Partnership Program expansion) for systematic urban-equity-park investment.

**G13-SD6-03: Wissahickon Valley Park NPS NRA designation and locally-administered architecture.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (NRA designation and PPR administration)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the architecture
- *Gap statement:* Wissahickon Valley Park's 1964 NPS National Recreation Area designation provides federal recognition; PPR locally-administers with Friends of the Wissahickon as principal philanthropic-stewardship partner; the conservation-partnership architecture's principal-anchor analysis is at D6 verified file (2026-05-11) SD6. D13 SD6 references the architecture as it operates at constituent-access level.
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (NPS NRA architecture); Section 2 Local Statutory Layer (Wissahickon PPR architecture); D6 SD6 cross-reference.
- *Representation implication:* NPS jurisdictional coordination; Friends of the Wissahickon partnership engagement; cross-reference D6 representation implications.

**G13-SD6-04: Multi-source capital architecture at signature-park scale (FDR exemplar).**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (FDR Park reconstruction); [SD] Structurally derived (pattern)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the FDR Park reconstruction and multi-source architecture; HIGH on the pattern recognition
- *Gap statement:* FDR Park Phase 1 ($263M complete) and Phase 2 (in planning) operate through multi-source capital architecture: federal-program flows, state DCNR subgrants, City appropriation, Fairmount Park Conservancy / philanthropic capital. This multi-source pattern is the operational answer at signature-park scale; it does not establish trajectory for systematic equity-gap closing at neighborhood-park scale (G13-SD6-02 cross-reference).
- *Evidence trace:* Section 2 Local Agency Layer (Fairmount Park Conservancy); Section 3 Triggering event 2; Section 5 Profile 2.
- *Representation implication:* Federal-program coordination on PPR signature-park architecture; engagement with Fairmount Park Conservancy and similar public-philanthropic partners.

**G13-SD6-05: Eastwick / Heinz NWR cumulative-burden geography.**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (Heinz NWR architecture); [SD] Structurally derived (cumulative-burden integration)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the Heinz NWR architecture; HIGH on the cumulative-burden geography
- *Gap statement:* John H. Heinz NWR at Tinicum (FWS-administered) provides federally-administered open-space access adjacent to Eastwick neighborhood; the same geography intersects SD2 flood-vulnerability (G13-SD2-05) and SD4 Lower Darby Creek Superfund adjacency (G13-SD4-03). Federal program architecture treats these as separate authorities (FWS NWR System; CWA NFIP/FEMA; CERCLA); constituent experience is cumulative. Cross-reference D6 SD6 for principal-anchor conservation-partnership analysis and G13-SD4-05 / G13-SD2-07 for cumulative-burden integration.

- *Representation implication:* Cumulative-burden federal-program coordination across FWS / CWA / CERCLA / NFIP; cross-domain congressional delegation engagement.

**G13-SD6-06: Post-Justice40 federal disadvantaged-community targeting retraction (parks/recreation).**
- *Evidence type:* [D] Directly documented (federal architecture change); [SD] Structurally derived (programmatic targeting consequence)
- *Confidence:* HIGH on the federal architecture change; MEDIUM on the precise effect on PA-3 parks/recreation flows
- *Gap statement:* Per MC-03, Justice40, EO 12898, EO 14096 revoked through 2025. Federal parks-and-recreation discretionary grant EJ targeting (Outdoor Recreation Legacy Partnership Program; LWCF state-side equity criteria; CDBG EJ targeting) operated through these frameworks; administrative implementation thinned; statutory floors remain. Affects PA-3 sub-area equity-gap-closing flows at administrative-targeting level. Cross-reference D6 MC-04/MC-32 for principal-anchor EJ content.
- *Evidence trace:* MC-03 (Justice40 broader revocation); Section 2 Federal Statutory Layer (Justice40 framework note); D6 cross-reference.
- *Representation implication:* Federal statutory EJ codification for parks/recreation; LWCF / NPS Outdoor Recreation Legacy reauthorization with EJ criteria.

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

PA-3 parks, recreation, and open space infrastructure operates within a federal-state-local architecture in which (1) **what the legal framework provides or promises:** federal LWCF (permanently authorized; FY26 protected) state-side and federal-side assistance; federal NPS jurisdictional authority including Wissahickon Valley Park NRA designation; federal FWS jurisdictional authority over John H. Heinz NWR; federal STBG/TAP/RTP and CDBG federal-program supports; PA DCNR Community Conservation Partnerships Program subgrants; substantive PA Const. art. I § 27 grounding; PPR locally-administered architecture under Commissioner Slawson; Fairmount Park Conservancy public-philanthropic partner; Friends of the Wissahickon and similar partnership architectures. (2) **What PA-3 constituents actually receive:** more than 10,000 acres of PPR parkland with sub-area access unevenly distributed; FDR Park Phase 1 ($263M) complete and Phase 2 in planning; Wissahickon Valley Park strong stewardship architecture; Heinz NWR adjacent to Eastwick cumulative-burden geography; TPL ParkScore documented sub-area equity gaps. (3) **What accounts for the gap:** federal-floor architecture provides project-specific capital support without minimum-trajectory equity-gap-closing requirement; multi-source capital architecture works at signature-park scale (FDR exemplar) but does not close systematic neighborhood-park equity gap; conservation-partnership architecture amplifies access where partnerships are strong (Wissahickon, Cobbs Creek) but does not substitute for federal-floor equity trajectory; post-Justice40 administrative retraction (MC-03) has thinned the equity-targeting implementation layer. (4) **What the gap means for the representation analysis:** PA-3 North/Northwest Core lower-income tracts and demographically-concentrated neighborhoods experience the systematic equity-gap most acutely; Eastwick residents experience cumulative SD2+SD4+SD6 burden across federal-program authority lines; Wissahickon trail users in Northwest experience strong conservation-partnership architecture. The structural representation question is whether federal House representation engages the federal-floor architecture (LWCF stewardship, IIJA reauthorization, Reconnecting Communities, NPS Outdoor Recreation Legacy Partnership Program expansion, federal EJ statutory codification post-Justice40 revocation) at the level where TPL ParkScore documents the most-acute sub-area disparities.
